2 FOR DEALNIMS AIN CAKES McDOUGALL'S Pategt SELF-RAISING FLOUP. The Examiner Saturday, January 30, 1904.

a pickaxe. A brisk tramp of about fifteen minutes brought us to the spot; we climbed over the crumbling walls, and dropped in among the long rustling grass. The desolation and dreariness of the place were beyond expression, nor was there any-

thing in the hum of awaking life in the great city beyond the Ajmeer Gate to neutralise the profound sense of loneliness which came over one on entering the wild, overgrown garden. Hideous bald-headed vultures wrangled with weird screams for fragments .of carrion, and clouds of flies, humming like locusts, hovered over the putrid carcase of a jackal,

or the body of a famished dog. In the growing

light I perceived four shadowy figures stealthily approaching, like ghouls gliding through the long growth: these were Rameer's assistants. I took a brief survey of the garden, which probably covered about two acres, in the form of a quadrangle, with the temple ruins in the centre. Rameer pointed out to me the spot in which he had discovered the little golden image: it was in one of the further corners of the place. I foresaw plenty of work in clearing away the dense tangle-weed, which we might easily have got rid of by the simple expedient of burning it, but that the flames would have probably attracted attention, and this I was naturally most anxious to avoid. I had every hope that by working quietly we should be unobserved, as not a dozen people a day passed in the direction of the lonely spot. The four Hindus approached, salaaming most obsequiously, and having rapidly made up my mind as to the best. method of beginning our search, I set them to worr They laboured vigorously, frequently pausing to refresh themselves out of leathern bottles, and the forenoon was still young by the time they had cleared the temple garden of its jungle- like growth. I then set them to dig deep trench the whole width of the enclosure, starting close up against the northern wall, my notion being to work along from this, and turn up all the soil as we went. The ground was of a loose, sandy nature, and readily yielded to the spade. Our first find was nothing of great value, certainly—neither gold nor jewels, but a fossilised camel, pressed as flat as a board. Our next discovery, made some dozen feet further on, was of a more interesting character, consisting of a long-necked porcelain jar, sealed at the mouth. "It is heavy," said Rameer, passing it to me, his eyes flashing with excitement • "it contains gold, sahib." I took an axe, my own heart beating high with expectation, and shivered the rate into a thousand splinters. Immediately out flew a mass of silver coins. All the natives set up a shrill peculiar cry of delight. I picked up one of the pieces; it was about the size of a rupee, but thicker, of a dull leaden colour, and bore the date 1399, together with an Oriental inscription I could not understand.

I afterwards discovered them to be pieces of the

period of Tamerlane, who, on this very spot, slew about 120,000 people at the close of the fourteenth century. The value of this discovery was not very great, to be sure, but it was "first blood," and acted as an encouragement. Rameer and the four blacks fell to work with renewed vigour, making

the sparks fly freely from their flashing shovels. The sun was beginning to get frightfully hot, but in our excitement we scarcely heeded the fierce rays.

I collected the scattered coins, and tied them up in my handkerchief.

I was thoroughly persuaded by this time that a large treasure was indeed concealed somewhere within the precincts of this sacred garden, and an idea presently taking possession of me, I strolled across to the ruined temple, and entered it. The place was miserably dilapidated, roofless, and the walls full of yawning chasms; the floor was carpeted with dust two inches deep, in which I perceived innumerable footprints, both of men and beasts. .1 scraped clear a small space with the sole of my boot, and saw that the floor of the place consisted of fine glazed tiles. Wishing to inspect one of these more closely, I opened the cartridge extractor in my big sporting knife, and inserted it between the chinks, which were fairly wide and flush up with dust. And now a marvellous thing happened. My knife slipped, and instead of dislodging the tile [the cartridge extractor ripped through the dirt in the crevice, causing it to spurt out. Amongst the debris I had thus disturbed there rolled at my feet, like several drops of blood upon the dust, a number of 'rubies! For a moment or two I discredited my eyesight —I could not realise this staggering revelation of the existence of a hidden treasure, which, for aught I could yet tell, was to be counted in millions. I fell upon my knees and began mechani- cally to collect the gems, putting them into my pocket one by one. None of them was very large, nor of great value, but then, if the floor of the

temple Were paved with jewels like this we might

indeed find some noble prizes amongst them. • stepped to the door and beckoned to Rameer and his attendants. They all came running towards me. "This is where we must seek the real treasure of the Guadrama Temple," said I, pointing to the floor. "See!" I pulled out the handful of rubies and displayed them. Loud were the exclamations of wonder and admiration, particularly from Rameer. "The floor is cemented with these," said ~ in Hindustani. "One hundred rupees a day to each man of you if you work well. We must turn up every tile."

They fell to with a will, stimulated by my offer of reward; besides, the ruined temple served as a shelter from the fiery rays of the sun, and rendered work tolerable. e first of all laid bare the whole surface ot

yellow soil that lay beneath the tiled floor of the temple, and then I bade the natives begin digging. I fear I should weary you, besides exceeding the limits at my disposal, if I were to recount in full, step by step, the story of our excavation of the floor .of that ancient sacred ruin. The task of discovery cccupied us for five days, and go fearful was I lest we should be observed and interrupted in our exploration, that as fast as the natives finished digging in one spot of the floor I caused them to re-lay the tiles and scatter the dust over them,' so that in case we were disturbed the place would wear its natural appearance, and people would not readily guess what we were about. Well, then, among the first things our research brought to light were a number of stone coffins, con- taining the embalmed bodies of Hindus, all in a perfect state of preservation, although they rapidly crumbled away into little heaps of dust upon being exposed to the air. But these coffins contained What was of more interest to us than mummies. In each one we discovered money and jewels in vary- ing quantities, and in the aggregate .amounting to a great treasure indeed. This, however, was by no means the whole of our noble prize. Amongst the dust which lay immediately beneath the tiles I collected as Many loose -precious stones as would have filled a pint measure. Many toys and images, all of them wrought in pure gold, and encrusted with gems, did we also bring to light, and one, in particular, was a fine model of an elephant, as heavy as a man could lift, with two large diamonds for its eyes, and its trunk, tail, and tusks literally zarnished with rubies and meralds. Of gold and silver money we found such a surprising quantity that we absolutely grew weary a collecting the coins. You may suppose that it would be a matter of no trifling difficulty to dispose of a great treasure like

this without exciting suspicion, but in a country like India such is not the case. The money-changers of the Chandree-Chouk, although they drive keener 1 argains than the most hardened Jew, are always • ager to barter in these goods, and by distributing (.ur valuable possessions among a 'number of the haggling 13rahmins I succeeded in realising, I dare :ay, fully two-thirds of the entire value of the treasure. This yielded us a sum of nearly half-a- -li:illicit rupees, which at the then exchange rate of Indian currency came to about £50,000. We divided 0,000 rupees amongst • the faithful natives, and Itameer and myself made equal shares of the r( minder. , A month later, very much to the sur- prise of my friends in Delhi, I resigned my position iu India, and returned to England [THE END.]

===== 0002601_19040130_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
(copyriy,,.) THE TREASURE OF THE TEMPLE.

BY HERBERT RUSSELL,

Author of "True Blue," "The Burning Sea," "The 'Longshoreman," "Elmira," "The Mystery ,of Vilitelat;y Island," &c.

It was the very height of the hot season in Delhi, and the great Oriental city lay parched and panting. I leant back in a bamboo chair upon the verandah of my bungalow, languidly puffing at a cheroot; the hour was about five o'clock in the afternoon,and although the sun was drawing towards the horizon, the earth seemed to literally palpitate with the heat which radiated from it. This was my first hot season in India; I had come out about four months pre-viously as private secretary to a high political officer, and the slow process of grilling, to which I had been subjected ever since, was but little to my liking. My native servant, or syce, came stealthily to the pea French window, made me a profound salaam, and stood motionless, surveying me with his dusky glittering eyes.

"What is it, Rameer ?" said I. "May the dog of a servant speak some words to the sahib ?'.

"He may,' said I, sufficiently used by this time to the extravagant form of Eastern address to be easily able to preserve my gravity. He thrust his arm into the girdle of his robe, and pulled out a small yellow object, which he placed in my hands. It was a miniature model of a Chinese pagoda, and the very moment I felt its weight I made no doubt that it was pure gold. "Where did you get this thing, Rameer ?" said I, turning it about.

"I got it by digging in the grounds of the old ivadrama Temple, sahib." This was a wild, lonely spot, about a mile outside the walls of Delhi, and amid the ruins of the ancient city of Fezorabad. The temple was a dilapi- dated mosque, standing in the midst of a dank and weedy tract; few people ever, ventured there, unless it was some wretched pariah or starving fakir seeking shelter in the crumbling ruins, for, no doubt, the long wavering grass harboured many a deadly cobra and puff adder. "In the grounds of the old Guadrama Temple, eh?" said I, viewing the tall Hindu curiously. "And pray what might have set -on investigating in that spot?" "I will conceal naught from the sahib, as with him I can do everything; without him, nothing. Lately there called here a Lud2nash (priest), begging for alms. I fear these people, and so I brought him into the house, and gave him of victuals and drink. I fed him well on the sahib's bounty; I gave him pice out of my own pocket. As he was leaving he stopped, put his bony hand on my arm, drew me aside, and said: Hearken, faithful son of Siva, for you are the only one who has given me of charity this burning day, and I sadly need it, for lam grown old, and people have no longer 'faith in my reading of the planets. For whereas I am poor and my astrology tells me that I cannot live much longer, and you have given me alms, therefore will I tell you a priceless secret, which is written in no book,nor engraven upon any heart. Despise not what I tell you now, but take a spade; go, dig well in the grounds of the Guadrama Temple, and with patience you shall find riches beyond compute.' When he was gone I thought much over what he said, knowing him to be one who could divine secrets it is given to very few to read; so in the shadows of evening last night I took my spade, and dug as he bade me, and—the sahib knows the rest."

I confess I was a good deal astonished by this narrative, borne out as the truth of it was by the little golden image I held in my hand. And yet, on reflection, the thing was not so remarkable after all, seeing that it is well known to all people acquainted with India that the soil in the neigh- bourhood of the ancient cities literally teems with buried wealth. Down to within quite recent times a hole in the earth was the only bank of the rich Hindu. That Rameer should have taken me into his confidence was, to my mind, very strange; though there was truth in his remark that the secret would be of little use to him without the co-operation of one in a position to help him in using his know- ledge, and to protect him against the suspicion which must naturally fasten upon a native in his pondition at any sudden, unintelligible acquisition of treasure. I asked him why he had given his secret into my hands, and he answered, with true Eastern servility, that it would have been unworthy of his caste to keep it dark from the sahib, whose salt gave him life. Well, it was but right that I should act fairly by the honest syce, who thus put me into full possession of a secret which indeed might prove valueless, but which was equally likely to turn out priceless. So I proposed to him that we should work together in the matter; that I should obtain permission to explore the deserted temple garden, and provide all the necessary implements and labour; and that we should participate in equal shares in any treasure we might discover. Rameer seemed highly delighted by the suggestion, and acquiesced in it with eager- ness. I asked him whether he could undertake to find some natives who were thoroughly to be trusted, to dig up the grounds, and with his bland smile he answered: "Oh, yes, he would find plenty in the Subzee-Mundee who would eat of his chupattees, and in that Hindu fashion swear eternal faithfulness to-him."

My first business, of course, was to arm myself with the necessary permission, so that we might not be molested during the coUrse of our researches, for the Guadrama Temple was Government property. However,l anticipated no difficulty upon this score, as the Crown Commissioner was personally known to me, and the thing was trifling enough to ask as a favour. Indeed, that same evening, after dinner, I strolled across to his bungalow, near the Residency, and finding him at home, made as though I had come for a smoke and a chat. I artfully introduced the subject which was the real purport of my visit, and told him, with an air of feigned reluctance, as though I were ashamed of my superstitious weak- ness, that I had a dream of treasuie buried in the temple bagh, and that although I expected he would laugh at me, I nevertheless wished for his permis- sion to explore the wilderness, to ascertain whether there was any truth in the presentiment of my slumber. He chaffed me a little, in a good- humoured way, but said I was quite at liberty to dig up the whole place if I chose. "especially," he added, "as you will be doing a distinct service in the gratification of your whim, by clearing the pestilential place of its snake-haunted growth." This was all I wanted, saving one thing more, to which I readily gained a laughing assent: the per- mission to keep anything in the shape of treasure which our researches might bring to light. I bade my friend good night, promising to let him know "whether I got the value of a farthing's worth of silver spoons "to reward me for my pains. On my return home Rameer informed me that he had secured the services of four natives of his own caste in whom he could repose absolute confidence, and that they would be in readiness to commence operations at dawn on the morrow. I proposed making a very early start upon the following day, for when the sun had got to the height of an hour or two above the horizon the °heat would be insufferable, and we should most likely be obliged to knock off work. I slept but little that night, thanks to the mosquitoes, and the excitement, which I am free to confess worked like a subdued fever in my blood, at thought of the treasure which I fully believed we should dis- cover in• the ancient ' garden of the Guadrama Temple. Rameer called me about four o'clock, just as the first greenish streaks of dawn were beginning to shew upon the eastern gloom. I sprang up and rapidly completed my toilet, taking care to put on knee-high riding boots for fear of trampling upon a snake, and, after a hasty breakfast, I set off in company with my syce for the ruins of the temple. He had stripped himself of everything save'a loin-cloth and a loose-fitting turban, and over his shoulder he carried a couple of shovels an

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS, The Medical Officer's Salary Increased.

A meeting of this Boar I was held in the Board Room at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning. Mr. A. Brookes presided, and there were also present: Mrs. Clay, Rev. Father Wilson, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Dr. Murray, Messrs. J. W. Knowles (vice-chairman), A. J. Jolley, G. Pilling, T. H. Ashcroft, S. Davies, J. Payton T. Halton, G. Mather, J. Billinge, J. Turton, P. Longton, William Houghton, R. Forshaw A. Jolley, T. Ritchie, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), A. Wright (superintendent Padgate Schools.) . _ _

A TAILOR'S SITPER,&NUATION. Mrs. Woodhouse, foster mother at the Padgilte Cottage Homes having become incapable of discharging her duties with efficiency by reasor of permanent infirmity of body and in consequence thereof her office having become vacant, and the office of tailor having also become vacant, owing to their joint appointment and the Schools Committee having recommended that four years be added to the length of service of Mr. and Mrs. Woodhouse respectively under the poor laws, for the purposes of superannuation allowance, it was pro- posed by Mr. ASHCROFT that the addition of four years to such length of service be approved, and that the Clerk apply for the sanction of the Local Government Board to same.

Mrs. CLAY seconded the proposition. • Mr. J. W. KNOWLES asked how long Mr. Woodhouse had been in the employ of the Board. Mr. WOODHOUSE: Ten years. In answer to further questions, Mr. WOODHOUSE said he was entitled at the present time to a pension of is. 4d. but if four years of service were added it would increase the pension to about 10s. a week. Mr. J. W. KNOWLES said he ventured to say that very few tailors would have been prepared to give an employe, who had been with them ten years, a pension for life. He did not think they were elected to give awarthe ratepayers' money. There were many who had worked for private traders for many years who paver got a pension. As Guardians they were only entitled to act according to law and give that sum which the law directe.f. It was not within the reason- able scope of ,their duties to ask for the length of ser- vice of any officer to be increased and to make an ex- ception in this case. Beyond the pension which the length of service allowed, he did not think they were entitled to go. Mr. WILLIAM HouotrroNsaid the Schools Committee discussed the matter in a ery exhaustive way and they were unanimously of opinion that the application for a longer term of service be made. A great factor in assisting the committee to make the recommen- dation was the accident which Mrs. ' Woodhouse sustained whilst in their employandikvhich had disabled one of her hands. Another point was that the Board was so troubled with frivolous officers that when they got officers like Mr. and Mrs.Woodhouse, who did their work in such an exemplary and unobstrusive manner, it WM an incentive to others when the committee re- cognised it.

_ Sir. A. J. JOLLEY said the Board could not give an additional four years7pension without the corn mand of the Local Governnient Board. The Local Government Board was not in the habit of sanctioning additional years of service except for some very special qualifications. Unless some years were added to the service of Mr. and Mrs. Woodhouse the pension would be less than ten shillings a week and in that case it was quite possible that the Board would be asked for some out-relief to make the pension sufficient for them to live upon. The CLERK said the accident which Mrs. Woodhouse had sustained was an important point to put before the Board in considering the application. Mrs. CLAY said Mrs. Woodhouse had been a great sufferer for moßths.

The resolution was carried unanimously. DR. BRAIDE'S SALARY INCREASED.

THE QUESTION' OF A RESIDENT MEDICAL OFFICER. The House Committee reported that the application for an increase of salary was considered, and after con- siderable discussion, Mr. Billinge moved cold Mr. Alf Jolleyseconded that the salary be increased £5O. Mr. Brookes proposed as an amendment that a sub-com- mittee be appointed to thoroughly investigate the whole Question of the medical and nursing arrangements now in existence with a view of ascertaining whether it would not be desirable to place these arrangements on a different footing by the institution of a resident medical officer. Mr. Mather seconded. For theamend- meat fiva voted, for the increase five. The Chairman (Mr. Pilling) gave a casting vote for the increase of salary by £5O. Mr. GEORGE PILLING, in moving the confirmation of the minutes, made sympathetic reference to the illness of Mr. Greenway, who for a.period of 40 years has been the tailor at the Workhouse. Referring to the pro- posed increase of Dr. Braide's salary he contended that the medical officer was one of the worst paid officials of the Union.

Mr. ICNowLEs : What is his salary? Mr. PILLING : The salary is £lOO. That is all he gets for his snvices as medical officer of the house. Dr. Braide, continued Mr. Pilling, sometimes visited the house three or four times a day. He came every morn- ing and in special cases he was bound to come at any hour of the day or night. Mr. KNOWLES : His salary works out at about ss. a day.

Mr. PILLING : Yes. I calculate it at about half-a farthing per head in the house. Continuing, he said since Dr. Braide commenced the duties, the number of patients had almost !doubled: On the male side of the hospital there were 105 patients and the number was increasing every week. The wards on the female side of the hospital were also full. The Master had been instructed to purchase new beds as required and every- one must be convinced of the additional work which had been thrown on the doctor in recent years. When the application of Dr. Braide was read the Clerk ad- mitted that the statements made by the doctor were correct.

Mr. KNOWLES : Has he to visit each patient every day? Mr. PILLING : The head nurse selects all the special cases which need his attention, and he attends to them. There are some patients who require the doctor's atten- tion every morning. Mr. A. JOLLEY seconded the adoption a the minutes.

Mr. A. J. JOLLEY said he would certainly move an amendment. The position the Guardians were placed in was not a very pleasant one. It was neither easy nor sltisfactory for a layman to criticise in public a professional man, and he would endeavour to refrain from embarking on such criticism. He must say, how- ever, that he did not associate himself with the lauda- tory observations which had fallen from the Chairman. The amendment he (Mr. A. J. Jolley) had drafted read as follows, and he thought it would com- mend itself to the majority of the Guardians : "That the minute recommending an increase of Dr. Braide's salary be not confirmed but that a special com- mittee be appointed to consider the duties and emolu- ments of his various offices under the Guardians, to inquire into the medical and nursing arrangements of the Workhouse, and to take such action as they may deem necessary to enable them to report promptly and fully thereon." Proceeding, Mr. A. J. Jolley said the first interests they had to consider were those of the sick poor. In the second place, ranked the interests of the ratepayers, and in the third plqce, the interests of the medical officer. If the Guardians accepted the amendment he ventured to think that no injustice would be done to the medical officer but that while his interests were being considered the interests of the sick poor and the interestsla the ratepayers would receive careful attention. They could not consider the duties assigned to the medical officer as though they were included in a sort of water-tight compartment, and speak exclusively of his duties at the Workhouse. Dr. Braids held two or three offices under the Guardians. If Dr. Braide's remuneration as medical officer was being considered, his duties and emoluments connected with his various offices ought to be considered at the same time. He was not now putting the amendment forward for the purpose of suggesting a resident medical officer. That was an aspect of the case which required careful consideration. Ho had an open mind on the subject and most assuredly he would not have put the amendment forward had it not been for the suggestion to make so substantial an increase in the salary of the medical officer. •Mr GEORGE MATHER seconded the amendment. _ _ _ _ _ _

Mr. KNOWLES said that although Mr. A. J. Jolley so daintily touched the question of having a resident medical officer no doubt it showed that that was the object in view. Personally he was opposed to the appointment of a resident medical officer. He knew places where such an appointment did not work well. Friction arose between the Master and the resident ',medical officer as to whether the master was master or whether the resid- ent medical office' was to take upon himself some of the master's authority. It was much better to have a medical officer from the outside who could visit the house daily and be within easy reach on the 'telephone in case of emergency. It was well-known that doctors throughout the country were sadly underpaid for the services they rendered. They found Dr. Braide giving his serviees as medical officer of the house for ss. 6d. a day, if they counted seven days a week. It seemed a very small fee indeed. A reconstruction of the present scheme would mean great expense to the ratepayers. If a resident medical officer were appointed his salary would certainly grow, his drug bill would also grow, and many other things, including the cost of mainten- ance would grow. He was of opinion that an increase off£so to the Medical Officer's salary would in the long tun be a benefit to the ratepayers. Mr. R. FORSHAW :I didn t understand it was a question of appointing a resident medical officer. Mr. KNowt,Es T didn't say iG was. THE EDUCATION OF CHILDREN. Passive Inefficients." REPORT BY MR. J. FULTON SMITH.

At the annual meeting of the iVarrington Chamber Of Commerce on Monday, Mr. J. Fulton Smith presented a report, as the representative of the Chamber on the Warrington Education Committee. The Education Act of 1902, he said, came into opera- tion in the county borough on the Ist of April last year, when a committee was constituted by the Town Council of several of its members, in conjunction with representatives from various bodies; including this Chamber of Commerce, and the authority thus consti- tuted was empowered to take over the control and management of all the schools in the borough. The consideration of the exist- ing scholastic arrangements revealed the fact that those methods were totally inadequate and non-effective for educating and training the children of this town to the best advantage. The schools generally are imper- fectly adapted or equipped for teaching purposes. The buildings are defective in constructionand accommoda- tion, and there is a great want of light, ventilation and other conditions necessary to the health and comfort of those engaged in them. The appliances, apparatus, books, are either wanting or obsolete and unsuitable for present day demands. The first duty confronting the committee was to reorganize the schools, and to set up a higher standard of qualification and efficiency in the leaching staff—a slow process requiring much care and tact in dealing with those concerned, and in smooth- ing the way for unqualified assistants to become qualified and capable instructors. This unsatisfactory state of matters operated against the headmasters, hampering them in the most valuable part of their work, their time being taken up with details whichassistants, had they been properly trained, should have undertaken. The more advanced scholars suffered in consequence, as they were deprived of that personal influence andsupervision of the headmasters so essential to those about to leave school. There is a large work- ing class population whose earnings do not reach more than 20s. per week, and to such it is a matter of moment that their children should leave school as early as possible to become wage-earners, and contribute to the support of the family. The boys themselves are eager to get away from what they consider the thraldom *of school life to the illusory idea of freedom and inde- pendence. And so it comes about that exemption from school attendance is claimed as soon as the requisite number of attendances are made, although the boys and girls may not be able to pass the third or fourth standard. They may be styled "passive ineffici- eats," whose intelligence has not been awakened to a liking for any desire to improve their knowledge. Apprenticeship to trade begins at or before 14 years of age, and takes many a boy from school, although his parents may be able and willing to continue his school- ing beyond that age. At fourteen a boy is begin- ning to realise and to understand the value of his lessons, and the perceptive and retentive powers of the mind quickly develop. Those responsible in trade , societies for this regulation regarding entrance would do well to consider the effect of insist- ing upon the hard and fast rule in regard to age. An intelligent boy who enters a works at fourteen years of age is for a space'of, two years or so chiefly occupied in being little better than an errand boy, during which time there is great risk of his for- getting what has been taught him, and of losing the taste for further study. (Hear, hear.) Now, of the 1,000 children who leave the IVarrington schools annually, a very large proportion belong to the two classes referred to, who will become the foremen, artizans and workers of the future. An urgent duty devolves upon the Education Committee and teachers alike to remedy this weak spot in the educational system, and lessen the number of inefficients who are to pass into our workshops. It follows that the Chamber of Commerce, representing employer of labour, should be concernec' and have something to say in the educating and preparing of the youth of the town for business pursuits. Manufacturers and tradesmen bear a considerable share of the burden of

===== 0002601_19040130_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
local rates. It is only reasonable, therefore, for them to expect that more intelligence and aptitude to learn should be found in those seeking employment, and it ought to be within the province of this chamber to co- operate with the Education Committee in determining the character and scope of instruction best suited for handicrafts or a commercial career. As a suggestion may T commend to the serious consideration of the Chamber of Commerce that negotiations with) the Education Committee shouldjbe opened up for the inter- change of opinions and experiences of the aver- age boy from the scholastic as well as from the practical business point of View? If we are to have more efficiency, more appreciative and initiative ability, in our places of business and trade the foundation must be laid in the elementary schools bythoroughness in teaching the primary subjects of reading, writing, arithmetic and geography. The w)rk in connection with the elementary schools has up till now mainly taken up the attention of the committee, and it remains with them to proceed with a scheme for secondary education as required by the new Act. The responsibility of planning out a scheme has been thrown upon the local committees throughout the country, but no principle for guidance has been laid down by the Government. The Warrington Committee recently con- sidered and approved of dwell-thought-out scheme, drawn up by the Director of Education, as applicable to the borough, and a conference was held last week to con- sider the same along with a deputation from the Board of Education. The scheme is intended to bring about closer continuity in scholastic methods, and to adopt them with special reference to the requirements of this important manufacturing centre. It will, however, be proceeded with gradually, as those needs become better understood, and when it is ascertained how far the existing higher schools for boys and girls can be incor- porated and co-ordinated. The scheme is put forward and the efforts of the Education Committee are directed towards the solution of the problem of national educa- tion, that is to provide and secure to every boy and girl a fair chance to obtain the best education their abilities demand, irrespective of their position in life." (Applause.)

LONDON AND NORTH-WESTERN RAILWAY ENTERPRISE.

Local Advantages.

A reference to our advertising columns will show that the London and North-Western Railway Company have at last given Warrington a service which will enable the trading public to go to London and back the same day, after allowing nearly six hours in town for busi- ness. This is an achievement which will be greatly appreciated, as it means practically that in respect of a London service Warrington is now put on a footing of equality' with either Manchester or Liverpool. The service, it will be observed, is not only given to War- rington but residents at suburban stations—such as Broadheath, Lymm. Thelwall, Latchford, Moore, Acton Bridge, and other places—will also enjoy the privilege as the connecting trains have been altered so as to allow of their joining the Liverpool and London break- fast car express at Hartford. We are also glad to observe that better facilities are being given to the Midland counties. For many years past loud complaints have been made inconsequence of there not being a service that would enable passengers to get to say Birmingham before mid-day without starting at an unreasonably early hour in the morning. There will be no further cause for grumbling in this respect as the alterations proxide for passengers from all the places named getting to Stafford, Wolverhanipton and Birmingham before 10.30 a.m. with connections to all the important stations in South Staffordshire. The additional trains that have been arranged on the low-level line between Warring- ton, Lymm, Broadheath etc will, doubtless, be of great convenience to the residents of that district, but there is still wanting an improved afternoon service into Metnrhester, and we hope that in pointing this, fact out the accommodation will be forthcoming sooner or later. We note with much pleasure that the service from Scotland to Warrington in connection with the2p.m from Edinburgh and Glasgow will also be materially improved by the running of a new fast train from Wigan at 7 o'clock in the evening, thereby preventing passengers being subjected to a stopping train south of Wigan with the unpleasant change of trains at Newton- le-Willows' rs and for the convenience of passengers residing at Latchford and all intermediate stations up to Broadheath, the low level connecting train has been altered so as to cover the arrival of .passengers from Stotland, a similar convenience being also given to passengers for Acton Bridge. There has been a long- standing grievance in regard to the Belfast boat train, which leaves London at 5.35 p.m., not calling at War- rington, and we are pleased to hear that this is receiv- ing the serhitts consideration of the authorities, and it is to be hoped that before long it will be our pleasure to announce that this boon will have been conceded— or if not this, something equivalent. The arrangements in connection with the goods traffic have also been largely re-organised. The public can now rely upon delivery of goods the following day at most important centres, including such distant places as London and Glasgow. Through trucks have been I put on the service between many other places of importance, and altogether the facilities for the prompt conveyance of merchandise has been greatly improved.

HARRIERS.

WARM OTON v. SUTTON.

IFOn Saturday Warrington harriers ran their inter-club race with Sutton Harriers from the Glassmakers' Arms Sutton, Trail was laid over about 6 miles of heavy country, and after an absence of forty minutes, C. Straw the junior champion, appeared in sight. He was fol- lowed by J. Hall, his club mate, who was about a quar- ter of a mile ahead of the rest of the field. GATESHEAD'S NEW MEMBER.

Mr. John Johnson, the new Liberal mem- ber for Gateshead, was born in the little Northumbrian hamlet of Wapping, in the parish of Benton, and received such education as then fell in the way of miners' children in the village school. There was no corn- pulsory school attendance in

near Stanley, MR. JOHN JOHNSON, M.P. and soon grew

ledge of his fellows, for during the years oi his arduous service underground he had not been idle in seeking for mental improvement. Refraining from the many outdoor pleasures which engage .so much of the attention of the colliers in the north and elsewhere, he gave himself up to more intellectual courses, and was soon ahead of the majority of his fellows in mental pursuits. So it was that in the early struggles of the union Mr. Johnson was a powerful speaker, a fearless advocate of the rights of the miners. When by the death of the late Mr. Crawford, a vacancy arose in the places of the agents he was elected treasurer and agent to the Dur- ham Miners' Association. Nine years later they elected him their financial secretary, the post he now fills. He has also been honoured with the confidence of the electors by being returned as a member of the County Council. Mr. Johnson is a member of a Primitive Methodist body, he was former- ly a Sunday school superintendant and a class leader, and has now for twenty-five years been a local preacher. One of his brothers is a well-known minister of the denomination, the Rev. Mathew Johnson, of: Hetton, co. Durham.

KOREA AND MANCHURIA.

From day to day for the last three months the great question has been "peace or war ? " but the negotiations have, owing to the pro- crastination of diplomacy, dragged on week after week. The attitude of Russia and Japan has been that of two wrestlers feinting at each other, looking .for an opening or a sign of weakness, and waiting to close. Japan, cer- tainly, has shown none, and, if the yielding comes, it will undoubtedly be from Russia, who is keeping the whole of Europe on ten- terhooks, preaching peace whilst preparing for war. In the East itself she is forced to be more or less passive in case of giving offence or committing any hostile action, which might easily precipitate war, especially in the pre- sent state of Japanese temper. So ruffled is the latter. and at such a pitch of tension is the feeling of the country, that it is feared peace would, even with full concessions, be none too popular, and unquestionably a strong hatred of Russia has been aroused through- out the length and breadth of the tight little island. The Russian fleet in the main ap- pears to be hemmed in in Vladivostock, which it dare not leave for fear of bringing things to a head; and thus, if war breaks out, Japan has an initial advantage by sea. On i land t. lopks as though the fighting would centre itself in Kotea, and both countries are making their preparations round this "Tom Tiddler's " ground of the East. Korea itself appears to be in a state of ferment, if not actual revolt. Korea stands where Japan stood many years ago before the "young Japan" party inaugurated the progressive measures, which have brought it to such a pitch of modernity and perfect organisation. Korea, however, is even more dilletante, even more the land of the lotus-eater. The high- caste Korean, none more than the Emperor, abhors hurry and bustle, and is the personi- fication of a well-bred inertia and laziness,

ONE OF THE MASSIVE STONE BRIDGES ON THE MANCHURIAN LINE AND THE TYPE OF ENGINE IN USE.

which go so far as to disdain the least effort. Even when riding, he prefers a man at each ler, to assist him! Eating, sleeping, doing nothing, waited on hand and foot, he passes a life of utter indolence, and he loathes and dreads any bother and fuss. Anything which will upset the even tenour of his way is ab- horrent, and war and rumours of war he hates. To be let alone, that is his sole ambition. He wlslips to play no part in politics, and the pre- sent prominence of his native land in the international game of diplomacy is distasteful to him. If anyone insists upon taking Korea under his wing, protecting and developing it, the Korean would prefer Japan; but, above all, he would prefer to he left alone to work out his own salvation in his own way. But both Powers are closing in upon this land of the lotus in view, of eventualities, and there appears to be a serious rising, if not an insur- rection, against the Government. The pro- gressive leaders are gaining more power, and in the south there are serious riots against the official tax - gatherers. Several thousand• Koreans have been called to arms in a sud- den access of energy, and Seoul, the capital, has the eyes of the whole world focussed upon it to its intense disgust. The Seoul-Fusan railway is being pushed on as fast as pos- sible under Japanese .auspices, and Major- General Ijichi, of the Japanese Army, has arrived in the. Korean capital. Advices irom Chefoo announce that Russian troops are slowly moving upon the Korean frontier in readiness in case of eventualities, training to Haichen and proceeding on foot to the Man- churian tide of the Yalu. Their security of tenure is very fragile as the last fifteen hundred odd lines of the single railway is not only infested by hostile tribes, but has according to report been mined by the Chinese in the Newehang district. In event of war General Kuropatkin will command the Russian troops and land forces, and, of course, Admiral Alexieff will be supreme at sea. 'lt is now definitely stated that a Chinese protec- torate over l‘lanchuria is absolutely insisted upon by Japan as the first clause of a peace- ful settlement. 'Meanwhile, poor indolent Korea is shaking in its shoes and wishing it were all over.

THE SOMALILAND V.C. HERO.

The act of courage for which the Victoria Cross has been conferred on Captain and Brevet-Major J. E. ,Gough, of the Rifle Bii- gade, is one of the most conspicuous acts of bravery yet seen in Somaliland. During the action at Daratoleir on the 22nd of April last, Major Gough assisted . C a p- tains Walker and Rolland in carrying back the late Captain Bruce, who had been mortally wounded, and preventing that officer from fall- ing into the hands of the enemy. Cap tains , Walker and Rol- land have al- ready been awarded t h e Victoria Cross for-their gallan-

try on this occa-

siojn, but, Major CAPTAIN AND BREVET-MAJOR Gough, who was J.• E. GOUGH, ,V.C. in command of the column, made no mention of his own conduct, which has only recently been,, brought to notice. The war correspondent, the late Mr. W. T. Maud, described the gallant part played by Major Gough, but his despatch • was censored by that officer, who passed everything in it except mention of himself. Subsequently, when his despatches were no longer subject to Major Gough's censorship, Air. Maud ,sent the full facts. Maio, Gough has also .been gazetted Brevet-Licutenant- Colonel.

RAILWAY AND CANAL COMBINED.

The latest and, perhaps, the most anoma- .ous use to which electrical power has beat put is instanced at the Teltow Canal, near Berlin, where the boats and barges are now towed by this means. It seems strange, at first sight, to have a railway to drag canal boats, but the idea is eminently practicable. The carrying capacity of the canal is con- siderably increased, as the system in use is zapable of drawing greater loads at a faster pace than the obsolete method, while it is

THE NEW ELECTRIC TOWING ENGINE.

Claimed to be cheaper, as the expense•caused by damage to the banks and bottom of the water-way is reduced to a minimum. Before deciding on the system to be used the Teltow L-anal administration offered prizes, which were keenly competed for by twenty invent- ors. In the system selected the power is obtained from a specially - built generating station, the current being conveyed by over- aead wires.

THE WEST COUNTRY CENTENARIAN.

Whatever may be said in disparagement of our climate, somehow it seems on the whole to be pretty healthy. That is to say, the death rate is not particularly high, and we manage to produce as many centenarians and old people as other countries. ' Take as an example the grand old Admiral who

died last week and was at

ninty-f o u r. as

fine - and hale

a specimen of an old English gentleman, full of energy and

activity, as one could wish to

see. On the

day of his funeral Mrs.

Bremridge, the

famous -centen-

arian of the West country, celebrated her

103rd birthday

at-Sidmoutli MRS. BREMRIDGE. amidst her large

circle of friends. Earlier in the ,week she had been slightly ailing and had given a certain amount of anxiety to her relatives: but, as her birth- day came round, she threw off her passing indisposition by the aid of her strong con- stitution, and on Thursday she was well enough to receive and appreciate the many presents and messages of congratulation which arrived at Spring Gardens. Amongst the former was one from Queen Alexandra, which was highly appreciated: and this Mrs. Bremridge acknowledged in her own hand- writing. porn in 1801, when King George 111. was on the throne, she is, as she has full possession of her faculties able clearly to remember the battle of Waterloo and the , great sensations of the day, when " Boney's " landing was feared in the West country, She was the daughter of a Barnstaple solicitor, and in 1824 married Mr. Bremridge, whc twice represented her native place in Parlia- ment. Her married life lasted just (wet fifty years, and she celebrated her golden wedding in 1874. She is one of the fe\N, people living who can clearly remember foul coronations. Now, despite her age, het memory is unimpaired, and she is able tc take part and interest in the affairs of every- day life: and er conversation is noticeabl( for its charm. Truly Mrs. Bremridge is a wonderful old lady !

THE NORTHERN UNION.

FIRST DIVISION.

KEIGHLEY v. WARRINGTON.

Fully 3,000 persons witnessed this match at Keighley. The home side had to face the slope at the outset, and with one or two exceptions they were kept on the stretch resisting the determined inroads of the power- ful Warrington team. In these movements, Hockenhall, Kenyon, Dickenson, and Isherwood took prominent parts, and but for really superb defence on Keighley's part, A. Pearson being conspicuous, a decisive lead must have been won in the early stages. Then Keigh- ley, helped by Crocker and Myers' well-known move- ments, changed the venue, .and Pearson served by Jagger would have scored a try had not the ball slipped from his grasp in grounding, and half-time arrived with the score sheet blank.

The second half found Keighley early in tro üble, but they soon became invaders. E. Parson aryl Myers were both temporarily hurt, but resumegand Myers a moment later served his companion, who nearly got over. Myers essayed a drop, which was too low, and then A. Pearson intercepted a visitor's pass outside the quarter-line and dropped a goal. From this point it was clear the home side had taken the measure of their opponents, and Crocker, Myers, and Jagger were among the busiest. Walker landed a penalty goal from near the centre, and this left Keighley winners by 2 goals (4 points) to Warrington nil. Teams : Keighley.—Walker, back; Jagger, Hardman A. THE GENERAL ELECTION.

It is coming. If not thii yew, next ; if not not year, the year after. Of course there will be lively times. General elec- tions are always lively. But this particular general election thit is hurrying up is going to be the hardest fought within the memory of living Englishmen. Poor John Bull ! He will be pulled right and pulled left. He will be thruit forward and held backward. He will •be tcld that his greatest achievements lie in the future. It. will be dinned into his ears that his star has for ever set and that all is over with him. Tim Free rPooders will pat his capacious. stomach, and advise hint to continue to fill it with foreign produce at cheap rates. The Tariff Reformer will hold before his eyes pictures of closed factories and processions of starving workmen, at the same time asking him what he intends to do about it. Verily, the turmoil will be great.

But amid all tile pother about food, and the best way of providing it for John Bull's little family of forty I millions Odd, no politician' will utter a word about its proper digestion,though food derives all its value from that. That task will fall upon the proprietors of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup,a sure cure for indiges-• ition and biliousness, made from fruits, roots and herbs. Ifor thirty-five, years the principal family medicine in use throughout the world.l • I"First nty appetite failefl and I could eat nothing with relish," writes Mrs. Middleton, of 14, Slades I Green Lane, Erith, Kent, on the 13th October, 193. 'Then food lcogan to cause ITI3 pain, waier increased to such an extent that at last I became afraid to eat. My ' stomach had lost the power to digest. My energy gradually left me. Every day I found myself more I languid and unlit for work. The least exeftion tired I me, and generally I was so drowsy that I could have I gone to sleep at any moment. T was attended by no i fewer than three doctors, but not one of them succeeded in relieving me. Though I left off solid I foOd and lived entirely on fluids I got no better. At /aft.l had to take to my bed, where I remained for nearl a year. Then I became an inmate of the Sea- man's Hospital at Greenwich, but derived_ no benefit from my stay there for nineteen weeks. As a last re- source I tried what Seigel's Syrup Could do for me and was much ralievoi by the first bottle. After that my , progress was stealy and continuous, until T was soon quite well again. ielieve that Mother Seigel's Syrup saved my life." The suffering is great when honest folks find it diffi- cult to obtain food, but not so great as the sufferings of those who, having it, are unable to digest it.

===== 0002601_19040130_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
DOES EUROPE E \IST?

BY G. K. CHEST.ERTON.

When controversies have been going on for a long time they tend to become so hot and confused that any cause is misstated both by its opponents and by its supporters. I do not mean merely that any cause and the merits of any cause tend to be exaggerated by the lovers of that cause. Of course the lovers of the cause exaggerate it, like all lovers. I mean that they tend to forget what it is. This is peculiarly so .with the old Liberal policy regarding the relations of Turkey and Europe. Liberals and Conservatives alike too often talk as if Mr. Gladstone's horror at the Bulgarian massacres had been humanitarian and nothing else, as if lig had been indignant at the slaughter of so many Christians for Turkish policy, as he might have been indignant at the slaughter of so many birds for ladies' hats. He had this indignation because he was a healthy and complete man, capable of indignation. Some people are not capable of it. A large number of modern strong men and Empire-builders view destruc- tion with the colossal calm of Napoleon, for the simple reason that they have not the animal spirits to be angry. Mr. Gladstone ond the old Liberal statesmen possessed this quality, and therefore no doubt they were angry about the mere physical agony and the mere physical waste of life in the perennial Turkish problem. But it is altogether wrong to suppose that they thought only, or even primarily, of this. It is altogether to miss the point to talk as if Mr. Gladstone and his party opposed any pact with, or concession to, Turkey from a mere disgust at the sight of blood. I repeat that they were not indifferent to this, and that if our imperial moderns are indifferent to it, it is not by any means because they are more virile. They are simply specimens of a type common in all high civilisations • and they like to have a good deal of blood all round them because they have very little inside. But all this was only an emotional aspect of the matter. Liberalism opposed pacts with, or concessions to Turkey because it had a certain philosophy of European politics. The real root of Mr. Gladstone's politics was something larger even than his particular protest; it was that he held a certain •view of Europe. He believed a rather curious thing about Europe; a thing that amid the general discussion, on the subject would very seldom be admitted. He believed that it existed. When our living Imperialists use the word Europe, it is much to be feared that they generally mean the whole of Europe except England. Allowance must be made.for their subtle anti-patriotic bias; but the fault of the matter is very evident. The modern politician will always prefer the thing which is defined and ticketed to the thing which is pervasive and vague. Believing himself to be a practical man he will believe in nothing he cannot see, which is one of the most unpractical maxims there are, as anyone may find by blowing the gas out. And this indifference to the thing which is without ocular boundaries constitutes the greatest difficulty we have in persuading our politicians to deal seriously with the case of the Turks in Europe. If you asked a modern -)olitician of the type devoted to what is •J strangely called efficiency, whether there was such a thing as Germany, he would answer that of course there was. It had such and such boundaries, such and such treaties; its flag, he would say, is so and so, its constitution written in such and such a book. Surely there can be no question about the existence of 'a thing which is thus defined in rigid utterances, presented by ascertained rituals, which is traced in histories, which is marked in maps. But suppose that you asked that politician another question. Suppose you say: "And do you know that there is in the world another thing older and more anonymous than this old and anonymous Germany, darker and more mysterious than this dark and 'mysterious Germany, larger and more indeterminate than this large indeterminate Germany, more living and more immortal than this living and immortal Germany ? It has no boundaries; it has no laws; it is powerful, but has not a single regiment; it is august, but no man has ever seen its banner; it has neither judge nor gibbet, but it is obeyed. Do you know that there is a thing called Europe ? ' if you say this to him he will say that you are talking mysticism. Perhaps you are, for mysticism is the same as common sense; they are two forms of the same one thing, the' thing which pedants cannot understand. It is by spiritual atmospheres, and by these alone, that we do all the practical things of life. Everybody knows, at any rate, that it is by mere mystical atmospheres that one decides to engage a housemaid. The same kind of common sense which leads one to know without any logical proof, that one housemaid is good and another bad, is perfectly capable of telling its this simple thing—that Europe exists. That is to say, there is a real community by which we live and to which we owe allegiance, which is the community which would in old days have been called Christendom, and to which we now have to give the older pagan name of Europe. I need not multiply examples of the practical solidarity of that Europe. We all know perfectly well that if a brilliant ethical. thinker appeared tlimorrow morning in Russia we should in a short time have heard of him. We all know that if a brilliant ethical thinker appeared tc-morrow morning in China (as he very likely may and very likely has) we should not hear of him. We all know that as the various nations of Europe once competed in the Crusades they now compete in scientific' gunnery, and that suggestions come from all parts of Europe in one common European atmosphere. We all know that., physical science, itself the newest of the forces, has only confirmed in its practice the old com- munity of Europe in the study of the Latin grammar. For Latin is still the lihytta communi.,, although this great tongue, whiCh was once employed for the definition of God, is now employed for the definition of microbes. The man who fancies that the civilisations of England and France and Germany and Italy and Russia do exist, but that the civilisation of Europe does not exist., is committing the most colossal blunder that a man can commit— he is preferring the thhigs that are seen which are temporal to the things which are not seen which are eternal. There are, as I say, many arguments, ranging from republicanism-to beer, ranging from Dante to dynamite, by which one might te prepared to prove the reality and unity of Europe. But the best of all the arguments is the simplest. It is the fact that into this European unity there has by an historical accident intruded a very small wedge or outpost of r.notlier c iii at on ; that outpost has suoceeden Itl:ettitig it:sell treated as a European' 1 ..t‘4o 44 11.1, European privileges: ard ' ifs-. or

outpost has succeeded in making itself more of a problem in our continent than all the gigantic nations of that gigantic continent put together.

-The real point about the old Liberal policy In the Near East is not that it was humanf. tarian politics, but that it was philosophical politics. It had this profoundly sensible realisation of a European civilisation against which the Turk militated. He militated against it when he was a Turkish brigand; but he would have militated against it quite as much if he had been a Turkish angel. What Mr. Gladstone believed was that certain ideas and moralities lie behind political facts and problems. He believed, for instance, that no European people can be pei manently cured of ;+,, hoh;t nf it:,eif as a people. 'iv nether tie was rigit. in the Doer affair, he was not acti:.:_ .iy as a moralist or a dreamer. The force • f.ind the Majuba settlement was not magnanimity, it was nationalism. Its originator believed in nationality, not merely because it was an ideal to be set against the brutal facts of conquest or commerce. He believed in it because he thought it was much more of a fact than conquest or commerce, a much older fact, a much fresher fact, a much more dangerous fact. He was not in these matters an opportunist, because he did not think oppor- tunism practical. That is to say, he was peculiarly a philosophical politician. What was true of him in the smaller and more doubtful case of the Transvaal, was true of him in the larger and more generally accepted difficulty of the Near East. It Is again to miss the point altogether to say that he wished for an alliance of Christians against Turks instead of the old Palmerston policy of an alliance with Turks against Christians, merely because he had a sensitive conscience, and thought massacres wicked. He wished it because he thought that again he was on the side of the eternal common sense against the temporary common sense. An Oriental civilisa- tion, an Oriental philosophy, an Oriental view of life, was planted in Europe as a European power, schemed with Etiropean diplomatists, fenced itself with European armaments, dotted its city with European hotels. And it kept within it, century after century, the sacred fire of a non-European ideal, the holy secret of a totally Asiatic faith and a totally Asiatic cruelty. The rationalistic politician of modern Imperialism says: "This is a European Power; let us treat with it, let us take account of it, let us bargain with its diplomatists, let us use its guns." The philosophical politician says: "This is not a European Power; it may be as good as we, it may be better than we, but it is not of us. Every convenience you arrange with it, every advantage you gain through it, is only putting off the evil day of an essential antagonism You are doing something. much more coarse and common than merely opposing justice; you are putting your money on the wrong horse.°

Cured by Bile Beans.

When a minister speaks for a medicine depend upon it it's good. When he tries it it's better. When it has cured him it's best of all. A well-known Baptist minister in Lincolnshire has proved that for indigestion and allied ailments Bile Beans are absolutely unequalled. They cured him; and in gratitude he penned the letter printed below. Indigestion, by the way, is a disease which is so varied in its if symptoms that scores of people have it and don't know it. They only know they're feeling bad. There is a loss of appetite, frequent belching of wind, sometimes pain, not only in the stomach but round the heart. At other times the sufferer is ravenously hungry, but a heavy meal, although enjoyed, brings on acute pain. Some- times there is dizziness ; at others specks before the eyes. Pain in the left arm is another symptom of this strange disease. Bile Beans cure it by toning up the whole digestive system. The Rev. J. Lindsey, the minister referred to above, writes:—

Donington, Spalding.

Dear Sirs, I beg to add my testimony to the benefit I have derived from the use of your Bile Beans. Some short time ago I was a sufferer from indigestion, which brought on severe pain. WARRINGTON'S TREASURE HUNT.

Extraordinary Street Scenes.

Hunting for hidden treasure has become quite a common ocdupation of late, and no one seemed astonished on Saturday night when Warrington suc- cumbed to the craze. Extraordinary, not to say amus- ing street scenes followed the publication of the "clues." In an incredibly short time it was noised abroad that a medallion entitling the finder to £25 was hidden near the cannons at Arpley. Crowds of people were soon hurrying to the spot. There were men, womenand children—nearly all bent upon the one object of enriching themselves, but as far as we can hear, no one succeeded in unearthing the treasure. Of course there was a certain number of unconcerned observers who came merely to enjoy the fun. The hunters followed the newspaper directions with all seriousness, "Thirty paces forward, and ten to the right ! " one would hear ' them exclaim, and then the distances would be carefully I measured out. This task completed, knives and other implements were brought into requisition and the searchers did not relinquish their efforts until they had left unmistakable evidences of their energy on the surface of the road. Every nook and corner in the vicinity was explored. We heard of one smartly-dressed individual who decided that a grid was the most likely hiding place. Handing his coat to a small boy, he rolled up his shirt sleeves and engaged in a fruitless search in the mud. Some young fellows peered into the cannon mouths but'received no reward for their pains. The lights from numerous lamps and candles le2t a further novelty to the scene. In addition to the disappointment of going empty away, the searchers had to endure the chaff of spectators who viewed the hunt from windows on the station premises. The damage to the road could not be allowed to go on unchecked and information being sent to the Chief Constable, a number of officers were specially told to prevent any further mischief. Until early on Sunday morning search parties were at work and during the day. other people renewed the hunt. Policemen patrolled the neighbourhood and kept the crowd well in hand. The Chief Constable took further precautions on Monday by issuing the following notice :

POLICE WARNING

"Whereas acts of damage have been committed in this town and other districts by persons in search of hidden medallions (said to entitle the' finders to money from the owners of a certain news- paper),the said form of destructive nuisance being now commonly known asa " treasure hunt," this is to give notice that the district of Warrington affected by this craze is for the present under con- stant police supervision, special o.flicers being told off to keep a continuous night and day watch upon the neighbourhood. The police will promptly arrest any person detected in the act of damaging any public or private property, and thepenalty of such acts is at the discretion of the magistrates, imprisonment without the option of a file. The advertising paper distinctly points out that destru- tive conduct,' far from being necessary would be wasteful effort, in the endeavour to find the ob- jects said to be concealed." THE LIABILITY OF THE INSTIGATORS.

At Manchester, on Wednesday, Mr. Yates, the sti- pendiary, in infiictin fines on a number of persons charged with committing damage to highways in search for hidden treasure. said in his judgment under the Highway Act, any person damaging the surface of the highway was liable to a penalty, and by Jervis's Act every person aiding, abetting, counselling, or procuring the commission of the offence by Others was liable to summary conviction. He would have.. no hesitatiGn in convicting newspaper proprietors. .

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Baptist Church, Colborne-street. On Sunday afternoon a united meeting of the Sunday school and Bible class was held, when the annual prizes for regular attendance were distributed to the scholars of the Sunday school and the half-yearly prize distri- bution to the Bible class in connection with the Book Fund, took place. The prizes consisted of Bibles, hymn books and other volumes, in all,over one hundred hand- some books being presented. The Pastor spoke a few words, and the Secretaries gave short reports on the year's work. Musical selections were admirably ren- dered.—ln the evening the Pastor preached from the text, "Thoujart not far from the kingdom," at the outset explaining that "The Kingdom of God" meant the kingdom within, God taking possession and control of the life. The character and attitude of the young man ' addressed by Christ in the text were studied, and while commending many points the Pastor uttered a warning against being near the kingdom and yet outside. Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. At this Church on Sunday special sermons were preached by the Rev. John S. Simon, governor of Dids- bury College. There was a fair congregation at the morning service, when Mr. Simon showed how St. Jude was gravely concerned at the creeping in of ungodliness amongst his fellow apostles, how near he felt the approach of the second coming of our Lord Jesus Christ, and how his desire was for them to keep more strictly , to the holy faith, once for all delivered to the Saints— the great central belief in the atonement of Jesus Christ. The anthem "0 worship the King," was creditably rendered by the choir. The evening service was well attended, when Mr. Simon preachetl eloquently on the subject of Jesus Christ drawing disciples by the way, illustrating three different types of men who, being drawn by the attractiveness of Christ, felt they could follow Him anywhere. Mr. Simon showed how the followers of Christ must be prepared at the commence- ment, for a series of self-sacrifices,ln giving up habits that could not agree with the Christian life. The choir ably rendered the anthem "Sun of my soul." The 'collections amounted to Rll Os. 9d. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.—Open Meeting. A very large congregation assembled in Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday afternoon on Ow occasion of an open meeting in connection with the P.S.A. Society. The President (Rev. John Yonge) pre- 'sided and briefly introduced Mr. W. Beecher Smith, of the Y.M.C.A.. Manchester, who delivered the address Mr. Smith, who had recently returned from a visit to Canada, spoke of some of the lessons to be learned from his experiences. He based his remarks on the text "The sea is His and He made it.. His hands formed the dry land." In referring to the sea as a great separator of families and of kindred, the speaker reminded his hearers of the Fatherhood of God, whose love for man- kind was illimitable and knew no physical boundaries. The Apollo Male Voice Choir sang with marked pre- cision and ability three pieces entitled "The Crusaders," "The Pilgrim's Chorus" and "The long day closes." "The Pilgrim's Chorus." which was sung with organ accompaniment, was especially well sung, the organ giving tone and effect to the whole. Mr. George took the solo part and Mr. Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at. the organ. Mr. T. Brough conducted the choir in his usually able manner. The New Vicar of St. James', Latchford. The Rev. H. B. Firth, the newly-appointed Vicar of St. James', Latctiford, was on Monday evening the recipient of a token of esteem from the congregation at Runcorn Parish Church, where he had been senior curate for nearly ten years. The gift was presented to him at the annual congregational party and entertain- ment and took the form of a handsome gold hunter watch and chain. The watch bears the following inscrip- tion:—" To the Rev. Herbert Boisneau Firth,M.A., vicar of St. James' Latchford. A token of affectionate esteem from Runcorn, his first and only curacy, where his work for nearly ten years has endeared him tc;:the parishioners. January 2,ith, 1904." The Vicar (Rev. Canon Wood) eulogised Mr. Firth's work in the parish and Mrs. Wood made the presentation. In the course of his reply, the Rot. H. B. Firth, who had a very hearty. reception, said they would like to know that he had had the kindest and heartiest reception at Latchford. (Applause.) It had been painful to sever his connection with Runcorn, but he could assure them that he had gone among a warm hearted people and already had a good number of new friends about him. They had in St. James' parish a girls' friendly society, a mothers' meeting, and nearly all the organizations he had been accustomed to so that he hadthe same sort or work, and he supposed this was the case everywhere, though places were different. He again thanked them very much for the present, and he could assure them no possession would be dearer to him than that which had come from his old friends in Runcorn. (Applause.)
