THE EXAMINER.

JA.NUARY ZOth 1904.

THE tragic end of THE MORA.L .OF Mr. WHITAKER WHITAKER WRIGHT. WRIGHT has excited sympathy, but it has not olosctired the moral of his career. He was a speculator on the largest scale, who resorted to fraud when threatened by disaster. He hood- winked honourable men like Lord DUFFERIN and Lord Lace, and he persuaded himself, apparently, that false balance-sheets are wcll within the code of commercial morality. It is not at all difficult to believe that the man was sincere when he said in court that he had done only what ninety-nine out of a hundred directors of companies would have done in his place. It is incredible that they would have done any- thing of the kind; but a financier of the WHITAKER WRIGHT stamp would naturally argue quite gravely that he was employing a temporary deception for the good of his shareholders, and all would have been well if the luck had not gone against him. He looked upon the fraudulent balance-sheet as a piece of strategy, justified by the rules of the game. To find himself arraigned as a criminal filled WHITAKER WRIGHT with a genuine I surprise. In 'his own eyes he was simply unfortun- ate, like NAPOLEON after Moscow. Moreover, it was plain that the confidence of his attitude was largely inspired by the stranle procedure of the Law Officers of the Crown. The Attorney-General ,

had declined to institute a public prosecution. The Solicitor-General had scoffed at the idea that a man could be successfully prosecuted for issuing a false balance-sheet. It was left to unofficia people like Mr. ARNOLD WHITE, who has suffered for his zeal, to bring the culprit to justice. There was a long struggle over the extradition question in America. Unremitting effort and great ex- pense were needed before this man could be put upon his trial, and before it could be shewn that Sir ROBERT FINLAY and Sir EDWARD CARSON were entirely mistaken in their construction of the law. This is a matter which demands the serious attention of Parliament. If the judgment of the Law Officers had been acquiesced in there would have been no prosecution, and it would have been tacitly admitted that a financier may forge a balance-sheet with impunity. Of course the Law Officers did not welcome such a conclusion. They simply held that the statute was defective, and although they talked of further legislation, there was a poor prospect of its being amended. Every- body knows that legislation which enforces the personal responsibility of company &rectors is sornehew very difficult to get. But it turns out that the statute was perfectly effective for the punishment of 'WHITAKER WRIGHT'S offence. There was never any doubt about it in the minds 'of judge and jury. Mr. Justice BIGHAM gave the I plainest direction, and he jury had no difficulty in following it. They rejected the suggestion that the fraudulent balance-sheet was no more than the tactical dressing of the shop window. They established a precedent of the greatest possible value in the regulation of company finance. But why was not this useful result brought about by the Public Prosecutor? What is the good of that official if his hands are to be tied by the extraordinary timidity of the Attorney-General and the Solicitor- General ? How was it that they knew so little about the statute they professed to in,erprat ? These are questions which call for debate in the House of Commons. There are friends of the Government in the Press who are disposed to be reticent- on this subject; but nothing could be more admirably direct than the candour of the Times : "There has been a mistake somewhere ; and it would be only seemly for the Governmea Ito recoup fully to the prosecutors all costs which they have incurred—not forgetting the public ser- vices in this matter of Mr. FLowza.—in bringing to justice a culprit of a kind that, under our laws, fortune generally favours." In future fortune, we hope, will have a different bias ;. but for that no thanks will be due to the Law Officers of the Crown.

THE meeting of MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S Parliament nex t CALCULATION, week will not find the Ministerial party in such exuberant spirits as their journals would have US believe. Many Unionists who are quite pre- pared to vote against any Amendment to the Address ate plunged in despondency. They admit that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has captured the party organisation ; but th9.t seems to be poor comfort.s Wnat is the use of a policy which works the mica if it does not impress the country? There was a time when the average party man on the Unionist side thought that everything was going hopefully for the new tactics. They seemed to have done pretty well at Lewisham and Dulwich. But Norwich and Gateshead dispelle this dream ; and now the average Unionist has a gloomy misgiving that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is lead- ing the bulk of his party into the wilderness, where their sojourn will be indefinitely prolonged. The attitude of the Unionist Free Traders is pro- voking some of Mr.CHAmBERLAIN's friends to such threats as Mr. WALTER LONG has employed against Sir JOHN DICKSON-PoYNDEn.At Liverpool, on the other hand, there is a desperate effort to make a compromise between the two sections of the party, the CHAMBERLAIN stalwarts under- taking not to oppose candidates who will pledge themselves to Mr. BALFOUR'S Fiscal policy. A compromise, however, to be effe2tive should be equilateral ; but the Unionist Free Traders who observe what Lord HUGH CECIL calls a benevolent neutrality towards Mr. BALFOUR'S policy will not extend that benevolence to Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. The Prime Minister seems to be calculating that the generalities of his " Economic Notes " will serve to stave off curiosity snout the working of Retaliation. Mr. WINaTON CHURCHILL already wants to know on what occasions Mr. BALFOUR proposes to retaliate, against what country, and by what procedure. Will Parliament be consulted in ev3ry case, or will the Government act by Order in Council ? To such questions it is highly im- probable that Mr. CHURCHILL will get any answer next week. Mr. BALFOUR will say that his policy is before the country id principle, and that he is not obliged to dismiss the details in the presen Parliament, which will not be asked to deal with the question of Fiscal reform.

On the surface this is plausible; but it is quite out of keeping with public opinion. It is very well for Mr. BALFOUR to postpone the whole subject and to invite the House of Commons to consider other things. But at every by-election the Fiscal issue predominates, and refuses to be postponed. While he electors are returning heavy majorities against Protection in any shape or form, it will be slightly absurd for Mr. BALFOUR, to pretend that nothing of the kind is going on, and that the public mind is absorbed in the Ministerial pro - gramme for the session. It :is this consideration which strengthens the doubt in Unionist circles whether the life of this rarliament can be prolonged after the Budget. Some Minis- terialists anticipate a dissolution in May. They argue that the situation is too artificial to last through the session, and that it is likely to be ended by Mr. CHAMBERLAIN himself. He must see that delay makes the Ministerial position worse, and that the longer the inevitable defeat at the polls is postponed the more crushing it will be. His calculation is that the Liberals will come in with a not too commanding majority, and tha their internal difficulties will break up the new Ministry in two years. There is no unwillingness on our side to give Mr. CHAMBEELAIN the oppor- tunity of testing his prediction. If these are hi real views, we have at least the felicity of agreein s with him that the Unionist Government has lost all moral hold upon the country, and that the sooner it takes its beating at the General Election the better.

A very heavy Council agenda has been issued for next Tuesday's meeting, and we fear both public and press are fated to endure a protracted sitting. There is a lot of very important and i interesting business,much of t concerning schemei which are already in hand or on the carpet. The latter will probably claim most attention, for it seems this month as if the Town Council was going to rectify deficiencies all round, and at the

same time increase the remuneration of its officials Tha bridge scheme—or rather a bridge scheme— oorns largely before us. Councillor Magian will propose the preparation of plans for a 6Xt. iron single span bridge across the river at Howley. With such undertaking we are in entire sympathy. A similar project has been talked about for years, but it has never taken practical shape. The need,of direct communication between the east end of the town and Cheshire has been severely felt as the town has increased, and it is getting time something was done. Apart from the convenience of a bridge here, the scheme would result in the opening out of a lot of valuable land which is at present inaccessible, and we imagine the pro- spective increase in ratable value from develop- ments hereabouts will form Councillor Maginn's main argument. We are glad to find him on surer ground than he was a month ago. A sub-committee has also been formed to consider and report upon the widening of Warrington Bridge. The Open Spaces Committee has likewise been very busy,and a report, published elsewhere, shows that the pro- vision of " lungs " for the town is occupying serious attention. Schemes for the laying out on the most approved lines, of open spaces in Orford, Howley, and Latchford Wards are given in detail with the estimated cost. A bold step l!as been taken, too, by the Town Hall Committee, which recommends that the Council Chamber be enlarged, additional committee rooms and offices be pro- vided, together with a new public entrance to the Council Chamber. There are sufficient projects here to provide a full-dress debate, but they by no means fill the 48 pages which comprise this month's : agenda. Our municipal managers are in fur a busy time.

While there is so much mono), being spent on public improvements and so much more likely to be required it is a consolation to know that the assets of the Corporation are increasing. Three perch and a blackbird have been presented to the acquarium and the aviary respectively during the present month, and a special resolution has been passed by the Parks Committee acknowledging these valuable gifts. We have something sub- stantial to set against the Borough debt after all!

The unemployed problem is causing some trouble, and various attempts are being made to solve the difficulty. As a result of 'Alderman Pierpoint's efforts, a room has been set apart at the Gymnasium as a registration office for tile new Labour Bureau, to be open two days a week. This is a step the wisdom and value of which should soon be proved by results. The General Purposes Committee has appointed a sub-committee to consider and report upon the question of the unemployed. Councillor Stott has gone further and in a special resolution he urges the Corporation to proceed with some of the contemplated street improvements or any other work, in order to provide employment for the present out-of-work Warr; ngtonians.

• An interesting statement from Mr. Cecil F, Parr can always be relied upon at the annual meet- ing of Parr's Bank Limited—one of the soundest concerns in the country. With a declared dividend of 19 per cent and a bocus of one per ceHt behind him Mr. Parr has tittle reason to be pessimistic, and he does not, in the course of his speech on Thursday, review the commerce of the country in any gloomy light. He has, however, much te say that is good and profitable reading. The condition of the money market he clearly attributes to the heavy war ex- penditure and ever-irnpendinY new Government loans, and at the same time ho prudently advises retrenchment all round. "Governments," he says " both home and abroad, must be content to pay their way without embarking on fresh issues. . . . . Municipalities, also must defer their schemes for benefiting or impoverishing their ratepayers." These are weighty words from an authoritative source, and merit the deepest con- sideration. Mr. Parr made special reference to the disabilities under which thecotton trade is labouring, and' heartily commended the home- grown cotton idea. "As long as America pro- duces 80 per cent. of the cotton supply, so long would we be at the mercy of speculators,and the only course was to make ourselves independent of the American supply." This was his comment on the question which, perhaps, more vitally con- cerns the prosperity of Lancashire than any other. We are sure Mr.Parrs' pronouncement on this and other important topics will be welcomed in the commercial world. Finally it is our pleasure to congratulate Parr's Bank, Limited, on the; exc lent report and balance sheet adopted so una mously by the shareholders of the company.

One has but to glance at the comprehensive and carefully prepared report of our Chief Constable Upon crime and police work in the Borough to be assured of the responsible and onerous duties Which are so efficiently performed by the local police force. Year by year, as the Chief Constable points out, the police responsibilities are aug- mented by the passing of new statutes or local bye-laws which open up new fields of labour for the Chief Constable and his staff. Rece-A develop- ments may be taken as cases in point. The Motor R9gistration Act and the local byelaws under the Employment of Children Act have cast additional work upon the law's executive officers whose hand- sorne:headquart.-rs in Arpley-street have been so often the subject of remark.lf the police work con- tinues to increase in the same ratio as it has done recently there will be little room for criticism on the score of excessive accommodation. At present the staff is below the average strength, and the tramways undertaking has put additional pressure upon the limited resources of the staff without any corresponding relief in the way of additional... officers. The subject is one which already de mands the attention of the Watch Committee.

It is satisfactory to learn from the statistical returns that the timber of indictable offences committed in the Borough has decreased from 131 in 1902 to 123 in 1903, and that the apprehen— sions for non-indictable offences show an even more marked decline. Whereas there were 547 appre- hensions in 1902, last year there were only 463. The number of summonses issued also point in the same direction. There may be many contributory causes to this satisfactory decline in crin‘e. The South African war and the home- coming of soldiers and militiamen to Orford Barracks may have helped to swell the figures of 1902, but an even more potent factor seems to be the state of trade. The fact that a decline in trade synchronises with less crime in the Borough seems to point to the conclusion that a reduced wages list has the saving grace of keeping some people out of trouble. At, any rate this is the opinion of our energetic Chief Constable who makes the following observation :—" Variations in the state of trade would seem to influence the rise and fall of these statistics, at least as power- fully any other factor.". We should be glad to think that the fall in this year's statistics is a permanent improvement.

" Now what about glass ?" asked Mr. Chamber- lain at Liverpool. The glass bottle industry is one of the worst organised in the country, and itimay be admitted at once that foreign competition has severely affected it. Still the trade is far horn be- ing extinguished, and there are many indications that despite the ample supplies of cheap raw material enjoyed by the German and Belgian bottlemakers the British manufacturers could hold their own were the industry placed on a sound basis. A paragraph in the Times with reference to the St. Helens bottle industry indicates that the trade is on the eve of an improvement. Works which have not been employed since October are being started on full time, and within the next few days "all the workmen will be given full em- ployment."

Sir John Brunner and the Miners' Eight Hours Bill. Speaking on Monday night at Winnington, North- 'inch, Sir John Brunner, :11.P.. said he had been asked to ballot for the Miners' Eight Hours' Bill on the .opening of Parliament. He was doubtful whether Parliament was morally justified in interfering with the labour of grown men, and he certainly objected to begin with colliers, who only worked about 'five days weekly. There were thousands of people -working seven days weekly and twelve hours daily, and, if a bill was brought in to make that illegal he would agitate for it with all his power. The Miners'Bill was too inelastic and too arbitary for his support.

The Influence of Journalism. The Fairfield Literary and Debating Society held their usual fortnightly meeting on Monday evening at the Fairfield schools. In the absence of the Presi- dent (the Rev. A. S. Cameron) Mr. R. G. Brandreth was unanimously voted to the chair. The subject for discussion was" Journalism." In a very able speech Mr. F. Webster cited various reasons and arguments to show that journalism influenced the government of a country. The opposition was taken by Mr. L. Wood, who was of the opinion that though journalism might influence certain individuals, the , influence was not sufficient to affect the government of our country. An interesting discussion brought a pleasant meeting to a close.

Looking after Deserters. Before the • stipendiary, Mr. W. J. Stewart, at the Liverpool Police Court, .on Thursday Arthur Caines, twenty-one years of age, was charged with being an absentee from the 4th King's (Liver- pool Regiment), at Warrington, and also with obtain- ing food and lodgings by false pretences from Ann Jeffries, 40, Aubrey-street. It was stated that prisoner went to the address named and asked for lodgings. She gave him a room, and he stated that the military authorities in Warrington would pay her, he having been sent to look after deserter's. Becoming suspicious, she gave information at Rupert-lane, and prisoner was arrested.ln reply to the charges,he said, "I am an absentee, and I intended to pay.' He was remanded on the first charge, and bound over on the second.

Persistence and originality. Originality is not generally a marked feature of letters addressed to public bodies and the worthy district Coun.cillors of Runcorn could not forbear a smile on Tuesday when the clerk submitted the fol- lowing epistle from Mr. E. Gleave, estate agent &c, of Northwich, with respect to the sewage at Moore : ." Have you ever read in the gospel of a woman, who by her continual coming; so wearied the judge that .she got her request? Ihave been writing to you and your predecessor as to this matter for six years, and 'unless you want a further instalment of letters pray let the matter have attention. It really is a disgrace that sewage should be allowed to be such a nuisance in a civilized country in the twentieth century. I think I certainly can claim that lam a successor of Job for patience, but please do not provoke me to rebellion.—The Clerk .said a com- mittee bad held a number of meetings to discuss the matter, but they had generally ended by passing a resolution to let the matter rest for the present.—lt was decided to elect Mr. J. L. Jones a member of the Moore Sewage Committee in place of the late Mr. Peter Gleave and that a further meeting be held to discuss the subject, in order that " we.tax not the patience of Job the second"--as the chairman (Mr. S. Woodward) put it.

The last named artiste, in response to an encore, sang ' Queen of the Earth." Mr. T. F. Wallington dis- .charged the duties of accompanist. Recitations were .given by several of the scholars. A pianoforte solo ,was contributed by Ruby Witts. The Wycliffe Juvenile choir, under the leadership of Mr. W. Brown, sang two part songs in a creditable manner. A flute and clarionette duet by Messrs. W. Ford and Mr. Cooke, was well received. Mr. Harry Hill greatly -amused the audience,by one of his humorous sketches. Fifty-eight prizes were distributed by the pastor to the. -scholars who had been regular attenders during the past year. Wallington who has recently vacated the position of superintendent, and also a silvet case to '‘fr. W. Brown, his successor in that position, in recognition of his services as secretary of the Sunday *school. In making the presentation Mr. IV. Beswick alluded to the long and devoted services of the recipi- ents of the gifts to the school. The gifts were acknow- ledged in a suitable manner by Mr. Wallington and Mr. Brown. The proceedings were brought to a close by a dialogue by members of the upper classes.—On Tuesday evening the infants held their annual tea which passed off successfully.

ASSAULT BY A POLE.

At Widnes, on Thursday, Vincent Ajuckes, Well ing ton- street. was charged with assaulting Joe Alishowski by throwing a piece of slag at his face, severely cutting his lip. Alishowski stated that he went to see his friends and upon going home he saw Ajuckes on the other side of the road. He did not speak to him. Defendant, hurried on after him, and when close to him threw the stone which caused the wound. A fine of 205., 3s. doctor's fee, and 2s. 6d. for the interpreter was imposed or one month in default.

SAD DROWNING CASE AT WIDNES.-A GALLANT RESCUE. _ _

Mr. -Coroner Brighouse held an inquest on Monday at the Widnes Accident Hospital on the body of a boy named Frederick John Pervin' aged six years, who was drowned in the Mersey on Saturday afternoon. The facts as reported to the police were that several boys were playing football on the higher terrace of the Promenade, when the ball was kicked .over the fence into the river. Deceased and another boy, named Baker, were with clasped hands, stretching out to .reach it, when they fell in.— William Wright, 59, James-street, stated that he was working on the new transporter bridge on Saturday afternoon, when, at about half-past three, he heard screams. He saw Baker in the water, and, diving in, brought him to the side. He then tried to find Pervin, who had drifted away with the strong tide, but failed. His body was recovered an hour afterwards on a sand- bank by James Wilkinson.—The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."—The Coroner stated to Wright that Baker would have lost his life but for his , courageous conduct, and he would bring it under I the notice of the Royal Humane Society.— The Coroner : How many lives have you saved before— Wright : About three.—Have you ever been rewarded before ?—No.—Not even a" Thank you "?—No.—The Coroner: When you have lived as long as I have you j will find this world full of base ingulitude.—The jury also added a rider sympathising withlWe parents of de- ceased, and also endorsing the coroner's expression respecting Wright.

MURDEROUS ATTACK ON A WIFE.—ST. HELENS MAN

===== 0002601_19040130_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
AND HIS RAZORS.

The St. Helens magistrates on Wednesday, after hearing the evidence in a charge of threatening his wife brought against Josiah Isherwood, a middle-aged man, decided that a new summons for aggravated assault should he laid.—Mrs. Isherwood said they had been married about twenty-nine years, and her husband had broken the home up eight times. Five months ago she got a separation order, and on Saturday night her hus- band came to 100, Junction-lane, where she was lodging, and tried by threats to induce her to promise to live with him. A constable was sent for, and he went away. An hour later she went into the back yard, and her husband was there in the dark. He asked her if she was determined not to live with him, and she said she was. He then slid, "Tha has not to live long on this earth, then." He rushed at her, and took her by the hair of the head. She saw the blade of a knife in his hand, and after a struggle she fainted.—Mrs. Guest, next-door neighbour, said that when complainant screamed "Murder!" prisoner said, "11l make you scream,l will finish you and then myself, and we will travel our journey together."—Police- constable Littler said he arrested the man later. Ahen he was kicking at a door in Hunter-street. He had two razors in his possession. Prisoner said he was changing his lodgings, and that was the reason he had the razors in his pocket.—He pleaded in a whining tone that he was too drunk to know what he was doing.— The magistrates said the prisoner had no excuse. The police-books sh4wed a record of violence, and Isher- wood was sent to gaol for six month, the chairman re- marking that it was a mercy that he was not being charged with wilful murder.

THE TARIFF LEAGUE GOMMISSION.I

Sir Jo'n Branner's Criticism.

A communication issued by the Tariff League Com.: mission states that much progress has been made with the preliminary work. Meetings have been held of the special committees formed to consider the manner in which the steel and iron trades, the engineering and machinery trades, and the textile trades could be best dealt with. The general form of inquiry for manufac- turers is being sent out this week. It is expected that the examination of witnesses will begin on February 10, the iron and steel trade being taken first. Speaking on Thursday 'night at Weaverham, North- wich, Sir John Brunner,M.P., said that not one of Mr. Chamberlain's hundred Commissioners represented the consumer. All were manufacturers. He knew many of them, and, without being disrespectful, he might say that they were all on the make. They never wasted their time. (Laughter.) As a child he remembered the Bread riots, and he prayed God the English people would never again submit to such a system as then existed. German workers were fed on food which greatly reduced their efficiency—he had seen the ghastly procession of wprn-out horses enter that country for human consumption. None save manufacturers bene- fited by the legalised pocket-picking called Protection.

WARRINGTON CRICKET CLUB. Fixtures for Next Season.

It may seem a far cry to the cricket season but al- ready Mr. W. H. The Examiner, Saturday, January 30, 1904

The Tramways Managership. It having been decided on the termination of Mr. Grimsdale's appointment as electrical engineer, and tramways manager, to separate the two offices, Mr. F. V. L. Mathias has been appointed electrical engineer, and Mr. J. Worrell, of New Brighton, tram- ways manager. Mr. Mathias was formerly chief assistant to Mr. Grimsdale, and Mr: Worrell was, for some years, manager of the Warrington Carriage Company. • . Death of a Publican. Mr. James Barlow, the licensee of the Coach and Horses Hotel, Market-square, Chester, died suddenly on Saturday morning. Deceased had been in indiffer- ent health for some months, but as late as Friday he was able to undertake a walk of four miles, and went to bed in good spirits. On Saturday morning he was taken ill and passed away rather unexpectedly about 9.30. The immediate cause of death was hemorrhage of the brain. Deceased was a native of Walton, near Warrington, and came to Chester some 26 years ago to take over the Coach and Horses Hotel. He was well known in the City, although never prominently identified with public affairs. He was a member of the local Licensed Victuallers' Association, and was 62 years of age. St. James' Annual- Tea Party. Success attended the annual congregational tea party in connection with St. James' Church which was held in the schoolroom on Tuesday evening. About 350 sat down to a capital tea, ladies of the congreuation presiding at the tables. A concert arranged by Mr. Davies, the church organist followed. A vote of thanks was heartily accorded to the numerous helpers who had made the tea party so successful. The rest of the evening was passed in a pleasant manner: The infants' room formed a con- venient place for the display of admirable lantern slides, illustrative of life in Japan, manipulated by Mr. Houghton, whilst the large, schoolroom was well- filled with a number of young people who indulged in dancing with zest. Emmanuel Church Tea •Party. The annual congregational tea party in connection with Emmanuel (Reformed) Church, 80ld-street, was held in the schoolroom over Arpley Station on Thurs- day evening. About 150 sat down to tea, the follow- ing ladies assisting at the tables: Mesdames Norman, Matthews H. Gandy, E. Allen, Armstrong, F. Lloyd and Ditclikeld, Misses Allen and Lees. An enjoyable entertainment was afterwards held. The Rev. W. H. Taylor, of Eccles, presided and said the large attend- ance was very encouraging to the church officers. He spoke of the necessity forthe Reformed Church in Warrington and :extended a hearty invitation to anyone who did not attend a place of worship to come' to Emmanuel Church. An interesting and varied programme was submitted and all the artistes had a cordial reception. . Selections were rendered by Miss Turner's band, and the Star Quartette party (Messrs. Grounds, J. Lloyd, Unsworth, and Woollacott) acquitted themselves creditably. Songs were contri- buted by Miss Bridge, Miss Jenks, Mr. Greenough and Mr. Woollacotti and Mr. A. H. Lloyd was responsible for some capital Lancashire sketches. The part songs "Children's home." "Happy eyes," and "When twilight falls " were well interpreted by the choir. Mr. Norman of St. Helens, ably sustained the humorous part of le entertainment. A vote of thanks to the chairman, artistes, and lady helpers concluded a successful gathering. St. John's Literary and Social Society. The members of this society held their weekly , meeting in St. John's schoolroom on Monday evening last, when they had a joint debate with the Latchford Baptist Debating Society, a , strong c)ntingent of which was present, along with the president, the Rev. J. F. Matthews. The Rev. J: L. Milne, B. D., of St. John's occupied the chair and welcomed the Lateliford friends. The subject of debate was "Should this country adhere to Free Trade ?" Mr. Wilkinson of Latchford, opened for the affirmative with a highly interesting paper. He referred to the origin of Protection, and drew attention to the greatly improved position of the workers of the present -day compared with that of the period pre- vious to the adoption of Free Trade. He was seconded by Mr. D. R. Hodge, of St. John's who said the real test of a country's prosperity was the condition of its eon:mon people. In this connection he argued that in Britain the masses were much better off than was the ease in any of the great preteeted countries, and quoted interesting statistics in support of his 'con- tention. The leader' on the negative side prop3sed practically an adoption of the suggestions of Mr. Chamberlain,—Free Trade within the British Empire and protection against "dumping," and the free admission of goods which are the product of °hes, p foreign labour. There was a large attendance of the members of both societies, but littl i time remained for discussion after the leaders had explained themselves. The meeting was, however, of a strongly Free Trade tone, and on a vote being taken 31 supported the affirmative while only 3 voted for the negative. Poor Children's Treat. Though several weeks later than usual, this year's treat to the poor children of the toma is none Vas) lass acceptable. The prevalence of infections disease made it necessary to postpone the event from the usual date, and a new departure is the holding of separate entertainments for boys and girls. 'Last evening about 650 girls enjoyed themselves to their hearts' content at the Drill Hall, and to-day the boys will have their innings. The arrangements, as usual, have been in the hands of a strong committee, and special praise is due to Mr. Henry Taylor, P.C.M., and Mr. W. H. • J.. Jenkins who have discharged the duties of secretary and treasurer, res- pectively. The tickets had been distributed to the deserving children through the head teachers at the day schools. There was a happy scene at the brightly decorated Drill Hall when the girls sat down to tea. Of course, they did full justice to the abandance of meat pies, Eccles cakes, buns -and tea placed before them. Their wants. were assiduously attended to by the following ladies.: The 'Mayoress (Mrs. Bolton), Mesdames Richardson, Bracecamp, James Fairclough,Forshaw, Taylor, Monro, Ferguson,Ewart, Clegg and Parkinson, the Misses Bolton, Margaret and Bessie Pierpoint, Boweock, Amy Pierpoint, Ewart s.nd Clegg. Others who rendered assistance, besides the officials,included Inspector Hackett, of the N.S.P.C.C., and Mr. James Skelland. After tea there was an enter- tainment which delighted the youngsters. The comic lantern slides exhibited by Mr. Taylor caused great merriment, and other items which helped to pleas- antly pass the time were ventriloquial entertainments by Mr. A. Grounds, and ccnjuring by Mr. Foster, of Earlestown.' As each child left the hall she received a packet of sweets and an orange. Hearty thanks were accorded to all who had contributed to the success of the gathering. Burns' Celebration at Warrington. . Monday was the 'anniversary of the birth of Scotland's national poet, Robert Burns, and on Wednesday evening last the members of the Warrington Caledonian Association held their annual "Burns' Night" at the Lien Hotel. The function took the form of a hot-pot supper, which was purveyed by Mr. Lee in a way which left nothing to be desired. The President of 'the Association, Mr. Jamesrloaro, Latchford, occupied the chair, and their were also present : Dr. Ferguson, Vice-president; Mr. Alder- man Francomb, Messrs. J. Fulton Smith, T. Mav, J. Watson, E. Bindloss, T. S.- Steel, and others. After supper the Chairman gave the usual loyal toasts which were duly honoured. He next proposed shortly "The imniortal memory of Robert Burns." He (the chairman) said, they grieved for Burns' shortcomings —that he had them they all knew— they sympathised with his' misfortunes, but they were thankful for his wonderful talents and superlative poetic genius. His fame had travelled to the ends of the earth. Let them that night feel proud of their great countryman; whose name during the week was being honoured in every country under the sun where civilization, patriotic sentiment, admiration of talent, or loyalty was obe found. The toast wee drunk in solemn silence. Mr. J. Hunter opened the evening's musical programme with the song "There was a lad was born in Kyle," Mr. J. L. Davenport followed with "Jessie the Flower of Dunblane," while Mr. Fieldhouse gave "The Young Rovalist."—The Chairmaif proposed the toast of "Old friends in Scotland.—.llr. Thomas May, in an interesting speech, introduced the toast of "The land 0' cakes.' He said Burns drew his inspiration from many sources, -as did all great powers. He (Burns) made frequent and fond allusions to the homely Scottish fare, the oat cakes, barley meal, bannocks, etc. Mr. J. Fulton Smith proposed "The land we live in," which was responded to by Mr. E. Bindloss. During the evening Mr. Hallard treated the company to two highly amusing humorous items. Mr. Ireland sang " Mary" and received a well-deserved encore, Mr. J. Grounds acted as accompanist, and the pro- gramme of songs, etc., was, as usual, in the hands of Mr. J. L. Davenport, who, along with the other gentle- men named, delighted the assembled Scots and their friends with some fine old favourites during the even ing. At eleven o'clock the proceedings terminated with the singing of "Auld Lang Syne."

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

Trade policy yields the highest wages for the least work I fancy our working men will stick to it. These returns also prove that, low as wages were in France or Germany, or Belgium some years ago, they have not increased so fast as the higher wages of British work- men have risen during the same period. And they prove also that the British workman can get more for his money than his European competitors. Need I sample any more " facts " from Joseph's basket? There is small choice in rotten apples, and every one of his facts will crumble to pieces on exam- ination. But why does he keep on telling such awful crammer s day after day? And how did he acquire his marvellous talent for misrepresBnting plain and palpable facts? He reminds me of a good railway story. A couple of navvies, who had beert boozing pretty freely, got into the carriage and began to use language that was pretty strong and highly seasoned. A quiet-looking parson in the corner began to fidget and get uneasy, and the other passengers looked down their noses and blushed now and then. But these navvies took no notice of the gathering storm. They were deeply interested in their own affairs, and they used some magnificent swear words that are not to be found in any dictionary. At last one of them let fall a peculiarly sulphurous oath that moved the poor parson to say, My good man, will you kindly tell us wherever you learned such language as that ?" With a look of mingled pity and contempt the navvy said, "Lam it, mesthur? Lam it? Yo canno lam that. It's a gift." Joseph has had a wonderful gift for deceiving other people ever since he nicknamed himself a statesman. Now he is doing his level best to deceive himself. And I am inclined to think he is succeeding very well.

SOCIAL SERMONETTES

• FOR WORKING MEN,

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of "Search Lights on Cant Labour, and Social Problems."

CHANI3ERLAIN'S FISCAL FACTS.

"Deceit is the false road to happiness And all the joys we travel to through vice, Like fairy banquets, vanish when we touch them."

For the past six months or more Mr. Chamberlain has been stumping the country, like a quack doctor at a fair, trying to persuade us that we have got all the ailments and diseases in the world, and we shall soon be dead and done if we do not swallow his infallible pills. Now. whenever any peripatetic quack manifests a, good deal of interest in my health, and tries to per- suade me that it is about time I ordered my coffin, and insists on dosing me with his physic, I begin to inquire about his motives. And I usually find that his chief concern is not to prolong my life, or promote my wel- fare, but to relieve me of some of my spare cash and transfer it to his own pocket. And take the trouble to examine his statements, analyse his nostrums, I am bound to admit that as a rule he is an accomplished liar and a gay deceiver. After my experience of quackery and deception, you will not expect me to buy a pig in a poke, or take any man's random statements as eternal verities, without investigating them for myself. So if you please we will dip our hands into Joseph's fiscal basket and examine his wares. He comes from Birmingham, and all is not gold that glitters there. I.—THE FIRST " FACE " I PICIC UP CONCERNS

OUR EXPORTS.

The burden of Joseph's complaint is that we are not j sending stuff enough out of the country. He does notl measure the nation's wealth by its ability to feed, and clothe, anti educate its own people, but by its power to feed, and clothe, and help the foreigner. Thig is a queer kind of patriotism any-way. I have falways been under the impression that the more a man has coming in, and the better off he is ; and that saving is better than soending. But our modern fiscal reformers tell me that I must drop these antiqua- ted and foolish notions. I must measure a man's wealth by what he spends and not by whathe earns. The more he parts with, and better off he must be. The less he has coming in, and the richer and happier will he be. Now, if you measure John Bull's prosperity by this idiotic and fallacious test, you must admit he is in a bad way, for his imports are still greater than his ex- ports, and he is growing rich, in spite of all the efforts of the Patriotic Party to impoverish him. But the modern Jeremiah asks us to believe that our trade with roreign countries is not increasing, and that other nations are not buying from us as mnch as they used to buy. He tells us that during the last twenty , or thirty years "our general. our export trade has re- mained practically stagnant." Now, this statement is ' either true or false, and there should be no difficulty in settling it. The Board of Trade returns show that in. 1870 our exports were valued at £199,587,000 and in 1900 they were £282,004,000. A very simple stun in arithinstic will show that during those thirty years our exports had increased more than £83,000,000, or more -than forty per cent. If Joseph calls this" stagnation " he has either played ducks and drakes with his arithmetic, or he does not know the meaning of the words he uses, or he is deliberately try- , ing to deceive us.

IL—THE SECOND "FACT" HAS TO DO WITH THE EXPORT OF BRITISH MANUFACTURES. Now, lam not patriotic enough to rejoice when all the best things we manufacture are shipped off to foreign lands. I would rather see them all absorbed, and used by my own country. men. If our working men would spend less money in boozing and backing horses, and more in feeding and clothing their wives and children, we should be able to use and wear,and enjoy the products of our own looms and factories, and we should have less to export to foreign countries. The weeping prophet from Birmingham believes that it is a good thing for, this country that we are weaving blankets and rugs for naked niggers in Africa,and print- ing calico for John Chinaman, and he tells us that we are doing less foreign trade now than we were doing a few years ago. He says that "during the last thirty years our exports of British manufactured goods have actually declined." Now, let me pin hint down to this plain and definite statement, and then you can judge the value of his "facts." Theißoard of Trade returns prove that in 1870 we exported manufactured goods from this country valued at £182,400.000, and in 1900 these exports had risen to £225,200,00). A simple sum insubtracbion will prove that during these thirty 'years our exports had enormously increased, and in 1900 we sold goods to for- eign nations worth £42,800,000 more than we sold them thirty years before. But Joseph asks us to believe that this is not a rise at all, and you can prove anything with figures. If we would save ourselves from committing rank blasphemy, we are to believe that" our exports of manufactures have actually declined" because he says so. I wish somebody would make him a present of a diationary that Ire may get to know what such words as " stagnant " and " declined " really mean. And while they are about it they might send him to some elementary school, where he could be taught the difference between simple addition and simple subtrac- tion. He has been getting nearly one hundred pounds a week out of a grateful country for the last seven or eight years, and after all these advantages he should not air his ignorance after this fashion.

lII.—THE THIRD " FACT " DELLS wura oua EXPORTS TO PROTECTED COUNTRIES.

Joseph has got the silly notion into his head that you can do any mortal thing with money and tariffs ; and that is just where he makes his mistake. He says that other nations can build tariffs all round you, till you can do no trade with them at all. But it is not true. Tariffs are not bludgeons to kno3k your enemies down. They are boomerangs that recoil on the fool that uses them, and they do more harm to him than to anybody else.

Now let me ask you to examine the following state- ment very carefully, because it is a good example of the methods that are being employed to swindle and dupe the men who never think for themselves. Mr. Chamberlain says that "countries which have Protection have taken from us very much less during the last thirty years than they took before," and he asks us to believe that they will continue to increase their Pro- tective tariffs till they have destroyed our trade with them altogether. He Says that "Russia, Germany, France, the United States, and other protected coun- tries have their own markets plus ours, and we cannot , touch any one of their markets."

Now, Ihis is a very artful statement to make, and it is a very awkward argument to handle, because it is founded on a double-barrelled falsehood, and then a false argum9nt is built on this rotten foundation. It is not true that or exports to protected countries are less now than they were thirty years ago. The very opposite of this is true, for other couptries have taken from us more thau they used to take. The lbard of Trade returns prove that Protectionist countries bought .C 94,521,000 worth of our goods in the year 1870. In 1900, which is thirty years later, they bought £115,147,000 worth of our wares. How could they "have taken from us very much less than they took before," when they paid us £20,616,000 more for the goods? And it is not true that "we cannot touch any one of . .

their markets" in protected countries. We 'do touch them, and we sell many million pounds worth of our goods to them in spite of their tariffs. In the year 1902 I we exported manufactured goods to the United States worth £20,800,009. In the same year we sold to Germany manufactured articles worth £17,000,000. France bought £10,500,000 worth, Russia bought £6,500,000 worth, and Belgium bought £4,700,000 worth. If you add these figures together you will find that five Protectionist countries. in spite of their high tariffs, bought £62,200,000 worth of our manufactures. And yet Joseph has the "cheek" to say that "we cannot touch any one of their markets." Now let us for a mament suppose that both these lies had been truths. Suppose the foreigner had bought less of us than he used to do. That would only have proved that he had less to sell to us, for the unlucky fore igner can take no more from us than he has to offer us in exchange. If John Chinaman's tea, and silk, and rice crops were seriously reduced, he would have to take so much less of our calico, and hardware, and cutlery. When a nation becomes so poor that it has nothing to export, it will very soon have nothing. to import, because nobody will give something far nothing. No tariff wall will ever prevent one nation from taking imports in exchange for its own exports, until the tariff has so impoverished the pe•ple that they are too pool to export anythinc at all. As long as human nature is what it is, men will willingly part with what they do not need, in exchange for something that they do need. If every nation in the world, except England, had a high tariff to exclude her goods, England,as a Free Trader, would go on selling to them, so long as they remained rich enough to have anything ta sell to her, IV.—"THE FOURTH FACT" HAS 10 DO WITH THE CONDITION OF THE WORKING MAN IN PROTECTIONIST COUNTRIES.

Joseph says that " workmen in Protectionist countries Are improving their circumstances faster than workmen in England." Now, in order to answer this very cir- cumstantial lie I should have to quote a mass of interesting figures from the blue books and the Board of Trade returns. I have neither time nor space to quote them in Viis article, butt may perhaps deal with them another day.

These returns clearly prove that wages in Great Britain are the highest in Europe, and that the hours of labour are the shortest, and so long as our Free

The Warburton Bridge Tolls.

The CLERK reported that as instructed by the Council he wrote to the Town Clerk complaining that small-pox patients were conveyed along Back Lane, Sankey, to the Sanatorium instead of along the main road. He had received a reply stating that the Heald} Committee lad given instructions for the patients to be conveyed along Liverpool-road.

The CLERK to the Poulton Parish Council wrote re- questing the Council to erect a gas lamp at the Ches- hire Lines:Committee's goods entrqnce in Green-lane.At present the road was so badly righted that there had been narrow escapes of accidents. The Council was also asked if it could see its way to dispense with the placing of large stones on the main roads as the Parish Council considered them dangerous7and a serious im- pediment to traffic. The use of a steam roller in form- ing the roads had obviated the _necessity for the large stones.

The Council considered the large stones were indis- pensable and declined to interfere with the present system. The Roads Committee was requested to con- sider the advisability of providing a new lamp.

As the Council contemplate revising their byelaws at an early date the Clerk was instructed at a recent meet- ing to obtain copies of the latest model byelaws drawn up by the Local Government Board. Mr. Sutton now reported that he had received the model byelaws and if the Council decided to make alterations the necessary forms would be forwarded in order that the suggestions could be submitted to the Board for their approval. He (Mr. Sutton) suggested that the officials should re- port upon the matter. The Council would either be re-elected or f,ro out of office in a few weeks and he thought no serious movement should be made in the direction of new byelaws until after the elections.

Mr. KNOWLES thought it was desirable that the Council should have byelaws dealing with the licensing of dancing rooms, &c. Dr. SEPHTON said it would be necessary for the Council to obtain urban powers before they could deal with that matter. Mr. KNowr.us said the Council would probably be able to adopt a section giving them power to deal with fffensive trades. They could then deal with the Long- ord nuisance.

The officials were instructed to report as suggested by the Clerk.

HF.A.LT/I OF TFIE DISTRICT. LONGFORD DEPOT NUISANCE.

Dr. SEPFITONj reported that during the month there had been 22 births and 21 deaths. The death rate was 227 per 1,000 against 36, the average for the past three months, and 30 the rate for the corresponding month last year. Thirteen deaths occurred in Winwick Asylum and there had been a death from smallpox at Sankey sanatorium. Exclusive of these the death rate for the district proper was 7.5. Since the last meeting ihe visited Longford dept along with the inspector and found extensive alterations were being made. They would take several weeks to complete but when finished would abate the nuisance so *long complained of. At present the stench was as great as ever.

Mr. Mather moved the adoption of the report and said he thought great credit was due to the Meigical Officer and Inspector for the healthy state of the dis- trict.

Mr. KIiOWLES: I suppose these experiments are going on. . . _

- The INSPECTOR: Not only experiments, bat the improvements. _ . _

kr. KNOWLES : How long will they take lire they are finished? Dr. SEPRTON: I think they will take thre.e months.

The report was adopted. • The INSPECTOR reported that the alteratioas at the Longford Depot were being proceodedlwith.A condensing tower had been built, but the tank had not been put up. Tenders had bean advertised for the construction of the reservoir. It was proposed to dry the treated refuse before discharging it. This would considerably im- prove matters. On the 9th inst he received a letter from !qr.-Knowles complaining of the font smells at Long- ford. He visited the place the same day and found that the nuisance arose in consequence of the discharginr of the refuse on the floorafter it had been boiled. This process the Corporation intended doing away with. Mr. KNOWLLS said that at the time he wrote the letter the stench made him unwell' for two or three days,: It was so strong that he could " taste it on his • lips "until he had had a wash. The INSPECTOR: The stench is very bad, but I think the alterations are_proceeding very satisfactorily. Mr. MA.THER : I can taste it, even so far away as my house. _ .

Nir. HOUGHTON: Taking the two examples here, it musthave a very nourishing efTe3t. (Laughter). The report was adopted. WARBURTON BRIDGE.

The SURVEYOR said he had received letters from the Rev. E. P. Wnethered, Hollinfare, complaining of the Ship Canal Company having,addel new charges to the list of tolls.. Conseinently he visited the bridge and found the new charges to be as follows :—Bicycle id; Motor Cycle to carry one 6d ; Motor Cycle to carry two is. ; Motor Car' 2s. 61 Under the Act the com- pany had the power to levy these tolls. Mr. . BROOKS suggested that the Lancashire County Council should be approached with a view to the Lan- cashire and Cheshire County Councils joining hands and taking over the Warburton-road. The nearest bridges connecting Lancashire and Cheshire were Warrington bridge and Barton bridge and he thought that, being on the fringe of one of the most thickly populated districts in the world, it was something of a scandal that two portions of the community should be divided for such long dis- tances, and that it should be left to private enterprise to provide a bridge. in this case private enterprise did not find proper means, as the heavier traffic could not cross the bridge for which tolls were charged If the County Councils took over the road there would be no necessity for the bridge to remain, as the stream was silting up, and he suggested that it could be filled up entirely. • Mr. Hotmirrox thought tile Canal Company would be glad to get rid of the responsibility attaching to the bridge, as the tolls received were not sufficient to pay for the maintenance of the road.

The SURVEYORWaS instructed to make inquiries from the County Surveyor as to the best course for the Coun- cil to adopt.

COUNTY COUNCIL GRANTS:

The SURVEYOR intimated that the County Main Roads and Bridges Committee would shortly be con- sidering the maintenance of main roads,and applications for special grants for improvements on both main and secondary roads. If the members would let him know improvements desired he would prepare and submit plansand estimates and include such applications to the County.

Over 100 persons assembled at a social party at the Plicenit Bowling Club's Pavilion, Heywood, on Wed- nesday evening to celebrate the 82na birthday of Mr. William Healey, the veteran Lancashire bowler, who for upwards of 30 years has been president of the bowling club at Heywood. Mr. Healey, who is the father of County Alderman John Healey, Councillor William Healey, J.P. (ex-Mayor of Heywood), and Councillor David Healey, is still hale and hearty, and was present at last night's gathering in the company of his wife. Mr. and Mrs. William Healey, senior, are reputed to be the oldest married couple in Hey- wood—both being octogenarians.

THE FREE CHURCHES OF WARRINGTON.

Interesting Gathering.

PROPOSED FREE CHURCH HALL AT WIN WICK.

Under the auspices of the Warrington Council of the Evangelical Free Churches, a pleasant social gathering was held in Bold-street schoolroom on Thursday even- ing. There was a representative attendance, amongst those present being: Mr. and Mrs. C. E. Parker, Mrs. Garnett, Rev. John Yonge, Rev. G. H. Carnson and Mrs. Carnson, Rev. R. Wilkins Rees and Mrs. Rees, Rev. J. S. Hughes, and Mrs. Hughes, Rev. John Gunnell, Rev. J. F. Matthews, Rev. Harry Wall, Councillor James Evans, Mr. and Mrs. NV. Winstanley, Mr. and Mrs. Harris, Mr. and Mrs. J. Griffiths, Mr. and Mrs. T. Prince, Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Dunn, Mr. and Mrs. W. J. Dunn, Mr. and Mrs. J. Bailey. Mrs. George Weaver Mrs. J. Fairhurst,Mrs. W. F. Wilkinson,Messrs. A. Perkins, S. B. Stringer W. Hickman, NV. Clegg, W. Beswick, G. Bailey, F. Stringer, J. Wadsworth, E. Eng- land, Coop, Barlow and Edwards. The hall had been decorated for the occasion with curtains, flags &c., and presented an attractive appearance. The PRESIDENT Mir. Parker) extended a hearty wel- come to all present. He observed that he heard some- one speaking of the different churches the other day and the remark was made that the Roman atholic Church, looked down upon the Church of Englano., and the Church of England lookedjdown upon the tVesleyans, and the Wesleyans looked down upon the Primitives, and the Primitives looked down upon the Salvation Army, and the Salvation Army looked down upon the whole lot of them. (Laughter.) They wanted to change that feeling and to realise their unity—(hear, hear)— and that social gathering was to aid in its accomplish- ments.

A short mu.ical programme arranged by Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. l3ae., was submitted and addresses were delivered. During an interval for social converse re- freshments were handed round. The following ladies assisted: Mesdames Carnson, J. S.. Hughes, A. H. Dunn, Fairhurst, G. Weaver, W. J. Dunn, Wilkinson, Miles, Lawton and J. Adams, Misses Carnson, Evans, Burrows, Weaver, Knowles, Porritt, Dann, Corney and England. The SECRETARY (Rev. J. F. Matthews) referred to the excellent arrangements made by the ladies, and pro- ceededto speak of the work of the Free Church Council. He pleaded for greater interest to be taken by the Churches connected with it in all that concerned its welfare. If the Council had a largerand more generous support from the churches concerned,it would be able to undertake far higher service than it had done, and he was prepared to prophesy that in the future the Free Church Council would be more greatly honoured than in days gone by. (Hear, hear.) The PRESIDENT said he wished he could in the few moments he could claim the attention of the Free churchmen of Warrington carry them in thought back to the genesis of the Free Churches. Hel thought they lost a great deal by not going back often enough to the great events which had to do with the formation of the great battles and the great principles which were fought for by their fathers. (Hear hear). Because of this he fancied many of them were inclined somewhat to look upon the Free Churches as a sort of second rate institution that had done their work and did not differ greatly from other religious institutions. He did not know that there was any better antidote to this *feeling than to go back to the times [that] were gone by and read and learn and enter into the spirit and the conditions which gave the Free Churches birth. He would have liked if there had been time, to tell them of the men who separated themselves from the Church of. their fathers for the sake of a purer worship and a suppler faith, and in do- ing so had to endure privation, persecution, the Joss of property, health and even life itself. Free Churchmen had need to be reminded of the period in English history when it was a crime to have a Bible, treason to meet together to pray to God and to sing a vesper hymn. They had need to keep fresh their recollection of the times when Pym and Hampden bearded tyranny and when Russell and Sydney dreamed of freedom. (Hear, hear.) It was well that they should ever and again enter into fellowship with the makers of the Free Churches. Such men as Henry Barrowe, John Greenwood, and John Perry— men who stood in the forefront of the battle for freedom 'against politicaliand ecclesiastical tyranny, and who in the end sealed their testimony with their blood and conquered death by dying. (Hear, hear.) He should have liked too, if he had had opportunity and the necessary gifts to carry them in thought into the heart and spirit of the great conflict for religious liberty in which the Free Churches were born .and cradled. He would that he had the power to stir their pulses and quicken their zeal by setting before them the conflicts that their fathers waged and the victories that their 1 fathers won. (Applause.) He should like to introduce them to the early Free Churchmen— those men of massive thought, burning word tktid herQic I action; men whose exit from the world was frequently by means of the scaffold or by the ascending flame at the stake. He would like to speak of Milton ancief Bunyan who by their writings gave to puritanism its noblest literary expression. He read the other day a charming description of Bunyan in prison from the pen of Morley Puushon, which he ventured to read because it was so much in harmony with his present purpose. Mr. Parker proceeded to read the extract referred to and said the mental power of such 1 men as Punshon helped them to realise the great heritage they had in the Free Church history. Reference was made to the sufferings of one John

Haydn who was imprisoned for holdirig..a conventicle —sufferinrys endured for the sake of truth and the prin- ciples wach Free Churchmen enjoyed. It would be a pleasing theme if he sketched out the different parts that the several churches took in those great struggles; of the Part played by the Independents who were perhaps the first in the field, of the Baptists, Presbyterians, Quakers anA Unitarians. (Hear, hear.) As a Wesleyan he felt a little disappointment that he could not include them in the list, for he was sure some of them felt as though they would like to have had a hand with Cromwell 's Ironsidas at Marston Moor. (Hear, hear.) They had to remember that when the invigorating blast of persecution had passed away that even the Free Churches as time went on ,passed into a state of coldness and lethargy when wickedness was rampant in the land. Then it was that the Methodists played their part, when little John Wesley heralded the second reformation, helped as he was by the silver-tonged George Whitfield and eloquent Charles Wesley. (Applause). Ile did not wish to point back to the old times and the old struggles merely to make them proud of their beginnings but rather with the idea that the same spirit which worked upon their fathers might lauence them and help them to play the man. He wanted them to remember that the hill-tops of their history were bathed in a glory that could never pass away, and he wished the Free Churchmen ,of War rington to play the man for their Godand theirconntry Rev. G. H. CARNsos pointed out that his name had been in the Christian ministry in the Independent line for 150 year ba7,k, without a break. (Hear. hoar.) He would be vary sorry to live on the recollection of it, but still there was a little insniration in it. As Free Churchmen, he said, they would have to stand by the, Bible as their forefathers did. Some people seemed tci think it was done for, and said it was criticised away. It was not, so far as he was con-!erned—(hear, hear)— and he hoped .Free Churchmen were going to stand by it. (Applause.) Then they must also be determined to maintain the sniritual life of the churches. He was delighted to find the Free Churches coming closer to- gether. He could remember the day when the Wesleyans would not allow a Baptist to occupy a Methodist pulpit, but the old lines of demarkttion were gradually dying away. There was no reason why the Free Churches should bee those definite characteristics for which they stood, whilst drawing closer together for mutual help and sympathy. He would be glad to see the ladies

_ taking a more direct interes-t in the work of the Free Church Council. He believed they ought to take a firmer stand on the Temperance question. That day Mr. Yonge and himself had been to see Tan Maclaren at

Liverpool. When he heard they came from Warrington Tan 'Maclaren said "WaTrington is full of beer is it not?" (Laughter.) The interests of "the trade" in the town were so wide-spread that the minister was a very bold man indeed who dared to speak out. The Free Churches ought to band themselves together on this question and stand firmly side by side and let the individual be sunk. (Hear,- hear.) There were enough men in the Free Churches to remove this stigma cast on Warrington if they were determined that it should be done. (Hear, hear.) They wanted a good mouthpiece on this ones- tion and at present they were rather short of it. Then there was the Education question, which was a burning one to all members of the Free Churches. Some people complained that Free Churchmen did not. want the Bible in the schools. Tt was the catechism they wanted ruling ent of the schools. (Hear, hear.) They did not want the Bible—the highest classic in the land—to be ruled out. They wanted it in,but the clerics said if theßible is -to be there, there must be a man to expound it. The B ible was able to do its own work if they would only let it have a fair chance. (Hear, hear.) Rev. JOHN YONG E said he was exceerlingly pleased to see so numerousa company present. He was beginn- ingto think that the day so far as he was personally con- cerned had been one of the most successful days of the year. As Mr. Carnson had,hinted, they had been to see an Maclaren with the object of getting him to corn e to Warrington and he was very pleased to say they had succeeded. (Hear, hear and applause.) Dr. Watson had declined the invitation twice before, but as the result of an interview that morning he had consented to come to Warrington on the 21st of March next on the occa- sion of the Centenary meeting of the British and Foreign Bible Society. (Hear, hear.) The Bishop of Liverpool and Dr. John Watson (Tan Maclaren) would speak. He hoped evangelical Christians of all demoninations would attend the meeting and make it agreat suocess. The question had often been asked "What is the Free Church Council doing ?" He would not trouble them with a list of its achievements, but a large amount of quiet work had been accomplished which had told upon the religious life of the town (Hear hear). The council

wouldido a great deal more if they could only induce al/ Free Churchmen to take a personal interest in its work. He wished to refer to al scheme which was on foot at Winwick. They were doubtless aware that the Lancashire Asylums' Board had erected an ' asylum at Winwick for the accommodation of 2000 inmates, and for the proper manage- ment of that vast number of unfortunate and un- happy individuals there was a stait of officers and servants of something like 500 persons, male and female. A considerable number of officers and servants- were Nonconformists, and any who desired Free Church privileges had to go either to Warringtnn or Newton- le-Willows, a distance of between two and three miles. The Rev. C. H. Hocken, who Was formerly the Secretary of the Council—and very ably he discharged the duties —received a letter from a gentleman in Bristol, point- ing out that there was no Nonconformist place of wor- ship or school in the whole parish of Winwick,and suggest- ingthat if the Free Church Council would take the matter in hand and erect a Free Church hall,undenominational in character, and under the control of the Free Church Council, he would give a handsome donation. A con- ference had been held at Winwick, and a movement in the direction indicated had been set on foot. The gentleman who made the offer was Mr. Oatley, the architect for the new asylum, and he had been generous enough to give them £lOO towards the cost. The Council had been in negotiation with the Warrington Corporation and had secured a site of ground leased from the Waterworks Committee of the Corporation. The rental was £3 10s. and they were now paying rent. A design had been furnished by the architect named I 6m and it was proposed to erect a building which would accommodate some 300 persons at an estimated cost of £6OO. Already £2OO had been promised to- wards the cost and it was necessary that steps should be taken to raise the remainder so that building operations could be commenced and the hall opened for public worship before the autumn of the coming year. In addition to Mr. Oatley's donation, subscriptions had been promised by Mr. Parker £25, Mr. A. H. Crosfield £25, Mr. Armitage £25, Mr. John Griffiths £5, Mrs. Evans, Haydock £5, and some smaller donations. He was very anxious that every church represented at that meeting should make a collection on behalf of the scheme. If the movement were taken up with unanimity by the Free Churches he believed it would be carried throttgh to a successful issue. (Hear, bear.) The CHAJRMAN said the scheme was laid upon them as Free Churchmen as a solemn duty, and it would be a great testing point as to the worth of the unity that they had been speaking about. It wasnot a great thing to accomplish if the burden was evenly distributed. During the evening, vocal items were contributed by Miss May Bouchier, Mr. A. Cotterill and Mr. N. Cook. The ins6rumental items consisted of violin solos by Miss B. Bouchier, and pianoforte solos by Master Pierce, who also accompanied.

PARR'S BANK, LIMITED.

Annual Meeting —Mr. Cecil F. Parr on the Year's Commun.

The thirty-ninth annual meeting of this bank was held in London on Thursday, Mr. Cecil F. Parr, pre- siding. The report recommended a dividend for the past half-year at the rate of 19 per cent. per annum,and a bonus of 1 per cent. The Chairman, in moving the adoption of the report, said that he thought few of them would have been sorry to turn their backs on the year 1903. The weather • alone was enough to depress all of them, and unfortunately, it depressed their greatest national industvy—agrieulture—and caused, he was afraid, very severe loss. Other employments and in- dustries which more or less depended on sunshine had also suffered. He was glad to say, however, that this depression had not extended to the company's profits, for these Were much the same as usual. Where they had suffered, in common with every other institution of the kind, had been from a cause which no prudence on the directors' part could avert. They had had to provide a large sum for the depreciation on their Consols by writing them down to 85. It had been asked on more than one occasion why so much should be carried forward from time to time. He thought that that policy had at last been amply justified now, for out of the sum of £107,000 which they had carried forward they had been able to meet this great extrane- ous call on their funds, and yet carry forward this year the substantial sum of £65,000. Many people were still apparently exercised by the low price of Consols. The explanation was really a simple one. Ccnsols were affected by the laws of supply and demand precisely like any other commodity, and the price of Consols would go up when the demand overtook;the supply,and not before. The demand was not ,likely, he feared, to improve in the near future. ,‘ The rate of interest was now only 2J per cent. instead of 21 per cent. Then, again, owing tv the war, the amount of Consols relatively was larger; and in addition to the Consols there were several Government securi- ties, such as the National War Loan and Ex- chewer Bonds, width, while equally safe, gave a bettet yield to the investor, and naturally were more sought after. These latter securities, like the fresh issues of Consols, were largely taken by the professional opera- tors. Some of these were still waiting an opportunity to unload their stocks on the genuine investor,and the genuine investor had not yet appeared to any great ex- tent. was impossible for the market to regain its elasticity so long as it was weighed down by the ever- impending new loans. The qnly remedy WM retrench,. ment all round. Governments, both home and colonial" must be content for some time to come to pay their way without embarking on fresh issues, except those to which they were actually committed. Municipalities, also must defer their schemes _

for benefitting-or impoverishing their -ra.e.paye-issTa; they knew, the bank was largely interested in cotton, and the violent fluctuations in the price of raw cotton, owing partly to the conflicting calculations as to the amount of the crop, but far more to the market manipu- lations by speculators on this side, he was sorry to say, as well as in America, caused general disorganisation and severe loss. In wages alone it had been computed that over £2,000,000 had already been lost, and short time, meaning more loss, had now generally been adop- ted. As long as America produced 80 per cent, of the cotton supply, so long would they be at the mercy of speculators, .and the only course was to make themselves independent of the American supply. He was glad to say thatlthe efforts of the British Cotton Growing Association and others had resulted in successful experiments being carried out in various of our colonies and other parts of the world. Of course, the production of sufficient cotton to make much impression on the world's supply must take some years, but he was glad to see that a British cotton grow- ing company had already been formed. It was, how- ever, necessary that private efforts should be supple- mented by Government assistance in the shape of railways for transport in the colonies, and he hoped prompt recognition of this would be shown by the Colonial Office. After alluding to the necessity for the reduction of the rate of interest in the Post Office Savings Bank, the chairman went on to refer to the inadequacy of bank reserves. One suggestion for cur- ing this was the publication of balances in the hands of the bank. They would then know how far the joint stock banks relied on themselves and on the re- serves of the Bank of England and conversely to what -extent the Bank of Englq,nd relied on the joint stock banks. Banks however, ,Itere not the worst offenders. It *astolbe deplored that the Government did not set a better example. The Government were the largest bankers in thit country, having nearly £150,000,000 in their 'hands fre_tifpNle,positors to the Post Office Savings Bank, and veWllll.eltkept not a single ounce of gold against it. /n Alclusion, the chairman said,with regard to the fiscal controv4sy, that they all should do their best to examine the question for themselves, and also to prevent it from being overwhelmed in the mire of party politics.

Tlie-adoption of the report was seconded by Mr. E. W. Nix, and unanimously t greed to. The retiring directors and , auditors were alse unani- mously re-elected.

PEN KETH.

WESLEYAN MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT ASSOCIA.TION.— A very interesting meeting of the above was held art Wednesday night in the Wesleyan School. The Secre- tary (Mr. E. W. Clarke) presided, and there was a good muster of the members. A new feature was introduced and was a great success, and this should be repeated in forthcoming meetings. The following ladies and gentlemen took part :—Mrs. R. Garnett, Mrs. Bell, Mrs. Clarke, Messrs. C. E. Parker, A. 0. Clay, W. Moores, T. Birch, H. J. Mainley, and J. MARY'S INSTITUTE.—On Monday night the members of the above were entertained to a lantern exhibition in their headquarters, the Infants School. Mr. James Woodward was in charge of the apparatus, and the lovely scenes of North Wales and differentparts of this country were much appreciatdd. Several humorous slides were also shown, and at the close Mr. Woodward was cordially thanked for his services. ST. MARY'S SUNDAY SCHOOL.—The annual scholars treatof the Sankey and Penketh Sunday schools, was held in the Sankey Sunday school on Thursday last. The scholars were entertained to a substantial tea, and afterwards a concert was gone through. followed by an impromptu dance, the scholars thoroughly enjoying themselves during the evening.

BLACKBURN TRAM LOSSES. An official statement of the working of the Blackburn trams during the nine months ended December last, states that the loss amounted to £2,144. The receipts inczeased from £32,832 to £34,177. This brings the, total loss on the tram undertaking to £24,640.
