BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

CHARGE AGAINST A WARRINGTON CLERK.-CASE DISMISSED. .

Edward Tueson, a clerk, of Higher Walton' Warring- ton, was charged at a special sitting of the Guildhall, Nottingham, on Monday with stealing a live tame rabbit, value £lO, the prciperty of Messrs. T. Gillett and Son, of Winds Or-terrace, Banbury, about October 4th or sth, 1902, and with receiving the rabbit well knowing it to have been stolen. Mr. Scatliff (of Messrs. Scatliff and Hunt, London), appeared for the prosecution, and Mr. T. S. Steel (of Warrington) defended.—There was a crowded Court.—The case for the prosecution was that at an exhibition held in Nottingham on the 3rd and 4th October, 1902, fessrs. Gillett and Son exhibited a Dutch tortoise-shell rabbit in a certain class and were awarded first prize. On the evening of the second day of the show the rabbit was missed from the pen. The rabbit, it was alleged, was exhibited several times by the defendant who, on being questioned, denied that it was the same rabbit. Subsequently the animal died, aud its body was sent to London, where it was recog- nised by a mark upon its foot—Defendant had given several different accounts of how he came by it.—A large number of witnesses were called to give evidence of identification. The preserved body of the rabbit was produced in Court. and bore all the distinctive featuros of the rabbit exhibited in Nottingham by the prosecu- tors.—Mr. Steel submitted that his client had no case toanswer. The case had taken up a long time,but it was a matter of serious importance to his client. The defendant was a young man of Food character, and held an important position as clerk in the Water Engineer's Department of the Warrington Corporation. The charge of stealing the rabbit was wholly unfounded, not a tittle of evidence having been given that he was even at the Nottingham show of 1902 when the rabbit was alleged to have been stolen. The only matter that could be considered was that of receiving the rabbit, well knowing it to have been stolen, and the,re was no evidence but possession, and the possession was not sufficiently recent. It had been held that after the lapse of many months a person could not b& called upon to account for the manner in which the property came into his possession: Mr. Steel cited two cases m which it had been held, by Mr. Justice Maule in the one case, and by Baron Channel! in the other, that where the possession was not sufficiently recent the prisoner was not to be tint on his defence, there being no other evidence than possession. In this case,assuming the evidence of identification was complete which he submitted was not, there was nothing beyond possession, and the case ought to be dismissed. Other cases were submitted, and he (Mr. Steel) strongly urged that tlie defendant ought not to be put on his defence. Sliould the Bench decide against !aim on the Doinc, raised, the defendant had a perfect antwer to t.he charge. but he did not wish to occupy their time further the case having taken so long. After a short deliberation the bench dismissed the case on the ground that the evidence of identification in December, 1902' was not sufficiently strong to warrant a conviction.—Mr. Steel thanked the Bench for the atten- tion they had given to the case.—The Chairman said it h-td taken a long time, but had been ably conducted on both sidds. OFFENCES AGAINST THE FACTORY ACT.—WARRINGTON EMPLOYERS FINED.

Yesterday at the Warrington Police Court, the White- cross Company, Limited, Milner-street, were summoned for ewloying a boy named John Riley after six o'clock in tht evening on January Bth.—Mr. Steel appear gl for the defendants.—Mr. A. Lewis, chief Factory InspEctor for the district,,, prosecuted and said the legal period of employment for persons under eighteen was from 6 a.m. to 6 p.m. On the date in question the boy Riley was found working at 7.5 p.m. It appeared that he (lid not commence work at gix in the 'morning but had been placed on the night 'shift. in one branch of the works the defendants were entitled to employ boys on the day and night shifts during alternate weeks, but that rule did not apply to the section in which Riley was engaged. The firm was fined for a similar offence on April 7th.—Mr. Steel said an sne regretted these irregularities'more than the direc- tors of the company, and he was sure the magistrates would appreciate the difficulty the firm had in carrying the Act out to the letter. Men were employed on piece work, and they employed the boys. The com- pany had taken every precaution to prevent boys under the statutory age going into the works .and they appointed a watchnian for that purpose. .On

Warrington Borough Police Court.

Minor Cases.

MONDAY.—Before Mr. Birchall (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst, Councillor Thornton, Messrs. Hayward and Wright. A WARNING TO LOOSE CHA RACTERS.—SeveraI women were sent to gaol for indecent conduct in the streets.— The Chief Constable desired it to be known that the police were making special efforts to rid the town of such characters and officers in plain clothes had been specially told off for that purpose.—The magistrates signified their intention of assisting the police in their crusade.

A "PERIODICAL BLOOMER."—Margaret Burke, 16 Ship-yard, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Arnold's-yard, Haydock-street, at 1.35 on Sunday morning. The Chief Constable: "She is another of our periodical bloomers, having bloomed thirty-five times." (Laughter.)—Prisoner was sent to prison for fourteen days with hard labour. BOYS IN TRouBLEAFive boys named Joseph Grey, 62, Lythgoes-lane; Samuel Beckett, 21, Turner-street; William Barker, 36, Lythgoes-lane ; Thomas Birchall, 40, Haydock-street ; and Harry Dix, 37,Winwick-street, were summoned for having made a loud and unseemly noise in Winwick-street, on the 15th inst.—PC. Hughes said the lads were throwing stones at doors.—Each defendant was fined 2s. 6d. " LAW YER " BECKETT AGAIN.—On the evidence of P.C. Hewitt a youth named Alfred Beckett, commonly known as "Lawyer" Beckett,. was fined ss. for having used obscene language in Scotland-road on Saturday night. He was further charged with having committed a breach of the peace by fighting on the night of Sep- tember 24th. It appeared that prisoner has been summoned to appear in answer to the above charge in September last, but failed to do so.—P.C. Newey proved the case, and Beckett was bound over in the sum of £5 to keep the peace. THIRTY-THIRD APPEA RANCE.—Mary Broderick, 40, Dallari-lane, was bound over in a surety of £5 to keep the peace for six months for disorderly behaviour in Dallam-lane on the 16th inst. This was her 33rd appearance. BURNING AN EFFIGY.—James Parry, 41, Greenall- street, was summoned for committing a breach of the peace by acting in a disorderly manner hi Ellesmere- street on January 18th.—The evidence showed that on the date named a large crowd engaged in a hostile demonstration against a woman named Sarah Haddock, 49, Greenall-street. An effigy composed of straw and. clothing was set on fire and defandant, who had previ- ously been annoying Mrs. Haddock, . threw it into the yard of her residence. It set fire to the rollers of a wringing machine and damaged them.- -The Chief Constable safd he had received several complaints about the disgraceful scene that was enacted by the crowd, and their Worships had in time gone by signified their intention of putting such things to an end. He (the Chief Constable). had had very good assistance from the magistrates in the past, and he again asked them to help him to maintain order. Defendant was bound over in the sum of £5 to keep the peace for six months, and also ordered to pay the costs.

TUESDAY.—Before Dr. Fox (in the chair), Alderman Hutchinson and Mr. Young.

DESERTED IN SOUTH AFRICA.—Samuel Clifford was charged with being a deserter from the Ist Battalion of the West Riding Regiment, on service in South Africa, since 1901.—Police-Constable Whalley stated that he was on duty in Bridge-street on Monday morning when he noticed the prisoner,who'was wearing a pair of mili- tary boots, and walked In military style. He went to him and asked him if he had been a soldier, and he said he had, but had finished his time. Witness then asked prisoner, to show his discharge papers, and as he could not produce them or give any satisfactory explanation be was taken into custody. On the way down to the Police Station prisoner admitted that he had deserted from the West Ridings while they were in South Africa. —He was remanded to await a military escort, the Con- stable being recommended for the usual award.

Two 'MONTHS FOR THEFT.—ThORIRS McCall, of no settled abode, was sentenced to two months' hard labour for stealing four pairs of corduroy trousers, of the value of £l, the property of Thomas Bowe, Horsemarket etreet.—P.C. Corbett met the prisoner carrying the trousers and on making inquiries found they had been stolen from outside Mr. Bowe's shop.

THURSDAY.—Before Mr. C. White (in 6e chair), and Mr. J. R. Young. A MILITIAMAN'S ESCAPADE.---Joseph McCormick, a militiaman sLationed at Orford Barracks. -.,as charged with breaking and entering the premises of Patrick Mulee; grocer and provision dealer, 45, Fairclough's Avenue, at 11.25 the previous night, with intent to steal.—Superintendent Monaghan said prosecutor and his family retired to bed about ten o'clock and an hour later prosecutor heard the breaking of glass at the rear of. the premises. On going downstairs he found the prisoner lying on the kitchen floor bleeding from wounds on the face and hands. He lifted him up and asked him how he had got there. Prisoner stated that six men had thrown him through the window. It was however found that the catch of the window had been broken and sufficient glass had been smashed to admit a man to the house. A brush had been taken from the rear of the premises and it was suspected that prisoner broke the window with it. As the man had been at Warrington only three weeks and was unable to give satisfactory accounts of himself, he (the Superintendent) asked for a remand for a week.—Prisoner was renianded accordingly.

EXPENSIVE RAILWAY JOURNEY.—WiIIiam Holden, Si, Church-street, Dukinfleld, was summoned for travel- ling on the London and North-Western Railway on January 2nd without paying the fare.—Mr. Fenna pro- secuted on behalf of the company and said defendant travelled from Manchester to Warrington. He was questioned by Detective Howells and after a time admitted thathe had travelled from Man- chester. It appeared that his employers had actually paid him the full fare to Warrington and back-.—A fine of 10s. and costs was inflicted. A NUISANCE TO THE TRAVELLING PUBLlC.—Patrick Richard Moran, 15 Portland-street, James Mulkearns, Bank-street, Michael Scully., Jolley's court, Peter Scully, Scotland-road, and Arthur Gregson; Friar's Green' were sunimoned for tiespassing on the London and North Western Railway Company's premises and refusing to quit when requested to do so by Detective Howells on January 20th.—Mr. Fenna said the company brought the case into court to endeavour to stop a nuisance at Bank Quay - station which had been growing very much of late. They were very sorry to proscute workingmen, but the defendants were continually loafinti about the station and the complaints from the travelling public were becoming very serious. The men annoyed passenger by almost insisting upon carrying their luggage, and the district superintendent for the company, Mr. Turn- bull, was recently subjected to this treatment. Defend- ants had been cautioned time after time, but they simply laughed at Detective Howells, and took not the slightest notice of complaints.—Michael Scully, who had not been summoned for the same offence before, and stated that he had a wife and two children to keep, was dis- charged. The other defendants who had all been in trouble for trespassing previously, were fined 10s. and costs, with the option of 14 days' imprisonment. A DANGEROUS PRACTICE.—John Durnbell,3B,Fother- gill-street ; John Finch, 3, Thelwall-lane • James Barrett, 39, Nicholson-street • Charles Rylands, 21, Farrell-street,and William Gibbons, of the " Star and Garter Inn," Friars'-green, were summoned for trespass- ing on the London and North-Western Railway thereby exposing themselves to danger.—Mr.Fenna poinped out that the men were employed at the cable works and in order to take a shorter cut to and from work,trespassed on the sidings where shunting operations were in pro- , gress.—Defendants were fined 10s. each. The Examiner, Saturday, January 30, 1904

WARRINGTON.

Fire Extinguished by Policemen.

Police Constable Hughes was passing along Butter- market-street at 5.20 on Wednesday morning when he detected a smell of burning. On going to Cloth Hall-yard he found that several provision boxes be- longing to Mr. William Isaac, Buttermarket-street, were ablaze. P.C. Whittaker came upon the scene and the officers SOCIIL extinguished the flames.

Liberal Meeting at Hatton. A Liberal meeting was held in the Council School, Hatton, on Monday evening and was well attended. Mr. C. Roylance presided and an interesting address on the fiscal question was delivered by Mr. John Taylor, of the National Reform Union, who showed how, in the end, the consumer would have to pay any taxes which might be imposed on foreign goods.

LADIES BET GLACE BOOTS and SHOES, UNDER the CLOCK, BRID GE-STREET.—Ad,

The Cotton Trade. The shortage of the cotton crops has resulted in an advance in the prices of raw material to such an extent that, it is stated, it is impossible for maufac- turers to take orders at anything like a paying price. The. prospects locally are gloomy, and at present the weavers at Cockhedge Mills are working only forty hours per week. In the spinning mills, however, the employes are working full time.

Treat to Juvenile Templars. On Saturday, the members of the Young Mission Juvenile Temple of the I. 0. G. T.,spent a happy time in Ebenezer church, Buttermarket street. At 4p m. the annual tea was served, among those present being Councillor. H. Thornton, Sister Mrs. Sheath, S. J. T:, Sister G. Hughes, A. S. J.T., and about a dozen mem- bers of the adult lodge. After tea, books (kindly sent by Mr. A.OE. Eccles, of Chorley), and toys were dis- tributed by Councillor Thornton. Games were after- wards indulged in, the adults romping with the children with zest. The party broke up about 9 o'clock all retiring tired but happy.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Girls', Club Silver-street. Thanks to the generosity of various friends the members of this club enjoyed a tea and entertainment on Monday evening. Mrs. R. Garnett, president of the club, was present, as well as the matron, Mrs. Davies, and several other ladies. The girls, number- ing about 78, sat down to an excellent meal, and during the evening sang several hymns in good voice. Captain Langton, of the Church Army, brought a lantern, and, after reading the story of "Teddy's Button," showed some good slides which were greatly appreciated. Cheers were given for all I riends, and an orange was presented to each girl on leaving the room. The Vice-President (Miss B. Dutton) was unable to be present owing to a previous engagement. All friends were thanked who had in any way contri- buted to make the gathering a success.

PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER the CLOCK for BES BOCT 3 in L A.NC.A.SFITRE.—.AdVt.

The Growth of Lancashire. The rapid growthof Lancashire is strikingly illustrated by 1;ome figures contained in a report by the Finance Committee of the County Council. When the Local Government Act, 1888, was passed the administrative county comprised an acreage of 1,124,450 and a popu- lation of 1,761,320. By extensions of county boroughs the acreage has been reduced by 35,868, but despite the diminished area the population is now about 82,000 larger, and the ratable value has increased by about £970,000. In round figures the gross annual expenditure of the county (ex education) is, excluding capital outlay, about £595,000, of which £365,000 is raised by rates.

ASA for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS 'MINIMA WATERS.—Advt. Fire at a Furniture Dealer's Shop. About half-past nine. o'clock on Tuesday evening, a fire vats discovered to have broken out in the store room Immediately above the shop of Mr. William Barton, furniture dealer, 54, Green-street Police- Constable Woods was early on the scene and went to the assistance of Mr. William and Mr. Thomas Barton, who wen) fortunately on the premises at the time. Several neighbours also lent willing assistance and the fire was speedily extinguished. A number of straw mattresses which were smouldering were thrown through the window and several articles of furniture which had caught fire were removed into the street. A messenger had been sent to Messrs. Crosfield's works for the fire brigade, but 'wing to the prompt measures which were taken, the services were not required and the men did not turn out.

ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASs MINERAL WATERS.—Advt. Warrington Field Club. _

At the opening meeting of the second half of the winter session, held on Friday evening, Mr. May pre- sided over a good audience, and Mr. W. Webster read a paper on" Ants." He first dealt with the classifica- tion, indicating the three great groups into which they fall, and demonstrating on the blackboard the peculiarities of the joints connecting abdomen with thorax characteristic of each ',group. He then des- cribed some of the more striking features of the anatomy of ants, and the functions of the various internal and external structures; concluding with a most interesting sketch of the habits and habitations of the various British species, largely the result of his own observations made during several years study. In the discussion which, followed Mr. Webster was warmly congratulated `on his able address. The President having arranged for Mr iNevvste,ai'... (Jr Chester, to fill February 19th with a lantern lecture--- at the usual hour 7.30 in the Museum Lecture RQOM —it was agreed to make that an open meeting; so that any non-member interested in the subject, " St. Kilda, its people and Natural History " will have the opportunity of attending.

UNDER the CLOCK or GOOD BOOTS.—Advt. Legh Street Primitive Methodist Band of Hope. A grand concert in connection with die above was held in the Legh-street schoolroom on Tuesday the 26th inst, the chair being taken by Mr. B. T. Parkes, who opened the concert with some very interesting remarks. The pupils of Miss Williams' school de- lighted the audience with their clever selections on the mandoline, and reflected great credit on their tutor, who Must have gone to considerable trouble to have trained the band to such a state of perfection. , Miss Elsie and Master Bertie .Spruce sung a duet in their usual accomplished manner, and secured loud applause. The Society were also fortunate in securing the serviees of the Ellesmere-street Band of Hope who went through a very amusing dialogue, much to the enjoyment of those present. Other dialogues and recitations were given by the members of the Legh-street Society, and Miss V. Hewitt proved to be an able and talented accompanist, her performance on the piano being excellent for one so young. Altogether Mr. Billingham (the Secretary) is to be complimented on the manner in which these entertainments are undertaken and 'carried out, and it must be very gratifying to him to be so successful in obtaining, at all of his entertainments, so many will- ing and -talented helpers in the noble work in which he is engaged.

LOCAL COMMERCE.

WORK OF THE WARRINGTON CHAMBER.

Warrington and the Ship Canal Company , MR. PARR AND THE FISCAL QUESTION. The Condition of Bank Quay Station. Several important matters affecting Warrington were discussed at the annual meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce held on Monday morning at the • Town Hall. I The President (Mr. J. Charlton Parr) presided. There I were also present' :—Messrs. C.E.Parker(vicepresident) i Edwin Woods, J. Fulton Smith, T. E. Gibson, C. D. I Parkinson, D. Garnett, J. H. Smethurst, H. Kingdom, H. Chambers, and Arthur Bennett, (hon secretary). Messrs. W. Long, F. W. Monks and T. P. Rathbone wrote regretting their inability to be present. THE ANNUAL REPORT. The HON SECRETARY submitted the 27th annual re- port and the following are the more important matters referred bo. IMPROVEMENT OF BANK QUAY STATION AND LOCAL TRAIN SERVICE. The President having introduced the question of the inadequate accommodation at Bank Quay Station, it was resolved that the Secretary be instructed to write to the London and North-Western Railway Company inquiring what plans they have in view, and how soon they expect to be in a position to carry out the desired improvements. He was also requested to communicate with them in reference to the local train service. He received the following reply, dated March 4, 1903 : "London and North-Western Railway Company, Easton Station, London, N.W. 4th March, 1903. Dear , sir,—ln reply to your letter of the 2nd inst., I beg to inform you that in connection with the widening operations an order has been given for the rearrangement and enlargement of the Bank Quay Station at Warrington, and when these works have been carried out I have no doubt that the complaints of your Council as to the accommodation at the station will be fully met. As regards the train service, the 6.23 from Warrington, which you refer to in your letter, is a summer train which will be reinstated in July. I would point out, however, that there is now a very good connection for London and the south by the 5.16 p.m. train from Warrington, reaching Easton at 9.15 p.m. I regret we cannot see our way to stop the 5.35 p.m. train from Euston at Warrington; there is already a goad connection off this train leaving Crewe at 9.25 and arriving at Warrington at 10.2 p.m.—Yours faithfully, F. HARRISON. The Secretary alluded to the improved passenger train service from and to Warring- ton (to which reference was made in Saturday's "Examiner "), and said the company had made con- cessions in addition to those which were mentioned in his report, so that the representations of the Ghamber seemed to have had the desired effezt. (Hear, hear.) EDUC ITION COMMITTEE. We have pleasure in reporting that provision was made for the representation of the chamber on the Education Committee, and that Mr. .1. Fulton Smith was accordingly appointed as one of the co-opted mem- bers of the new authority. This is a useful concession in view of the necessity for better technical training in many of our local industries, and the Council confi- dently look to great advantages as a result of the in- creased and practical attention which this subject is at last receiving. PROPOSED REDUCTION IN PASSENGER FARES. A letter was read from Mr. F. W. Monks calling attention to the reductions allowed by the Scotch Rail- way companies on certain classes of tickets; and sug- gesting the desirability of similar concessions on the lines proposed in the resolution adopted by the Asso- ciated Chambers, and it was decided to communicate with the local companies in the sense desired. Replies were sent by all of them, together with the following letter from the Railway Clearing House :-16th July, 1903. Sir,—` Week-end and ordinarSo return tickets. Your application for the following concession, viz., (a) For the issue generally of week-end tickets ; (b) For the issue of ordinary return tickets at a fare and a half, available forsix months, and allowing break of journey at all Places en route without any distinction being made between ports, manufacturing towns, university cities, and other places of historic note ; in fact, with- out discriminating between business, educational, and pleasure purposes, has been carefullyconsidered by the railway superintendents in conference:and I am desired to inform you that theyftre unable to see their way to accede thereto.—Yours obediently, H. C. SMART, Secre- tary. A. Bennett, Esq." THE LATE MR. ROBERT GARNETT. It is with great regret that we record the death of Mr. Robert Garnett, who was for two years the presi- dent, and for many years an active member of the Counci, of the chamber. IMPERIAL TARIFF PROPOSALS. The question as to the desirability of calling a general meeting to discuss Mr. Chamberlain's proposed amend- ment of our system of imperial tariffs was discussed at length, the Secretary suggesting that it might be well to have both sides of the question put before the mem- bers,Nand to give the various local trades the opportun- ity of expressing their views; hut this was not considered desirable, and it was finally resolved: "That the Executive of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce considers it premature to express any opinion on the important changes which Mr. Chamberlain suggests in the fiscal policy of this country, but would view with satisfaction any thoroughly national inquiry into the conditions under which our important international and colonial trade is carried on, and is prepared to offer any local facilities or help within its power." MANCHESTER SHIP CANAL PROPOSALS. Whilst general approval was expressed concerning the plan, the company suggest in order to maintain the level of the water in the river around Warrington at lift. 6in, above ordnance datum, the clauses which suggest that Warrington should finance the scheme and divert its own sewers were not received with favour. The Secretary suggested that, as items in a general settlement, it would be well to secure an exten- sion of the time allowed ,to Warrington for the construction of the dock; the right to widen Walton-road; and an undertaking from the Ship Canal Company not to deposit any further matter in the old river bed at Wilderspool. A deputation consisting of Messrs. C. E. Parker, J. A. Ewart, John Fairclough, E. G. George,John Hutchings. William Long, Frederick Monks, Andrew Rathbone, Henry Roberts, NV. Peter Itylands J. Fulton Smith, and Edwin Woods, was appointed to (Lend a conference at the Town Hall between the Rivers Committee, the Canal Company, the Rivers Preservation Society and the Chamber of Commerce. The conference resulted in a protracted sitting, and an agreement was arrived at on a number of the leading points at issue; but, as the Ship Canal Company made it a sine qua non that the action against the company should be dismissed, the negotiations- were abruptly ended. The company had previously declined to agree to an extension of time for the construction of the dock upon the ground that they desired to sell the site for building purposes, and they are now proceeding with their Bill and their appeal con- currently. The refusal to extend the time for the construction of the dock may ulttmately prove a serious thing for Warrington, as no other site so suitable is anywhere available, and, as the town develops, it will probably desire to take advantage of the close proximity of tho canal, and the low tolls secured by the original Act, to cultivate a larger trade than will be posssible upon the river, even though it should be deepened as proposed. WIDENING OF WARRINGTON BRIDGE. The following resolution was unanimously adopted: "That the Corporation be respectfully requested to reconsider the proposal to widen Warrington Bridge by cantilever footways, with a view to substituting a new, bridge which shall be adequate for the accommodation of any probable future developments in both road and river traffic." LOCAL TRAFFIC TO AND FROM THE LAY-BY. The Secretary also reported that negotiations were . in progress with a view to getting several of the lead- ing z•tna:r.ciiip lines to Manchester to call at the lay-by for Warrington goods. and that some of, them were willing to do so if sufficient cargo couic. ot, col:ectcd to make it worth their while. He suggested that the Chamber of Commerce might usefully act as intermedi- aries in the matter. THE BALANCE SHEET. The SECRETARY also presented the balance sheet 1 Twelve month 3 ago there was, he said, a balance in . hand of .CG 65.10 d. The present balance was RC 45. 10d., so that the chamber had practically maintained its position. MR. PARR AND TETE FISCAL QUESTION. AN UNPREJUDICED TRIBUNAL NEEDED. INDIGESTION.

• Whim the stomach refuses to digest its food all manner of troubles arise. This is Indigestion. The food lays in the stomach, and instead of its nutritious qualities being extracted to make the life-blood and tissues of the body, it ferments, sours, or decomposes, and thus gives rise to sickness, nausea,headache,bilious- ness. and all manner of complaints. So bad was my Indigestion," said Mr. J.Harle, 6, Oakland-terrace,Fern- dale, Glam.," that it was impossible for me to go to work. Pains in the chest and stomach were often very acute. Mention this paper and enclose Id. stamp for postage.

There was, however, one great question which could not ' be ignored, and which must affect our commercial inter- ests vitally and radically—a question upon which they should welcome an authoritative pronouncement if they could have it from some infallible and omniscient source. He felt that to attempt to discuss even the fringe of the question at that meeting would be inopportune and inexpedient. But, although that was not the time or place for discussing the great fiscal question, it behoved us to bake it into our serious consideration with a view to formulating a policy and an opinion upon it. It was a question which affected the commerce and trade of the Empire, and it was to chambers of com- merce that the public would look for authoritative guidance, for chambers of commerce could calmly and deliberately weigh the pros and cons apart from politi- cal inspiration, and independent of the interested trade combinations. (Hear, hear.) What they wanted tc achieve was the safeguarding of our trade and commerce first in the interests of the United Kingdom, for charity began at home, and next in the interests (if our great colonies and dependencies. He would, therefore, urge that chamber to take the matter into its earnest and serious consideratidn, and would specially urge those members of it who were capable from their business experienceand knowledge of facts and figures to bring their information to bear upon the subject, not from an interested and selfish point of view, but from an imperial and patriotic standpoint, for we were all citizens of one great empire, and our main object should be not the advancement of one trade or one industry, but the general prosperity of the mother country first, and then the welfare of her children, her colonies and dependencies in all parts of the world. (Applause.) Before coming to a conclusion he hoped we might be taught and advised by those whose know- ledge and experience were greater than our own. We had teachers and talkers enough, some armed with facts and figures more or less accurate and reliable, but amongst that multitude of counsellors how many were there who had more than the mere elementary and hear- say knowledge of the subject they are so ret.dy to discuss and dogmatize upon, and, as the decision upon that momentous and vital question must ulti- mately rest with the great body of electors, who might not have the time, the means, the opportunity or capacity for judging for themselves, and must depend upon the advice of others, it was to his mind most essential that time should be taken and precipitate action be avoided in order that full inquiry might be made and figures verified by some compete9t and un- prejudiced tribunal like a royal commission before that most important issue was submitted to the ultimate arbiter—the voice of the people. (Hear, hear, and applause.) It was here, he thought, that chambers of commerce might very usefully intervene with advice and assistance, having at their command information and machinery for acquiring and testing such informa- tion, from practical and reliable s ources. But above all, he hoped that the great fiscal question would not be made the shuttlecock or the battlefield of political parties, but that the Government would approach it from a neutral point of view, and by means of an ex- haustive inquiry elicit all the information that could be obtained, and give time for party and personal feeling to subside in favour of imperial and national interests. Alluding to Bank Quay Sta- tion, the President said the Secretary had read a satis- factory letter so far as it went, but it was dated the 4th of March, and they had very nearly reached the 4th of March again. Personally he did not see that very much had been effected at the station, and he, therefore, sug- gested that another letter should be written to the authorities. If greater expedition could be used it would be more satisfactory to the chamber and to the town generally. They could congratulate themselves upon the representation they had on the Education Board, and if any particular body deserved representa- tion, where information and instruction could be given, it was a chamber such as their own, which merely met for the advantage and the instruction of the people at , large. They all, he said, regretted the death of Mr. Robert Garnett, a former president. Mr. Garnett was , a man who was very much respected, and he took a ; leading part in the work of the town and in trade and -' commerce generally. He was a useful member of the Chamber of Commerce, and he (the President) suggested that a letter of condolence should be sent to the widow I and family of the deceased.

The HON. SECRETARY promised that that should be done.

The PRESIDENT, continuing, alluded to the Congress at Montreal, and hoped the effect of the Congress would not be confined to that meeting, but that the informa- tion would be widespread and useful all over the Empire. He was sorry that chamber was not represented. He and others were unable to attend, but the exhaustive report which had been read brought them very !much into touch with all the objects of the Congress. (Hear, hear.) With regard to the Ship Canal, that was neither the time nor the place to discuss the question. Matters were pending, and they were in good hands in the Cor- poration, but as a consultative body they would be ready to give any advice and assistance they could to render help to the Corporation. (Hear, hear.) He thanked Mr. Parker for attending the annual meeting of the Associated Chambers of Commerce; he did so no doubt with pleasure, interest and instruc- tion. With respect to the widening of Warrington Bridge, he (the speaker) felt that of the two suggestions before them, the building of a permanent new bridge was the wisest course to take—(hear, hear)—because he could not help feeling that, even if the suggestion now made as to the widening of the footpaths, which would to a certain extent give more easy access, was agreed to, it would only be for a time, and it would to a certain extent be throwing away money, because in another decade, and that was but a short- time in the life of a town, the bridge would have to be taken up and a more comprehensive, and probably a much more important and worthy bridge, would have to be established. That was the view of the chamber, and if they could accentuate that view they would be prepared to do so. He moved the adoption of the report and the statement of accounts.

Mr. J. FULTON Smiim seconded, and observed that there were many sides other than those which had been mentioned in which the chamber could exercise an influence for the benefit of the trade of the town. Mr. PARKER said he wished New Zealand had,instead of giving the Ereference, reduced their duties. They all wished that. It was put in the report as if New Zealand had given them a reduction. The SECRETARY: I did not mean that. Perhaps I might alter a word or two in the report to express it differently. The motion was then carried. . • BOARD OF TRADE RETURNS. CONSTITUTION OF THE COUNCIL. "OUTSIDE MEMBERS TOO QUIET." Mr. KINGDON, alluding to the Board of Trade returns said it might be interestinsg, to the members to know, if they were not already aware of it, that they were ad- vised that certain other important 'additional informa- tion was shortly proposed. (Hear, hear.) The HON SECRETARY: Relating to local matters? Mr. KINGDOM : Chiefly dealing with the diffentiation of the countries of origin. The HON SECRETARY said the chamber felt the diffi- culties which prevented it exercising its full degree of usefulness. It was a fact that outside members were too quiet. THANKED.

Mr. PARKER, in moving a vote of thanks to the President and to Mr. Fulton Smith for their admirable addresses, alluded to the fiscal question, about which many of them differed very much, and very radically. It was desirable the country should have time to think the question well out before any radical change was adopted in their policy. He was pleased to hear Mr. Parr suggest a Royal Commission. A similar sugges- tion was made by the Duke of Devonshire the other day in Liverpool. Whatever their idea of politics might be, his suggestion should carry weight with the country, and if they could get the matter discussed apart from party politics, and brought before an authoritative Royal Commission, an amount of light would be brought to bear on the subject which it would be difficult el find in any other way. (Hear, hear.) He should like the Chambers of Commerce to press upon the Government the desirability of adopting the sug- gestion made by the Duke of Devonshire, and con- firmed by their President that morning. They were very grateful to Mr. Parr for having taken the office of President, and they were also pleased they had such a good representative as Mr. Smith on the Education Committee. His report showed that he had grasped the essential features of the problem of education.

No Breakfast Table complete without

Mr. GIBSON seconded and said he hoped that means would be adopted during the ensuing year by which the sympathy and active co-operation of the business firms of Warrington could be gained. He did not wish to criticise the past, but simply to throw outa suggestion for the future.

_ The SECRETARY put the resolution to a meeting, and it was carried unanimously. ELECTION OF PRESIDENT. _ _ _ MR. PARR RE-ELECTED.

Mr PARR expressed a wish to be relieved of the office of president, as he felt he as no longer in the right place. He would, however, continue to take a deep interest in the work of the chamber. The SECRETARY suggested that Mr. Parr should con- tinue in office another year to preserve the continuity. It was usual for the president to accept office for a second year. _

Mr. ['AIM said there had been several opportunities during the year where a more active president than himself might not only have been useful at the confer- ences but also able to give information to the members when he came back. He thought someone more directly interested in business would be a more pseful president. He felt that he was not sufficiently strong to undertake the responsibility of the office. He, therefore, thought he might be allowed to retire.

Mr.-SmEniu • said they all appreciated very much the remarks wh had fallen from Mr. Parr, and were delighted to think that during the past year they had had the pleasure of his services to the chamber, but he did not agree with the remark that he wasnot interested in business. He did not know many men who were as greatly interested in business and in the welfare of the borough as Mr. Parr, and if he would do them the honour of accepting office for another vear he could again assure him of the assistance he asked for last year and the assistance of the executive in all matters. He 'hoped Mr. Parr would see his way to serve another year. (Hear, hear). Mr. EDWIN ‘VOODS also hoped Mr. Parr would serve again, as he had always taken a great interest in the town and done much to advance its welfare. Mr. PARR said he did not wish to dissociate himself with business in the slightest degree. Information reached other people which did not reach him, and he felt thaz not having sufficient knowledge, and not being in the way of acquiring that knowledge, he was not the proper man to preside over such a responsible chamber as that was and, he hoped, would be. He would be glad to assist the chamber in any other way. Mr. SMETHURST said the man who hesitated was lost. (Laughter.) Mr. Parr was thoroughly suitable for the position of president, and he moved his re- election. (Hear, hear.) This was seconded and carried unanimously. Mr. PARR, replying, stid he could only thank them for their kind and cordial opposition to his wishes. (Laughter.) He did not wish to retire where he could be of use. ANOTHER STEP IN ADVANCE,

In no department of science has there been such con- tinued advance as that of the treatment and cure of disease. Yesterday, the world believed Diabetes an in- curable disease; to-day, the annduncement is made that a remedy has been discovered which will cure any case of diabetes.

--fti-e following statement is given as proof: Mr. George W. Bates of St. Thomas's Road, Derby, says :—" For two years Diabetes held me in its grip ! I had some of the best doctors in Derby and attended the Royal Infirmary for a long time without getting the slightest relief. I wasted away to a mere skeleton. I was so weak I could scarcely stand. In fact, I thought I was going to die. "But I am not dead, for Dodd's Pills have saved my life. From the first dose I 'began to improve, and within a few weeks I weighed nearly twelve stone. HILL CLIFFE LIGHTING.-LETTER FROM MR. H. ROBERTS. The Clerk read a letter from Mr. H. Roberts, of " Kingsley," Hill Cliff e, Warrington who, writing under date 29th December, 1902, suggested that, as the Council had now placed additional gas lamps on the Northwich-road above London Bridge, Stockton Heath, an opportunity arose of extending the lighting into Windmill-lane, the approaches to which, he contended were in a dangerouscondition. He requested that the Council would take early steps to improve both the state of the road and the approaches to it. Mr. Pilling considered that Mr. Roberts was asking for too much. The Council had gone to the full extent of the gas main and if they did as Mr. Roberts sug- gested it would require about half a mile of additional main off the main road. He did not see how they could do anything in the matter at present. In the course of the discussion it was pointed out that the cost of extra lamps would devolve upon the parish. Mr. Billington pointed out that some few years ago two lamps were erected and the Appleton Parish Coun- cil refused to pay for them. Consequently the lamps were taken down. Recently at the request of occupants of new houses in the neighbourhood more lamps were erected, although no request was receive(' from the Parish Council. He understood many ratepayers were not -satisfied with what had been done and he did no think the Council ought to extend the main any further Ir. Darlington pointed out that new property had b4en erected and this had largely increased the ratable value. He therefore thought the refluest was deserving of some consideration.

Mr. Piling agreed that the assessment had increased very considerably of late and it would continue to do so almost every month. He did not, however, see why the Council should extend the main until more property was erected on the road. _ _

Mr. S-IN-Tin-ti;;; ;id Windmill-lane was not in very good. order. It had not been much used until new property was recently erected. He could provide for an im- provement in his estimates for the coming year. The Clerk was instructed to inform Mr. Roberts that the matter was receiving the consideration of the Council.

TRE ROADS AT NORLEY. The Clerk made a statement in regard to damage alleged to have been done to the roads at Norley in c9bnection with recent Liverpool water undertakings. AUessrs. Holme and King, the contractors, had been un- able to come to terms with the surveyor on the matter and they would not now agree to the ap- pointment of an independent surveyor to assess the damage. He had communicated with the Town Clerk of Liverpool who replied that he had noticed the attitude taken up by th 6 contractors with regard to the re-instatement of roads. The Water Com- mittee were not in a position, having regard to their obligations to their contractors, to come to any settle- ment with the Rural Council and under these circum- stances the best thing would be for the Board to pro- ceed in the ordinary course. On the motion of Mr. Woodward it was decided that the roads be repaired and the account forwarded to the Liverpool authorities.

WESTON ROAD IMPROVEMENT.

Mr. Guest moved the rescinding of a resolution passed on December Ist. "That subject to the surveyor being able to obtain a portion of the garden in front of Mr. Tinsley's house, the roadway in front of and adjoin- ing the Roman Catholic School, Weston, be widened in accordance with plans laid before the Council by the surveyor at a cost of £2O Bs. 3d. ; and in the event of the portion of the garden being unob- tainable the roadway be widened in accordance with the suggestions of the Parochial Committee at a cost of £l6 Bs. 3d." He pointed out that up to the present they had been unable to obtain the corner of the garden but the committee were desirous that the full amount should be expended on the improvement. The High- ways Committee had agreed to the proposal at a. previous meeting. Mr. Antwis seconded. After considerable discussion the resolution was carried.

THE SHIP CANNI. BILL.

Mr. Linaker reported that the sub-committee appointed to consider the Manchester Ship Canal Bill had inspected some of the outfalls which would be affected. After- wards they met Mr. Hunter and the Company's solicitors in Manchester and it was agreed that clauses should be submitted by the company for the approval of the Council. Tt was coasidered advisable that the Clerk Should see the Parliamentary agents and as he (Mr. Linaker) had to go to London on other business Mr. Ashton accompanied him. They saw the agents and submitted the clauses Prepared by the company which they thought wanted a little enlargement. The committee had passed a resolu- tion that the necessary clauses be obtained if possible; and it was hoped to have an undertaking from the com- pany that the clauses would be inserted in the Bill be- fore the next meeting. Failing that, the clerk had been instructed to prepare a petition to lodge if neces- sary. The Council must do their utmost to protect the outfalls. The company admitted that many outfalls would be seriously affected and they seemed perfectly willing to meet the Council in a friendly spirit. The Council was guile as friendly towards the company. The chairman, vice-chairman, the clerk, and himself had been instructed to engage an engineer in connection with the Bill if necessary. The minutes were confirmed. -

Mr. J. S.Jeacock, Clerk to the Norton Parish Council wrote stating that the Council at their last meeting had inspected the plans, &c., contained in the proposed new Bill of the Manchester Ship Canal Company when it was resolved that the District Council be applied to to watch the interests of the parish of Norton with re- spect to the outfalls of the drains, &c., so that there might be no interference with the drainage of the parish.

ELECTRICITY I,ND POWER GAS BILL. The Chairman presented a report in reference to this Bill. In the original allocation of the costs of the opposi- tion by the Association of Rural and Urban District Councils the charge against the Council was £77.As the result of an arrangement .arrived at at a further confer- ence the contribution had been reduced to £45.

The Clerk was instructed to ask the promoters of the new Bill to be introduced, for the insertion of a clause protecting the Council in regard to their roads. ".“.•.• • Dr. Adams reported that during the month there had been a case of diphtheria at Stockton Heath, a case of scarlet fever at Norley, and two clses of scarlet fever in the Workhouse. It was suspected that the source of infection at the Workhouse was several parcels sent to the children from outside friends during Christmas time. The children attacked by the disease were the only ones who received parcels. Cases of anthrax at Weston Point and4ton (2) had been notified. The Chairman expressed the opinion that one of the cases at Acton was not anthrax, but Dr. Adams said he received his information from the police who relied upon the veterinary surgeon.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Royal Wedding.—lt is nearly thirteen years since the last Royal wedding at Windsor Castle, where the marriage of Princess Alice of Albany, and Prince Alexander of Teck is to take place on February 10th. This will be the sixty-fourth anniversary of the wedd- ing of Queen Victoria and Prince Albert, which was ?solemnised at the Chapel Royal in St. James' Palace. There were only five Royal weddings at Windsor Castle during Queen Victoria's reign,. including that of the puke and Duchess of Albany in 1882. Princess Alice will have the two daughters of the Duke and Duchess of Connaught as her principal bridesmaids,and the little °nes will include Princess Mary of Wales who is not Yet quite seven, and this will be her first undertaking as a bridesmaid. • . .

A Pretty Custom.—The late Queen introduced the Pretty custom of planting a sprig of myrtle taken from the wedding wreath or bouquet of a Royal bride. When the Princess Royal was married in 1858, a sprig was carefully culturea, and from this tree a cutting has figured in the floral decorations at all the Royal weddings that have since taken place. There is a fine Specimen of myrtle planted by Queen Victoria at Osborne House in 1873, on the marriage of her grand- daughter Princess Charlotte of Prussia, and though Osborne is no longer a Royal residence, sprigs from this tree will no doubt figure.in the wreath or bouquet Carried by Princess Alice.

Royaltyand Rates.—The bouquet fora Royal wedding is always made up by a firm of Windsor florists and it may be readily imagined that the Royal borough greatly 'welcomes such an event in the Royal Family. It fills the Castle with guests, and means not a little to the People of Windsor in the way of business. At the same time though Windsor owes so much to Royal favour, the borough has on more than one occasion measured Swords with the authorities at the Castle. It had a Memorable dispute with Prince Consort, who objected te.pay rates for the Flemish Farm. and in the end it gained the day, though not before the comic papers, dFew harrowing pictures of the bailiffs being in posses- sion of Windsor Castle. After that the rates were alvmys pod, as is done now, but they are paid volun- tarily and not asa legal liability. Dollars for Social Position.—Up to thirty years ago la belle Americaine," wasa comparative stranger to l'ondon Society, and until then, says a writer in one of the New York papers, she never though of buying social 13,0sition with her dollars. It is a sore point with many A -mericans that their great heiresses should marry and settle down in this country, and, according to them, it the wicked wife hunters from this side who are to lame. Evil communications corrupt good manners,a,nd we are told that " American women for the most part lived simple Christian lives, until they came into rela- toll with English Society. It should be interesting to now what our American peeresses think of these re- criminations. Since Mrs George Cornwallis West—then LadY Randolph Churchill—made her debut, they have rifled so many conquests as not to be altogether free ora jealousy for taking partners from British girls. Dowry by Weight.—The American parents' who are .B.ocused of unnatural conduct in selling their daughters !or a title, are simple folks compared with the more OUsinesslike citizen of Berlin who has announced his intention of disposing of his daughters by weight. In the East, and with many of the uncivilised races, the fatter the maiden the more she is sought after, but this rule is generally reversed amongst Western nations, and the father announced that he would give each of his five daughters a dowry proportionate to their weight ma their wedding day. This soon brought suitors, and ene being accepted, he was introduced to the family. After a week he chose the stoutest of the five, to whom he was married the other day, and the lady, weighing n stone, received the largest dowry. The dreaded Crinoline.—Accordine to Messrs. Paquin, we need not fear the renewal of the crinoline. Many Women seem to have looked upon the introduction of narrow bands of flexible metal in some of the latest skirts, as the thin end of the wedge, but we are assured by the great Parisian firm that we have nothing to dread in this direction. We still have with us, the softest of soft clinging fabrics, artistically draped, and we are well aware of the value of this partictilar style ever to part with Lit again. Quite a new stuff, is a compromise between crepon and crepe de chine, called crepe de soie. This wears excellently and is by no means so costly as crepe de chine. Then there is the new chiffon-voile a heavier and more durable make of chiffon, which if carefully manipulated, washes well. That exquisite fabric chiffon-velours is enjoying an immense vogue, and is perhaps the most becoming and delightful material we have yet had offered us. Evening Bodices.—Regarding the cut of evening bodices, this is more varied than ever, but the Victorian Style prevails. The wise woman however, will not be guided by the fashion plates and her dressmaker, but Will study her figure impartially, and then endeavour to Make the best of it. The best plan is to drape some soft material before a glass arranging it first one way and then another, and consider well the results. Having found a style that really suits one, it is best to remain faithful to it, modifying it from time to time as occa- aion arises. Queen Alexandra has followed this rule all her life with the result that Her Majesty is the best dressed woman in the kingdom. The Bridal Veil.—Many and various are the ways in Which recent brides have arranged their veil and wreaths but neQrly all seem to have agreed on banishing the 'veil which falls over the face. One idea is to arrange the veil in the old-fashioned way, the front drapery 'being considerably shorter than the back, which should ithnost fall to the edge of the train. Over this the wreath is Placed, and the front drapery thrown back over it. &lather idea is to gather the two ends of one side of the square into soft, becoming chou.r, and fix these on tithor side of the somewhat highly arranged coiffure. lc et another notion is to catch a veil so arranged, up on to one or both shoulders, and from thence allow it to over the train. But the first idea is the most be- coming, and desirable in every way. 'he Fur Toque.—The sable toque—so much in evidence—has always proved a little unbecoming to ,e,wners of dark brown hair, but the happy notion of a 'lee of white, or color, between the hair and the toque, ,Lelakes it possible for the brunette. Flower toques are "e in g built for early spring wear, but comparatively few spring flowers are being utilised for them. We have ceased to symbolise the season of the year by the flowers we wear in our hats and toques. Fish and Cancer.—One of the latest theories of the Zifiin of cancer attributes this terrible disease to fish. .:•,ae investigations carried out by the Cancer Research fund, have resulted, it is said, inthe discovery of cancer 'n flak and if that is so. it may lead to a similar scare as exists with .regard to oysters and typhoid. That oYsters are the most common cause of typhoid isbeyond all question, but it will be much less readily believed eat fish cause cancer, though it is supposed that salt may perhaps have something to do with the disease. There is, however, nothing as yet to shew that cancer is more prevalent amongst large fish eaters than amongst ' other classes. The fishermen and their families mostly 'Live upon the produce of the sea, and yet it has never (ieon asserted that they suffer more than any other People float cancer. MAD GE.

FASHIONS OF TO-rdilY.

The weather in our little island at this sea- son of the year leaves much to be desired as we are for the most part subjected to much rain and piercing winds with only occasional brief interludes of sunshine. These climatorial vagaries are certainly not conducive to light- ness of heart, :for alas, too many of us, if for- tunate enough to escape the fiend influenza, find ourselves victimised by colds and coughs. When, however, the clouds lift and the winds are lulled we fairly revel' in all the too fleeting sunshine and ponder as to how and in what manner we shall clothe ourselves when the weather becomes brighter and more settled and heav ysand sombre garb may be cast aside for smarter and newer garb. It is, of

course, much too early to speak definitely as to the spring modes, for no one has yet any real knowledge on the subject_a,nd we can only meantime enjoy the pleasures of anticipation. The shops have not yet recovered from the annual sales and there are still so-called bar- gains to be obtained at some, but unless the articles are washable they will not be worth buying, for in the past few weeks they have not escaped being soiled by constant handling. Ribbons and trimmings of all kinds are, how- ever, quite worth investing in and are being sold at really greatly reduced prices,. while underwear of all sorts will always be found useful. Sometimes undergarments which have been previously exhibited in the window, and are therefore soiled, can be purchased very cheap, and after a visit to the laundry are as good as ever. The smart toilette depicted is made with one of the fashionable capes. The material is smooth surfaced navy blue cloth trimmed only with stitched bands of silk to match, and a double row of small round gold buttons down the front. The skirt has a spring at the foot

A PRETTY BLOUSE

given by inserted pleats, and the hat Is of felt trimiged with a large silk bow. The style is one Edited to a girl of sixteen or seventeen years of age. Many of the smartest costumes at present are made with one or more capes, although the single tape is perhaps the most chic. The success of the cape, of course, depends on its cut and fit. In no case, how- ever, are the capes lower than the elbow line. Some coats are made with a three-caped tippet turned back either side in front with revers, while others have a single cape with a narrow embroidered stole arranged flatly round the neck and extending down the front either to or just below the waist line. Some, again, take the form of a crossover, disappear- ing intet narrow points at the waist. Em- broidered fichus and cape-like collars are also worn on blouses, to which they give a dressy appearance. Fortunate indeed is the girl' who has some of these charming accessories laid "y among family relics as they are no* in- valuable oand can be taken into immediate wear.

The pretty blouse shown in my second sketch is finished round the decolletage by a dainty lace berthe which falls over the shoul-

ders, while it is finished in front by a hand- some and soft bow of pale pink silk crepe. The blouse itself is of very pale mauve and is simply made, its colour being carefully selected so as to harmonize and not clash ;with the tones of the pink bow. The loose sleeves are finished at the wrists by turning back lace cuffs which match the lace berthe. Embroi- dered lawn fichus and cuffs are precious heir- looms and indeed now is the time when one should overhaul their old stores of lace and trinkets as among them will be found many treasures which the ever-revolving wheel of fashion has again brought into vogue. The beauty of many of these old-time articles of bigotiterie make them a fortune in themselves for we nowadays appreciate the true artistic value of dress as well as the mere fashionable- ness thereof. There are also dainty embroi- dered collars and cravats which give a neat and smart finish to a plain morning shirt of flannel or cashmere.

Very picturesque indeed is the charming hat depicted made of white felt bordered with brown fur and with a sweeping ostrich feather curling over the brim at the back, on to the hair of the wearer. The style is an exceeding-'!, ly becoming one to most faces and is well suited to the present modes of dress. The tricorne and marquise hats are still much in vogue in felt and velvet, bordered in some instances with fur. As yet, however, the millinery modes show few novelties worthy of note and we must content ourselves with Things as they are and await further devcieß. meats.

THE FARMERS AND

===== 0002601_19040130_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CHAMBERLAIN.

COLONIAL v. BRITISH INTERESTS.

FREE TRADE AND PROSPEROUS

===== 0002601_19040130_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
AGRICULTURE

LZAFLET No. 39 issued by the Tariff Reform League informs us that:

Mr. Chamberlain wants two things. The first thing he wants is to buy from our Colonies as far as possible instead of from foreigners. The second thing he wants is to take care, whilst dc!:iing with the Colonies, not to increase the cost of living • to any working man, woman, and child in the kingdom.

If Mr. Chamberlain really wants the second of these, then Mr. Chamberlain wants two very opposite things. Ile has told us that "IF WE GIVE A PREFERENCE TO THE COLONIES WE MUST PUT A TAX UPON FOOD." Very well, you cannot put a tax upon food without increasing the cost of food to every man, woman, and child in the kingdom.

===== 0002601_19040130_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
Colonial Competition.

BUT Mr. Chamberlain tells us that under his scheme the Colonies will be able to supply us with all the food we require, and to send it in such large quantities that, in spite of the tax, it will not cost us tiny more. Now if this were to happen vthat would it mean ? It would mean that our markets would be so flooded with Colonial corn that the Colonies would be able to undersell the British grower. Would that help British agriculture ? Mr. Chamberlain's scheme would offer the Colonies special terms to compete with our own farmers' produce. Farmers grumble at foreign competition, but it is nothing to what Colonial competition would be if Mr. Chamberlain had his way.

===== 0002601_19040130_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Cattle

IT is always important to remember that 68 percent. of our British farms are under fifty acres in extent. These farmers make their living by rearing cattle, and by markat gardening. Mr. Chamberlain's scheme would, while increasing the sale of 'Colonial meat, also increase the price of feeding stuffs for British cattle, and so make cattle-breeding unprofitable. Then his general scheme of taxing everybody would increase prices to such an extent that the working classes would be able to buy less and less from the market gardener. •

Small Holdings. WE have seen that an increase in the price of corn would mean hardship for small holders. Not only would a rise• in the price of corn render unprofitable many agricultural in- dustries which are now flourishing, but it would offer a direct inducement to landlords once more to drive the poorer man off the iand in order to add field to field and secure high rents for farms of large area. On the other hand, if Free Trade is maintained, the small farmer can face the future without anxiety. With the decreasing price of bread the consumption of all articles of a more luxurious character, such as milk, butter, eggs, poultry, fruit, and vegetables, will increase, as it has increased since the fall of corn prices.

The Free Trade Remedy. FREE TRADERS are taunted with having no remedy for agricultural depression. ,There is no truth in the taunt. Our remedy is to place the men upon the land. We do not believe in trying to reproduce prairie farms in England. Where a large farm will fail we can put men on the land, under. fair conditions, and succeed. We have abundant evidence •of success of small holdings in Lincolnshire and Norfolk. We are prepared to advocate the extension of small holdings throughout the country.

Successful Agriculture and Free Trade. WHAT nonsense it is to talk of agriculture being a lost industry when foreign butter fetches a bigger price in our markets than British butter! Butter is not the only thing where WE ARE BEATEN BY THE FOREIGNER SIMPLY BECAUSE WE ARE NOT UP TO DATE IN OUR LAND SYSTEM AND METHODS. Last year we imported: £20,299,000 worth of Butter £6,255,000 „ „ Cheese - £6,284,000 „ „ Eggs £4,242,000 „ „ Lard 213,110,000 „ „ Bacon £8,042.000 „ „ Beef £3,738,000 „ „ Hams £6,906.000 „ „ Mutt ,n £1,723,000 „ „ Pork £1,037,000 „ „ Poultry £4,404,950 „ „ Other meats £3,325,000 „ „ Vegetables T is a total of £79,855,000 worth of produce which could be produced in this country if men were put on the land to produee it. Little Denmark is able to make agriculture a success why cannot we in England?

Denmark's Secret of Success. WILY Is it that Denmark is the most successful agricultural country in the world? Because Denmark is a country of small hold- ings and Free Trade. Denmark not only supplies her own needs, but exported during the years 1896 and 1900 (the last year we have figures for) : Produce. 1896. 1900. Eggs £550,000 ... £1,000,000 Bacon ... £2,111,000 ... £3,278,000 'Butter ... £5,167,000 ... £6,667,000 Meat ... Z 208,000 ... £708,000 Agriculture on the Denmark small holdings has grown more prosperous each year.

===== 0002601_19040130_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
Pro(perity Without Protection.

• DENMARK does all this without Protection. She imports her feeding-stuffs free of duty, and is not protected in apy way. The remedy for British agriculture is a thorough revision of our land system, and a sweeping reform in our railways. When we can get men tlie land without any difficulty, when rel. 3 are fair and railway rates are low, we sha see agriculture flourishing in Great Britain as it flourishes in Denmark. But it is thc interest of the landed proprietors to try and persuade agriculturists that their only remedy is Protection.

SIR JOHN BRUNNER AND THE

FISCAL QUESTION.

Sir John Brunner, M.P., addressed a Liberal meeting held at Chester on Tuesday evening in furtherance of the candidature at the next general election of Mr. Mond. He remarked that whereas in France, Germany, and the United States manufacturers were extremely rich—in America rich to a degree which had never before been known in the history of the world—the great mass of the people were not prosperous. They were much poorer, ill-red, and ill-clothed than the corresponding classes in this country. If they adopted Mr. Chamberlain's policy, the price of every article that went in their homes would be raised. If a man had to pay an extra 10 per cent for his articles he would, of course, buy 10 per cent less from the shopkeepers, and the latter would lose to that extent. A policy that did not result in higher prices would have fewer advocates. (Hear, hear.) They should not believe that other countries were prospering with Protection. True it made manufacturers very wealthy, but the taxes on necessities were levied by people who would not feel them. He had reckoned that it had taken Mr. Andrew Carnegie thirty years to accumulate forty millions. He (Sir John) divided that over the average population of the United States, and he found that if each one of them had only paid eightpence per head per annum it would amount to Mr. Camegie's fortune. Nobody would feel the extra eightpenee on their taxation, but the fact that it was not felt did not make it morally right. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Chamberlain's policy simply meant legalised pocket-picking. (Applause). If they adopted Mr. Chamberlain's policy they must say good- bye to honesty in public life, awl he prayed to God it might never show its head in this country. (Applause.)

2,500 FOR PERSONAL INJURIES.

Through the negligence of the drivers of a tram and a 'bus belonging respectively to the London United Tramways and the London General Omnibus Company, a collision occurred and the 'bus overturned. One of the passengers who sustained injuries was a Mrs. Phillips, of Chiswick. who has since become a mental and physical wreck. She and her husband were awarded on Tuesday, before Mr. Justice Grantham, damages to the amount of £2,500 against the two companies besides £420 expenses.

From the evidence given at the inquest at Wigton, on Tuesday on the body of Mr. Nordaunt Lawson, third son of Sir ‘Vilfrid Lawson,M.P., it appeared that death had resulted from prussic acid poisoning, self-adminis- tered whilst journeying in a first-class railway carriage from Carlisle to Wigton on Monday night.

===== 0002601_19040130_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
The Co-operative Society.

Sir,—As one who is very much interested in the Warrington Co-operative Society I wish to express to you my sincere thanks for the kind allusion to it which appeared in last Saturday's "Examiner." I am sure the members are well pleased with the services Mr. Thomas Birchall has rendered for so many years, and they will heartily agree in his re-appointment as President, hoping he may continue to render valuable service for many years. But I am afraid that the one person who is really the most deserving of praise was quite unintentionally omitted from your complimentary leaderette. Those who are at all acquainted with the growth of this most successful society must be well aware that to the Manager and Secretary, Mr. A. H. Rathbone, the chief credit is due. In my opinion he is one of the most modest of men, and would leave him- self out of any reference he might be called upon to make respecting the society for which he has laboured for 40 years. I think it is due to him, seeing that perhaps not a sixtieth part of the members attend the quarterly meet- ings that an expression of great gratitude should be given to one who, in season and out, vary early and late, has devoted his life to the building up of a society whose benefits are now far reaching, and bene- ficially affect probably 20,000 of the inhabitants of this town. May he also be spared to continue his work and to feel that he still possesses that great esteem inwhich he has long been held in the hearts of the people.— Yours &c., __January 29th, 1904.

A MEMBER.

, [We are grateful to "A member" for pointing out the omission we were unintentionally guilty of last week. Mr. Hornby Rathbone should certainly not be over- looked in apportioning honour where honour is dues —ED. "Ex."]

===== 0002601_19040130_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
The National Service League.

Sir,—The enclosed letter Wll., written by the under- signed Directors a few days back, and sent to the Secretary of the National Service League,who inserted it in/the principal London papers. As we think this should be of interest to people living inWarrington we should esteem it a favour if you would kindly insert it in your next Saturday's issue. Yours faithfully, for JOSEPH CRO,FIELD AND SONS, LTD.

_ G. R. CROSFIELD. Sir.—Whilst we have watched with groat interest and sympathy the growth of the National Service League in our town,' and have also read .r eports of its work throughout the country, we have been struck by the fact that no notice has been taken of the very great benefits which might be obtained from a commercial and manufacturing point of view as the result of the adoption of the proposals of the league, and thus we venture to address these few lines to you. Everybody who takes an interest in commercial matters knows that we have been, and are being, beaten in many trades in this country mainly on account of the superior education and knowledge of our Foreign competitors. Professor Dewar has stated that the standard of education of the German people is two generations ahead of us. Their extraordinary commer- cial expansion and success is admittedly due mainly to the mental development of the whole nation, and this has been caused by the stimulous,to education produced by certain military advantages, including partial exemption from service given to all who have reached a given standard of education. It is difficult to estimate the immense value which this directand strong inducement to the youth of the country to work and educate themselves has been to the manufacturing and commercial interests of our continental competitors. If we are not ultimately to be much more seriously outdistanced as a manufacturing country, it is absolutely essential that we should lose no time in raising our general standard of education. Although the proposals of the National Service Leagne, as regards service, are very moderate, still they present an opportunity for creating an incentive to all the youth of this country, rich and poor alike, to acquire a higher standard of educational efficiency, thus providing us in the future with captains of our industries—who are so badly needed—backed up by a more capable and intelligent rank and file. _ _

One point which, as manufacturers, we should like specially to emphasize is the necessity for almost uni- versal improvement in discipline on the part of em- ployees. The annual training of the youth of Great Britain which would follow the adoption of the pro- posals of the National Service League would largely meet this want. _ _ - -

We welcome then most cordially the advent of the National Service League as giving an opportunity for improvement in the directions we have indicated above and which may be briefly summarised under the three following headings : (1) Discipline; 2) Physical development ; (3), Mental development; and unless we progress in these three respects, no Fiscal policy yet invented, whether Free Trade or Protectiop, will save the country from being left behind in the race for commercial supremacy. —Yours faithfully, (Signed) J. J. CROSFIELD, K. E. MARKEL, H. ROBERTS, F. R. JONES, • C. F. HLTFFAM. Directors of Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited, Warrington. 25th January, 1904.

===== 0002601_19040130_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AND THE PRIESTLEY

CENTENARY.

A Commemorative Tablet to be Erected.

It is but fitting that Warrington, which was the home of Dr. Priestley for many years and the scene of some of his most important scientific discoveries, should worthily commemorate the centenary of his death. Steps are being taken locally to commemorate this unique event, which takes place on the 6th of February. As a preliminary, the members of the Warrington Society observed last evening as "a Priestly night, 'and short papers were read as follows :—"Priestley at War- rington" by Mr. H. Stuart Page ; "Priestley as a chemist "by Mr. F. G. Ruddock ; "Priestley and the Riots "by Mr. W. H. Sherburn. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. Rymer Young, in the unavoidable absence of Dr. Markel. The proceedings were rendered additionally interesting by 'the exhibition of some inter- esting Priestley books and relics. Ma. Madeley brought from the Museum some autograph letters of Dr.fPriestly., cousisting chiefly of letters from him to his brother-in-law, Mr. Wilkinson, from the year 1790 to 1802. The interesting antique chair occupied by the Chairman was the one used by Dr. Priestley whilst a tutor at the Academy ..and had been lent cy the Cairo-street congregation. The books exhibited included "A history of Electricity" by Dr. Priestley, written at Warrington and printed in this town by William Eyres. Another book illustrated Dr. Priestley's experiments on the different kinds of air' with pictures of the apparatus used. Rev. C. Harvey Cooke brought a book of photographs shewing Mill Hill Chapel in which Dr. Priestley ministered after leavingWarrington, and other interesting views at Leeds connected with Priestley and his work. Another inter- esting exhibit was a Wedgewood medallion of Priestley. Next Friday, upon the eve of the Centenary, the Rev. F. E. Freeston. of London. is to lecture at the Academy on "Priestley and his friends," illustrated by lantern slicks. On the day of the centenary, a commemorative tablet is to be unveiled by Dr. T. E. Thorpe, C. 8., F.R.S., head of the Government laboratories in London, and author of a new life of Priestley, on Dr. Priestley's former residence in Academy-street.
