NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football. By " Ovalist."

It was quite like Warrington to go and lose against the bottom club in the League when nicely in the running for the championship. Somehow or other most of us had a foreboding of what might happen on the peculiar enclosure of the:Keighley Club. They were known to be formidable opponents on their own patch, and the fact that Salford had only succeeded in drawing there did not bode well for Warrington's success. Fish's absence was another disturbing element, though much was hoped from Kenyon, Warrington tried hard enough anyhow, and were much the better team, but the home side's ability to tackle and kick goals won them the match. '

Warrington's defeat does not put them entirely out of the running for final honours, but it has had such an effect on their position that one cannot expect with any reason that the medals will eventually come War- rington way. Six of the clubs above Warrington in the table have an infinitely better chance, and the competition is so keen that it is impossible to make more than a hazardous guess. at the ultimate champions. It was particularly unfortunate that War- rington should have lost on Saturday, for the defeat of Oldham and Salford improved what chance there was for the " Wirepullers." Still, as I have already said, it was just like WarrinF.Tton. They were ever a team to make a slip betwixt the cup and the lip.

The match at Keighley was remarkable for the number of times that Warrington just failed to score. They went off with a rare burst at the commencement and fairly peppered the home line. Kenyon twice, and Hockenhall once ran over, only to be recalled for haying stepped into touch. On a ground of avec-aril) width they must have scored easily and won the match for Warrington, but there was so little room to oper- ate on the narrow field that the backs were badly hampered. In fact I should think the peculiarities of the Keighley ground save them 5 points per match at the very least. Any reference to the table of results will prove at a glance how different the Keighley men are at home and away. There has been little scoring on the Keighley ground, and there is likely to be as long as the playinglportion is only 61 yards wide. The Northern Union ought to fix upon a minimum measure- ment for first-class grounds, and see that the standard is maintained.

The superiority of the visitors was most marked at three quarters, where Kenyon, Isherwood, Dickenson and Hockenhall combined well. The new recrait Kenyon, filled Fish's place with distinct credit and it was not his fault that he failed to score. He showed any amount of dash,and in the opinion of a competent judgelike Mr. J. H. Smith, he is a most piomising wineplayer. Hallam put up a grand exhibition of full back play and earned high praise from the York- shiremen. He was streets ahead of Walker, the home custodian. In the front rank and at half back, each side got as much as they gave. Warrington's great efforts in the early stages of the game told on them and they tired before the close,but the forwards could not be blamed for the loss of the match. They did their duty.

There should be a fine crowd at Wilderspool to-dag to welcome Broughton Rangers. The fact that War- rington won at Wheater's Field early in • the season does not make their success to-day any more certain. Last season both matches resulted in draws, the game at Broughton ending without a score being registered, whilst atoWilderspool each team managed to score a try. It is as follows—Poole, back ; Widdeson, Harry Wilson (captain), and Hogg, three-quarter backs; S. James and W. James, half backs • Trotter, Scott, Oram, Beetham, Whitehead, Winskilf,Thompson,and Ruddick forwards. The famous right' wing was responsible for no less than six tries against Swinton. Will Fish bottle Wilson and Hogg up to-day as he did at Broughton?'

The Warrington team will be practically at full i strength, though Harris is not yet n his place. The selected 15 is ; Hallam, full-back ; Fish, Isherwood Dickenson, and Hockenhall, threequarters; Davies and Bate, half backs ; Fielding, Boardman, Morrison, Edmondson, Swift, Thomas, Jolley and Naylor, for- wards. For his own club, Ponty Newydd he was a prolific try getter, but up here the scrummagers, do not .often distinguish themselves in this line. T shall be very pleased if Fielding proves anything like so capable a man as his club mate Thomas.

Warrington were also sending a strong team to Broughton to tackle the undefeated second string of the Rangers, but the match has been postponed owing to the Northern Union fixing a Lancashire Junior Cup Final at Wheaters' Field.

A. benefit match in aid of Sam Houghton will most likely be decided at Wheater's Field on a date not yet fixed—probably a Wednesday in the near future. It has been suggested that the sides should be selected, one from the West Lancashire League Clubs and the other from Swinton, Salford, and Broughton-Rangers.

Runcorn and Widnes fought their annual battle on Widnes soil on Saturday, and as usual the home team won. With a view of assisting St. Helens to regain a Position in the First Division of the Northern Union League, the Warrington Committee have decided to transfer two of their capable reserve players to that club. Ernest Radcliffe, three-quarter, has several times assisted the senior team, but an injury last season kept him out of active service. He is, how- ever, now perfectly sound, and should strengthen the Saints three-quarter line. James Allen,half-back, has on many occasions assisted the first team. TO ESCAPE THE BERTILLON SYSTEM. Two smart young up-to-date shopbreakers are Harold Fitzgerald and Fred Reynolds. When arrested for an offence in Stockport-road, Manchester, they were wear- ing gloves, and explained to the detective that they "didn't want to leave their finger impressions on the window sill." On Wednesday, at Manchester Sessions, they were committed for six montlis apiece.

GRAMMAR SCHOOL OLD BOYS.

ANNUAL DINNER.

The second annual dinner of the Old Boys' Union in connection with the Boteler Grammar School was held at the Lion Hotel, on Thursday evening, under the pre- sidency of Mr. Edward R. Pickmere, M.A. (Town Clerk of Liverpool). There were upwards of sixty Governors and Old Boys present, the Company including the Rev. Canon Willis, Rev. E. C. E. C trleton, Messrs. F. Monks, William Long, H. Roberts (Governors), and the head- master (Rev. E. J. Willcocks). The catering was in the ablehands of Mr. Lee, and it was in every respect satis- factory.

===== 0002601_19040130_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TOASTS.

The PRESIDENT proposed the toast of the King. in a few appropriate words, and it was honoured m the usual loyal manner. "THE PIOUS IVIENIORY OF SIR THONIAS BOTELER."

Mr. C. S. BURGESS (the Chairman of the Committee) in proposing the above toast, at the outset said he wished all Old Boys who had not already signed the re- cord book would do so. He thought the success of the gathering that evening would lead to the settlement of the Union on a firmer basis. He felt quite con- vinced that they were acting upon the right lines, and that the good feeling which they were endeavouring to foster among Old Boys must be for the good of the school. He was very proud to welcome that night his old schoolfellow, Mr. E. R. Pickmere, and thanked him for accepting the invitation of the committee to preside at their second annual dinner. (Applause). He asked them to drink in solemn silence to the pions memory of Sir Thomas Boteler. ABSENTEES.

The SECRETARY (Mr. J. H. Smthurst), referring to the absence of several Governors and Old Boys, said that Mr. Robert Pierpoipt, M.P., Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., Mr. John White, the Rev. Richard Greenall, Colonel Ireland Blackbarne, Mr. J. C. Parr, Mr. T. H. Sutton, Mr. flertzog, Mr. Alyward, Mr. Young, the Rev. George Feather, and the Rev. 0. H. Cary—(applattse)— had all written regretting their inability to attend.

===== 0002601_19040130_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
"PROSPERTITY TO THE SCHOOL."

This toast was proposed by the PRESIDENT. who re- marked how honoured he felt by being elected to that office and to preside at the dinner. Mr. Burgess had kindly sent him a list of the Old Boys (a list which in- terested him exceedingly) and he was horrified to find that he (iVlr.Pickmere) was one of the oldest of the Old Boys, having been at the Grammar School in 1865, leaving in 1874. He thought the idea of an Old Boys' Union an excellent one, and suggested that if possible, for the sake of tradition, a history of the old school should be compiled, and a prize awarded to the scholar whowrote the best essay on the 13)teler Grammar School. With regard to education generally, he was pleased at the progress Warrington was making, and that the town was making quite a reputation in that respect. Education was quite as big a question as what theycalled the fiscal policy or,as he had heard it referred to by a certain after dinner speaker,the "poscal filacy." (Laughter.) Whatever may be said against it •he thought it was the duty off every thinking man to make the Education Act workable if possibte. Referring to the study of the classics, he hoped that that was a subject which would never be excluded from the school's curriculum—in fact he hoped it would be insisted upon. He, personally, had a great love for the classics, and he thought the study of them meant great mental improvement to any man. Every subject studied nowadays, it seemed to him, was regarded from a money making standpoint. Could they earn money by it? was asked. He urged them to see that the minds of the boys were elevated by their studies. and that they should not study a particular subject with the idea of seeing how much they could make out of it. (Hear, hear.) However, he felt such matters were safe in the able hands of the present headmaster. (Applause.) He trusted that while this education question was being so much debated, they would back up Mr. Willcocks in his endeavour to maintain the honoured name of the school. They as Old Boys should if possible strengthen the headmaster's hands, and take some interest in the important work he had before him. (Applause.) In proposing the toast of "Prosperity to the Boteler Grammar School," he coupled with it a wish for long life andsuccess to the excellent Headmaster. (Applause.) The company sang "For he's a jolley good fellow," after which three cheers were given for Mr. and Mrs. Willcocks.

The Rev. E. .T. W[LtcocKs, in acknowledging the toast, said they knew how very pleased he was to meet 1311 the Old Boys, especially the President, who, as they all knew, had had such a distinguished career, and by his ability and energy had forced himself into an honourable position in a neighbouting city. Before coming to the dinner he had looked over the list of sixth form boys, when the President stood second in the school, and he could well remember Pickmere going to Oxford in 1874. Of course there were many other successful names, the bearers of whom had led success- ful lives. Referring to the work of the school, which was a very interesting subject, especially to masters,he could say that the school had kept up its reputation for efficiency during the past year. In fact, every candi- date sent up to the Oxford local examination last summer had passed. (Heir. hear.) In conclusion he thanked them very much for the way in which theyhad drunk the health of the school and that of Mrs. Wi II cocks. (Applause).

"PROSPERITY TO THE COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.

The toast of "Prosperity to the County Borough of Warrington," was submitted by Mr. W. E. Atkin, who said that under this title he recognised the good old toast of "The Town and Trade of Warrington." In commenting at some length on the trade of the town he compared Warrington's commercial growth with that of several other places, remarking that Warrington was not a town of mushroom growth, but a town whose in- dustries had steadily increased. Trade in the town was undoubtedly sick, as it was in other parts of the United. Kingdom, but he hoped before long matters would im- proY-e, and he asked them to drink therefore• to the increased trade of the town and prosperity to the ancient borough of Warrington. (Applause). Mr. HENRY ROBERTS, who was cordially received, in acknowledging the toast said it afforded him very great pleasure to be with them onlthat night for two or three personal reasons; he thought that he couldclaim to be the first Old Boy who had reached the hon3urable position.of Governor of the Old School: secondly, he was the head of two generations of Old Boys who were represented there that night. Thirdly, he was glad to be present because he had the honour of being mayor of Warrington during the year in which it became a county borough. (Applause). Referring to the state of the trade of the town mentioned by the proposer of the toast, Mr. Roberts said that together with the increase of population, there had been a remarkable increase in the growth of the industries and various arts. He most cordially re-echoed Mr. Atkin's wish that the prosperity which had attended the town in the past might be greatly increased in the future. In regatd to the administration of affairs in the town, even now we might claim that Warrington is a fairly well adminis- tered Borough, and he hoped there would always be a sufficiently strong feeling of patriotism to induce the best men we had amongst 'is to take their fair share of municipal work. (Applause) To his mind the most crying need which our industries to-day were suffering from was the want of a better general education amongst employees 'of every class, and he thought to the attainment of this end, the attention of all thinking men should be directed. Suggestions had been made that night as to how the old school.of which they wereall very proud, should retain its propsr place in the educational structure of the Borough. He did not think it would pass the wit of man to devise a method by which the Grammar School could remain the crowning point of the whole edifice. (Hear, hear), Mr. Roberts referred to the pleasure it gave him to meet several of his old schoolfellows, whom he had not seen for many years, one, in fact, whom he had not seen for twenty years. He thanked them very much for the corgial manner in which they had drunk the toast and the Way in which they had received him on rising to respond. He knew they all wished every prosperity to the town they loved and the old school which had done so much for them years ago. (Applause).

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL.

to be given to the Ship Canal, aud the Canal COmpany would thereupon be required to do the necessary work of dredging in order to comply with the terms of the agreement. In this case there was no complaint of their non-fulfilment of that obligation. The Judge had found that as regarded varying datum the Ship Canal Company had toperform their original obligation. Mr. Justice Vaughan Williams said that their original obligation was to perform a thing, certain but in the event of a contingency arising which rendered necessary further steps being taken that did not seem to him to be a question for an engineer or an arbitrator to deter- mine. The learned Judge held that they viere bound to maintain the dredging according to the arrangement which had been entered into, and this he submitted the Corporation had done.

Cripps said that the Corporation of Warrington, in his opinion, had sought an altogether wrong remedy. He contended that the Manchester Ship Canal Company had in no sense exceeded its Parliamentary powers. Counsel then proceeded to argue that the Court had no jurisdiction to deal with a matter crf this kind, and that any disputes between the parties ought to be referred to arbitration, and he asked their Lordships to decide this point before proceeding further with his argument. Lord Justine Vaughan Williams: Why not, under those circumstances, go to arbitration? Mr. Bousfield said that never occurred to the parties. Having referred to numerous authorities bearing upon cases of this description, counsel confidently submitted that the Court had ample jurisdiction to deal with the matter.

Lord Justice Vaughan Williams: Nobody would seriously argue against you that where the Court has power to stay there is jurisdiction. Mr. Bousfield submitted that the authorities carried him further, and showed that the Court had jurisdiction in a case similar to the one before the Court. The further hearing was adjourned. _

On Thursday Mr. liousfield-, continuing his argument again controverted the proposition sought to be estab- lished by Mr. Grippe, that under the agreement which had been entered into between the parties the j urisdiction of the Court had bean ousted, and that all questions in dispute were to ba 'determined by arbitration. The learned counsel submitted that not only had the Court a discretion but that it had ample jurisdiction to try and determine the issues involved in actions of this kind, and he cited authorities in favour of his conten- tion. What was intended by the Legislature was to give the pirties a fitting tribunal for the purpose of referring to it matters of importance, In order for his learned friend Mr. Grippe, to succeed he must show that, the effect of the language used was to prevent the Court having jurisdiction in those matters. If he failed to do that then he (Mr. Boast'sld) succeeded. He was going to point out that the language of the section gave a right to the parties to go to arbitration on cer- tain things, but he said that power would be dead when the works which the section contemplated were done. Tna language was intended to refer only to certain things, and not to a question of damages before the canal was opened, which might arise. This claose was not intended to deprive the parties of the right to go to the Court in a case where they thought it would be for their benefit to do. so.

Mr. Hughes followed on the same side, and dealt at great length with tha rights of the traders and manu- facturers of Warrington under the agreement, and after- wards dealt with the interests of the Corporation of Warrington, ;Vho, he said, were profoundly interested in this question. The hearing was adjourned. Yesterday's Proceed' ills. [By TELEGRAPH.] The hearing of the anneal was continued yesterday. Mr. Hughes continued his argument in support of the contention that the Jurisdiction of the Court was not ousted under the agreement into which the parties had entered. He then submitted that the traders them- selves had a right to enforce the clause which was passed for their protection without the intervention of the Corporation of Warrington if they thought neces- sary. The traders of Warrington had independent rights and the power of the Corporation to vary was only as regarded the future,and not as to the past, and they could not take away the rights which had accrued by reason of past breaches. In this case the contract was in the nature of a contract between the Canal Com- pany and the traders, and', if that was so he thought that by virtue of the contract there was a right to enforce the provisions of this Act. The alternative proposition was this—Suppose it was an agreement entered into between the Company and the Corporation and incorporated in this Act, then anyone, even a member of the public, was entitled to sue for damage caused by breach of the obligation. There had been breaches of the statutory obligations to maintain this channel and traders had suffered damage owing to that. The damage, it was conceded, was very substantial. Messrs. Crosffeld and Messrs. Fairclough and Sons did an immense trade on the river Mersey, and if their boats were forced to navigate a five feet instead of an eight feet channel the damage would be very serious. The only effect of the trans- actions of 1898, he submitted, was to modify the agree- ment so far as the traders were concerned and they would be quite content to go upon section 88 without the agreement.

THE DREDGING OF THE MERSEY. Appeal Against Mr. Justice Byrne's Judgment.

An aetion of great importance to Warrington, as far as the navigation of the river is concerned, was com- menced in the Court of Appeal on Tuesday before Lords Justices Vaughan Williams,Sterling, and Cozens- Hardy. The IkTlnoh9st3r Ship Canal Company appealed from a judgment of Mr. Justice Byrne's in the recent action of Crosfield and Sons, Limited and others v. the Manchester Ship Canal Company. The object of the action was to enforce the obligations undertaken by the Manchester Ship Canal Company with reference to the dredging of the river Mersey where it flows through the limits of the borough of Warrington. The plaintiffs were Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited, James Fairclough and Sons, Limited, and. Monks, Hall, and Co.. Limited, traders and manufac- turers, carrying on business at or near Warrington and on the banks of the river Mersey, and the Mayor, Alderman and Burgesses of the Borough themselves. The first three plaintiffs sued on behalf of the msedves and all others, the traders, manufacturers and others carrying on business at or near %Varrington, and by aipenduent, made after the opening of the action, in their individual capacity. By the Act of 1885 the Canal Company undertook to dredge ant maintain dredged ce:tain portions of the bad and banks of tha Mersey before the canal was opened. As the work wis not completed in time, by another agreement the Company undertook to eomplete the dredging by July 1, 1394, it being provided that the Graders and manufacturers of Warrington should carry goods free of rates along certain portions of the canal until the cempletion of the dredging. It was alleged that the dredgine was not completed, and until 18)5 the treders of Werrington used certain portions of the canal free of tolls. Another agreement was made in April 1893, it being provided that the Canal Company should effectually execute Bad complete the dredging operations, and that the privileges given to the traders of Warrington should cease. The latter, hpwever, alleged that the Company had not carried out the agreement in respect of the dredging. In the early part of 1897 the Company claimed payment of dues from the traders of Warrington, and payment had been made under protest. It was alleged that notices had been given to the Canal Company from time to time, pointing out that they had not fulfilled their obliga- tions, and that nevertheless they had insisted upon pay- ment of dues, and had not remedied the things com- plained of. The Canal Company maintained that they had asea fact executed their obligations, and in the alternative that the dredging of the Canal had been made much more difficult by the discharge of lime and other refuse into the river by the traders of Warrington or soils of them, and also that they had duly complied with all notices which the plaintiffs were entitled to give. Mr. Justice Byrne gave judgment for the plaintiffs, holdingithat there was no impossibility in the defen- dants fulfilling their statutory obligations to give the statutory width of channel and depth of water. It was from this judgment that the defendants now appealed. Tbere was also a cross notic of appeal by the plaintiffs. Ar. Fletcher %loulton, [C.C., M.P., Mr. Cripps, K.C., M.P., Mr. Pickford, K.C.. and Mr. E. 0. Leigh Clare, M.P., appeared for the appellant, who were the defen- dants in the Court below ; and Mr. Bonsfield, K.C., M.P., Mr. Hughes, K.C., and Mr. F. Thompson were counsel for the respondents, who were the plaintiffs in the Court below. _

Mr. Cripps, in opening the appellants' case set out the facts as given above, and said that as regarded the channel not having been dredged, the learned Judge found in favour of the appellants, but he found against them that they had not maintaind the dredging according to their obligations. That depended upon three points. In the first place, the defendants said this was a matter which the Legislature had provided to be determined by arbitration, and, therefore, was a matter outside the jurisdiction of the court. In the trial of the action fifteen or sixteen days had been occupied with hewing evidence the whole or which was irrelevant, if ha was right on this preliminary Point. The second paint between the parties was this: In dredr_zing the .canal their obligation was to be determined in reference to a particular datum line. The datum line was differently constructed by the defendants and the plaintiffs. The formersaid it was a fixed datum line, well known in reference to the conditions of the Mersey. It was used as a term of art because the conditions of the Mersey, which were rather peculiar, had caused a particular datum line to be used in all matters of this kind ever since 1811, and in all the negotiations and in the agree- ment between the parties the obligation hid reference to that fixed datum line, and it was only in reference to it that they could fulfil their obligations at all. The judge below had held that the defendants were wrong as regarded the fixed datum line, but if they were right they would have fulfilled their obligation, not only as regarded making, but also main- taining the channel dredged. There was a third point. They had to fulfil their obligation as regarded dredging to the reasonable satisfaction of the surveyor of Warrington, and they said that on the form of the agreement and the clause, the question whether the surveyor was reasonably satisfied was a matter which the Legislature had said should be determined from time to time by an engineer. Until the engineer had foiled that what they were doing was not reason- able, there was, he contended, no right of action on the part of the Corporation or the traders. The obligation on them was to dredge the bed of the river to a certain depth, and for ever after maintain the same dredging. The defendants contended that a channel could not be dredged in the first instance without having a fixed datum and a fixed obligation as regarded depth of the channel. The depth of the channel depended chiefly on the fresh water. He knew it very well. Most of it was in his constituency, and he had the highest respect for it. (Laughter.) It was possible, owing to a dry season, whick was really one of their difficulties, the fresh water level was lowered to the extent of a foot. If they were not to have a fixed datum, they would never know when they had fulfilled their original obligation. He submitted that they were bound merely to make the channel at a fixed depth in the first instance, and having made it, to maintain it at that depth. Low water at spring tides at Warrington was known to everyone in the Mersey estuary as a mark which would be ten feet above the Ordnance datum at Warrington Bridge. To insist on eight feet of water at all times and under all conditions was an impossible construction of the Act because it imposed an obligation which nobody could undertake to fulfil under the conditions of the Mersey. A peculiarity of the Mersey was that when there were spring tides thEtwater was more banked up at Warrington than at neap tides. Lowest spring water at Warrington did not denote the worst conditions of the river at low water, but the best, and the words had been adopted purposely. The hearing of the case was adjourned. The hearing was resumed on Wednesday. Mr. Cripps continuing his argument in support of the appellants' case, repeated his submission that it was a fixed datum line under which the Canal Company incurred respon- sibility. Their lordships would see from the plan which WARRINGTON AND ITS PLAYGROUNDS. had been handed up to them that the low water at ordinary'spring tide was put at 10ft above the datum Important Recommendations by the Open line. What the Canal Company said was that they had Spaces Committee. a fixed datum line giving at low water at spring tide a ' m deputation from the Open Spaces Committee depth of 10ft. above the ordnance datum which come visited Wallasey on July 10th, and as a result the Corn- from the Corporation of Warrington. 'lt was only the Company who brought in the agree- ment and who said that it modified the rights of the Traders. He agreed that the rights of the traders were modified, but with respeat to the question of tolls the matter stood this way—Notice jiad been given to the Company under the clause that the dredging of the channel was not being maintained and those notices not having been complied with, the Traders said" Now we must have the passage of the Canal free of tolls," but the Canal Company said "No, you shall not; you must pay the tolls." one of the purposes of this action was to recover those tolls as oi id under duress. Lord Justice Vaughan Williams asked if the Corpora- tion gave 'notice in writing. Cinder section three Mr. Hughes said he should not like to say that. His point was that the traders had suffered damage and were entitled to iecover. He wished to point out that the agreement of 1896 did not affect Section 202 for any purpose of arbitration between the traders and the company. Lord Justice Vaughan Williams: Do you doubt as between the Corporation and the Company under the the clause you might have raised a question affecting the rights of the traders? I think you could. Mr. Hughes said he had no doubt they could refer to arbitration questions which would effect traders in the future, but not questions referring to the past. 'qLord Justice Vaughan Williams: They could ask the arbitrators to say whether they were entitled to have something. Mr. Hughes said that was so with regard to the fu- ture, but not with regard to any accrued rights. To sum up he said the traders' rights alternatively were the nature of contractual rights under'section 88, or if not they were rights of persons who had been injured by reason of a breach of statutory obligations. Those rights had accrued many years ago, as the Learned Judge in the Court below had found. Numberless com- plaints had been made that the statutory channel had not been maintained, and there had never been a sug- gestion of arbitration before.

- Lord Justice Vaughan Williams: It seems a terribly hard case. _ _ _

Mr.lnighes warmly protested against his Lordship treating this as a jesting matter. It was in fact a most important matter to the people of Warrington. Lord Justice Vaughan Wiliiams : Not only is it important, but I say it is a terribly hard case. Mr. Hughes said this was a matter of vital import- ance and if the contention of the other side was right the whole of the expenses connected with the trial would be thrown away, because they would have to go back to perhaps two, three or four tribunals and then probably have to come to the court on a special case. Mr. Cripps, in reply, repeated his proposition that the jurisdiction of the Court was °listed. JUDGMENT RESERVED.

At the conclusion of the arguments, their Lordships said they would take time to consider their judgment on the point which had hem raked.

LIBERAL MEETINGS LAST NIGHT. ; ADDRESSES ON THE FISCAL QUESTION.

The fiscal question was the subject under discu,-,ion at two Liberal meetings held in the district last night —one at Penketh, which forms part of the Newton division and the other at G-rappenhall, which is included in the Knutsfard division. , The meeting at Penketh was held under the , auspices of the Liberal Club. A large company. ' assembled in the Wesleyan schoolroom and the pro- ceedings were presided over by Mr. J. G. Withinshaw, I a thoughtful politician holding Liberal views and. a convinced Free Trader. He was accompanied on the platform by Mr. J. W. S. Callie (secretary of the ; Financial Reform Association) and amongst those present were Mr. C. E. Parker, Mr. J. B. Aitken, Mr. F. Wilkinson (hon. secretary of the Liberal Club) and Mr. B. Tilston (hon. treasurer.) The Chairman observed that they were met together to discuss one of the most important questions that ; could concern them. It was what they might call a I bread and butter question—a question of the greatest magnitude,and one that had been rushed upon them at a moment's notice. When they got a new fiscal policy , put before them without any adequate cause they must look for some reason. He believed it had been put 1 forth to cover up mistakes of the past. (Hear, hear.) Not long ago they were told that they had no reason to complain of the state of trade • now they were told that unless some sudden alteration were made ' they would be commercially dead. As a rule changes in commerce did not come so quickly as that. ! (Hear, hear.) Even the last returns of the Board of I Trade showed an increase in the volume of our trade. The members of the Government could not agree that our trade was as bad as some were trying ;to make out, and there was plenty of time to have an impartial commission to inquire into the matter instead of taking people's word for it. (Hear,hear.)lf they looked at the platforms of the tariff reformers they gen3rally found them imposed of one of 2 sorts—either big landlords]or wealthylmanufacturers. (Hear, hear.) They did not find many working men or trades union leaders supporting the fiscal proposals of Mr. Chamberlain. An attempt was made to hold out old age pensions as an inducement to workingmen to accept protection, but it was found that that would not work in, and the cry now was more wages for the working- man. The Chairman proceeded to state he had yet to learn that workingmen in this country got less wages than those received by workingmen in other countries. If they compared the wages of British workers with those of any on the Continent they:would find that the British workman had the advantage. (Hear,hear) Proceeding to discuss the state of trade in America, the chairman said as most of them were interested in the leather trade he would give an instance showing the condition of the trade in America. The American leather trust—one of the largest in America—was 24 million dollars behindhand in their preference dividend which did not show a very satisfactory state of affairs. As a matter of fact American Tanners were going over to Canada, because there they could get a larger and cheaper supply of hemlock bark. If this transference of the tanning business to Canada continued, Canada would soon be the competitor of England in the export of leather, and under lie. Cham- berlain's scheme,English mannfacturers would be placed at a great disadvankage owing to the increased cost of production. (Hear, Wear.) Mr. Callie gave an able review of the whole fiscal problem. By bringing forward his scheme of fiscal federation, Mr. Chamberlain had prevented them from discussing such important matters as the land question and sent them back to the a.b.c. of the question, which their forefathers settled long ago. A discussion ensued at the close. THE MEETING AT GRAPPENHALL. Mr. W. Longs Views. •

Mr. William Long, J.P., presided at the meeting held in the Large Room, Grappenhall, and said the fiscal question was of very vital importance in the interests of the country to all classes, except perhaps the richer classes and some manufacturers. Protection would tend to make rich men richer, but on the other hand it was his firm conviction that it would make poor men poorer. Dealing with Mr. Chamberlain's argument that any increase in the price of food would be counter- balanced by a reduction in the price of tobacco and other articles, the chairman said it worked out that the workingman was about a farthing per week better off. (Laughter). How would such a reduction on tobacco help a man with a large family? What workingmen wanted was to get the staff of life "as cheaply as possible. (BFear, hear.) Mr. Long ex- pressed the hope that the question would be discussed in a non-party spirit. He proceeded to contrast the life of the British and German workingmen, and pointed out that Lord Goschen had said that German people ate horse-meat. The hours of employment were longer and the wages smaller than in England. Speaking of the member for the division, Mr. Long said they did not want to be represented by one who had expressed his determination to adhere to a policy which they be- lieved was against the best interests of the country. (Hear, hear.) An interesting address was delivered by Mr.'.T+ahn Taylor, of the National Reform Union._ - -

A vote of thanks was accorded to the chairmag and speaker and to the Rector for the use of the rod*, on the proposition of Mr. C. Jackson Holmes.

TOWN COUNCIL BUSINESS. The Question Box.—Soma Awkward

Queries.

A meeting of the Town Council will be held on Tues- day evening next, when the following qnestions will be asked, and replied to by the Chairman of the different Committees concerned. ABOUT STREET PAVING.

By Mr. Councillor GOUGH— To ask the Chairman of the Paving and Sewerage Committee.- 1. Why is Jockey-street, which is a front street in the borough, being paved with macadam instead of beingpaved with setts or cubes? 2. Does not Alderman Heaton (the Chairman of the Paving and Sevverage Committee) own all the property on one side of this street?" Are Aldermen and Councillors exempt from the provisions of the law as to the street- making, or is there one law for Aldermen and another for the property owners who are not members of the Council ? 3. Are Councillors Arthur Peacock and John Wilkinson paving part of Dudley-street, and is the work under the supervision of the Borough Surveyor? Does he see that quantities and qualities of materials are being used in accordance with the required specifica- tions of tho Corporation? How it it that property owners who are not members of the Town Council are forced to have their streets made through or by the Corporation? 4. Is the contractor who is making Algernon-street and other streets near to it, using Portland cement in making the concrete? If not, what test will the cement bear that is being used, and after the work has stood. seven days ? Will this work bear the test of 4501b5. to the square inch? And can the Borough Surveyor take up a square yard to test in the presence of ,7-embers of the Council on a day to be named? 5. When are the Paving and Sewerage Committee going to provide a weighing machine at Howley Storage yard, so that members of the Council may get a correct account or knowledge of what material the Corporation use during a year, what materials there is in stock, and whether the Corporation gets weight for the money it pays !

CHEAPER BATHING FOR WOMEN AND CHILDREN. By Mr. Councillor Witsos— To ask the Chairman of the Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee :—Whether the committee will con- sider the desirability of encouraging the art of swim- ming among women and children by granting half- penny bathing tickets.

HALF-PENNY TRAM FARES FOR SCHOOL CHILDREN.

By Mr. Councillor WILSON— To ask the Chairman of the Electricity and Tramways Committee—Whether the time has not arrived for con- sidering the question of allowing school children to travel by the cars at halfpenny fares?

ODDFELLOWS AT DIN:ER. Presentation to the Past Grand of the " Prince of Wales " Lodge.

Managed by capable officers, who have its interests thoroughly at heart, the `.` Prince of Vales" Lodge of the Independent Order of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity) has made rapid progressive strides, and a con- gratulatory tone pervaded the proceedings at the third annual dinner held in the well-appointed lodge room at the ," Pelican " Hotel on Thursday evening. The function was a complete success, a large company par- taking of an excellent repast prepared by Bro. W. Chalmers. Afterwards a capital musical pro- gramme was submitted, and various toasts were heartily honoured. The chair was occupied by Bro. T. P. Bradshaw, P.G.' and he was supported at the cross table by Brothers R. H. Halford, G.M., Prink Webster, Prov. C.S., G. F. Freeman, P.G., Joseph Taylor, D.G.M., T. Wadsworth, P.G., (lodge secretary), J. Massey, P.P.G.M., and others. At the outset the chairman remarked that the year 1903 had been a successful one. Although they had paid out the large amount, of £257 18s 10d in sick paid, they had been able to save £lB7 13s id which had been added to the capital and that he thought reflected very great credit upon those responsihle for the management of the lodge. (Hear, hear). The toast of The King" was honoured on the pro-- position of the CHAIRMAN. MAssEv, P.P.G.lVl.lproposed th e health of the "Manchester Unity Board of Directors." He referred to the magnitude of the work which devolved upon the- Board of Management and said he was sure they gave just decisions in all applications made to them. (Applause.) In responding -Bro. R. H. HALFORD, Prov. G.M., said they had every reason to be proud of the Manchester Unity, particularly as it owed its origin to the efforts of workingmen as to its strength and resources, the Manchester Unity now consisted of 458 districts, with-1397 lodges, and 862, 673, financial members. There was, however, still room for an increased membership, and the enlarge- ment of the Order depended chiefly upon the active workers in the -lodges and districts. The Order was worth over £11,000,000. During the year 1902 the total income from contributions &c. was £1,327,000. The amount paid out in sick and funeral pay, management expenses &c. was £945,396, leaving a clear gain for the year of £381,754.

A pleasing incident now took place, Brother T. P. Bradshaw, P. 0., being presented with a Past Grand's gold jewel in recognition of his valuable services to the lodge for several years, including the Coronation year. Bro. R. H. HALFORD made the presentation in fitting terms and eulogised Bro. Bradshaw's faithful services to the lodge. Considering the comparatively short time he had been an active member of the Order, Bro. Bradshaw was remarkably well known throughout the district. Soon after hie initiation he gave evidence of his desire to become an active worker. He made rapid strides in the lodge until he attained the premier office of N.G., and now he had very successfully passed through his period of office. Although he was now, he supposed, going to live in retirement as far as the lodge work was concerned, they hoped to retain Bro. Bradshaw' services as an active worker for Oddfellowship. (Ap- plause.) In thanking the members Bro. BRADSHAW said he joir ed the lodge in December, 1900, and delivered his maiden speech the same night. (Laughter.) In June 1901 he was.appointed inside tiler and had remained in harness ever since. He proceeded to allude to the new departures in the way of social functions, and interchange of visits with other lodges which had been made during his terra of office and paid a special tribute to the help he had received from Bro. G. F. Freeman. He asked the members to second the efforts of the officers by taking a keen interest in the work, and regularly attending the lodge meetings. They could not retain their position as the premier lodge in Warrington unless each member recognized the responsibility which rested upon his shoulders. The membership of the lodge was 468, and he hoped the members would do their utmost during the coming year 'to raise the number to 500, and thus create a record in the district. In conclusion Bro. Bradshaw acknow- ledged the services of all the ofEcers of the lodge. Bro. J. F. NORBURY, P.P.G.M.,proposed " The War- rington District" and urged the necessity lot' more individual effort to extenrl the Order.

Bro. FRANK WEBSTER, Prov. C. S., responded. He pointed out that for the first time in its existence the funeral fund of the district stood at over £2,000 which was invested at 31- per cent. During the year the in- crease of membership had been 49 and he believed the secessions were fewer than usual. The number of mem- bers at present was 1953 and the amount paid in sick and funeral funds during the year was .£9OO. Only one lodge had lost any money and it had experienced a very heavy drain in connection with the old members. He hoped to see the district prosper until it was one of the finest in the Manchester Unity. .(Applause): The health of "The district officers" was honoured and Bro. R. H. Halford suitably responded. 8r0.,J. TAYLOR, Prov. D.G.M.,proposed "The Prince, of Wales' Lodge ' and alluded to the importance of en- couraging the younger members. So long as the lodge had the services of men like Bros. Bradshaw and. Freeman they need have no fear as to its progress. (Applause.) Bro. T. WADSWORTH. P.G. (secretary) responded and said they were now disbursing money at the rate of almost £3OO a year in sick pay alone, and most of that found its way through very Acceptable channels. The interest on the capital had paid off more than one third of their liabilities during the ;ear, which was very creditable. The energy infused into the lodge by the younger members was no doubt largely responsible for its prosperity. The officers all performed their duties: to the best of their ability and they heartily apprec- ated the manner in which the other members rallied round them in promoting entertainments and furthering the interests of the lodge. He was confident that in. the future the lodge would attain a position which at one bime they never even dreamt of. (Applause). The toast of "The host and hostess" was enthusiastic- ally drunk on the proposition of Bro. G. F. FREEMAN, 13-G., and a vote of thanks was accorded to the com- mittee and artistes. During 'he evening Mr. W. H. Ireland, who was in ISplendid voice sang "The star of Bethlehem" and "Mary" with taste and expression, and Bro. J. Willett creditably rendered "Mona." Bro. T. Reddish gave a spirited interpretation of "Boys of the Old Brigade" and later contributed the humorous item " Finnigan's wakes." Humorous songs by Bro. J. Atherton P. 0., were a popular feature. The piano- forte accompaniments were supplied by P.G.'s F. Webster and H. Farrar. The arrangements had been ably carried out by a committee with Bro. T. P. Brad- shaw as chairman and Bro. G. F. Freeman, as hon. secre- tary, and to these gentlemen a special word of praise is due for their indefatigable efforts to ensare the success of the function.
